A local sign decomposition for symplectic self-dual Galois representations of rank two
Ashay Burungale, Shinichi Kobayashi, Kentaro Nakamura, Kazuto Ota

Abstract
. We prove the existence of a new structure on the first Galois cohomology of generic families of symplectic self-dual p p -adic representations of G ℚ p G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}} of rank two (a local sign decomposition): a functorial decomposition into free rank one Lagrangian submodules which encodes the p p -adic variation of Bloch–Kato subgroups via completed epsilon constants, mirroring a symplectic structure. The local sign decomposition has diverse local as well as global arithmetic consequences. This includes compatibility of the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant and completed epsilon constants, answering a question of Mazur and Rubin. The compatibility leads to new cases of the p p -parity conjecture for Hilbert modular forms at supercuspidal primes p p . We also formulate and prove an analogue of Rubin’s conjecture over ramified quadratic extensions of ℚ p {\mathbb{Q}}_{p} . Using it, we construct an integral p p -adic L L -function for anticyclotomic deformation of a CM elliptic curve at primes p p ramified in the CM field.



1.
Introduction

This paper aims to study the arithmetic of symplectic self-dual
p
-adic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two.
Such representations arise naturally in arithmetic geometry and automorphic representation theory, and deform in
p
-adic families.
The Bloch–Kato conjecture suggests epsilon constants to be pivotal to their arithmetic.
Our main result reveals a new structure on the first Galois cohomology of such representations (a local sign decomposition): a functorial decomposition into free rank one Lagrangian submodules encoding Bloch–Kato subgroups via completed epsilon constants, mirroring a symplectic structure (see Theorem
1.3
). It has local as well as global arithmetic applications (see §
1.3
).

1.1.
Overview

A fundamental conjecture of Deligne, Beilinson, and Bloch–Kato
[
5
]
posits: special values of the complex
L
-function
L(M,s)
associated to
a motive
M
over (a finite extension of)
{\mathbb{Q}}
at integers
encode the arithmetic of
M
in the guise of
Bloch–Kato Selmer groups
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}},M_{p})
associated to
the
p
-adic realizations
M_{p}
. It is a far-reaching generalization of the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture for elliptic curves.

The Bloch–Kato Selmer group
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}},M_{p})
is a
p
-adic avatar of motivic cohomology.
A foundational insight of Bloch and Kato: to introduce the Bloch–Kato subgroup

H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)

for any
p
-adic de Rham representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}:={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

via
p
-adic Hodge theory, and define
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}},M_{p})
consisting of global
Galois
cohomology classes whose localization at
p
lives in
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},M_{p})
and which are unramified at other primes. This notion encompasses
the Mordell–Weil group of rational points on an elliptic curve
as well as
the Chow groups of cycles on an algebraic variety in terms of Galois representations.
For example, if
V=V_{p}E
is the
p
-adic Tate module of an elliptic curve
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
,
then

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=E({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\hat{\otimes}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and a discrete analogue of the Bloch–Kato Selmer group
for
E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}}
is the usual Selmer group
{\mathrm{Sel}}_{p^{\infty}}(E)
,
encoding
E({\mathbb{Q}})
and the Tate–Shafarevich group
\hbox{\cyr X}(E)[p^{\infty}]
.

An enriching landscape arises from self-dual motives
M
.
The associated Galois representations are also self-dual, arising naturally in arithmetic geometry and the realm of automorphic forms. For example, a Tate twist of the middle dimensional étale cohomology of a variety over
{\mathbb{Q}}
(such as the Tate module of an elliptic curve)
and Galois representations associated to a self-dual cuspidal automorphic representation are self-dual.
The corresponding Deligne–Langlands epsilon constant
\varepsilon(M)
(with a suitable normalization) equals
+1
or
-1
, conjecturally the sign of the functional equation of the
L
-function
L(M,s)
. It is a product of local epsilon constants
\varepsilon_{v}(M)\in\{\pm 1\}
.
The global
\varepsilon
-constant
\varepsilon(M)
encodes the arithmetic of
M
.
For example,
the
p
-parity conjecture posits
(the Bloch–Kato conjecture mod
2
):
\varepsilon(M)

determines the parity of the rank of the Bloch–Kato Selmer group
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}},M_{p})

for any prime
p
.
Among self-dual representations, an innate dichotomy presents itself: being symplectic or orthogonal. In the latter case

\varepsilon(M)
equals
1
1

1

More precisely, Saito
[
81
]
proved
\varepsilon(M_{p})=+1

for all but finitely many primes
p
.

+1
.
The symplectic case is
more of an enigma,
and akin to phenomena in symplectic geometry.

As for the automorphic realm,
self-dual representations also form a distinctive landscape. They are enriched by the Gan–Gross–Prasad (GGP) conjectures
[
GGP
]
, linking central
L
-values (resp. derivatives) of certain self-dual automorphic
L
-functions
L(\pi,s)
with automorphic periods (resp. arithmetic cycles) if the automorphic
\varepsilon
-constant

\varepsilon(\pi)=+1
(resp.
\varepsilon(\pi)=-1
).
The GGP conjectures have witnessed striking progress over the last decade (cf.
[
91
,
4
,
90
]
). Their
p
-adic analogues are also emerging, with applications to the Bloch–Kato conjecture.
For example, the
p
-adic Waldspurger formula
[
1
,
56
]
is instrumental in the recent progress
[
83
,
19
,
20
]
towards the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture.

A salient feature of
p
-adic Galois representations is that they deform in
p
-adic families: global and local deformation rings parametrize self-dual deformations of such a fixed mod
p
representation. In the automorphic habitat eigenvarieties and completed cohomology parametrize
p
-adic families of automorphic representations.
For a
p
-adic family
{\mathcal{M}}
of symplectic self-dual Galois representations,
the variation of
\varepsilon
-constants
\varepsilon(M)
at geometric
specializations
M
of
{\mathcal{M}}
is elemental to its arithmetic. For example, if
\varepsilon(M)=-1
systematically over
{\mathcal{M}}
, then the
p
-parity conjecture predicts
existence of a
p
-adic family of non-torsion algebraic cycles, a systematic example of which arises from the arithmetic GGP cycles.

In this paper we explore local analogues of such phenomena for symplectic self-dual Galois representations:
connections between local
\varepsilon
-constants and local arithmetic cycles.
Specifically, for a
p
-adic de Rham representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, an element of
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
is considered as a local rational point or arithmetic cycle associated to
V
after Bloch and Kato.
For symplectic self-dual de Rham representations
V
of rank
2
,
we explore connections between the
\varepsilon
-constant
\varepsilon_{p}(V)\in\{\pm 1\}

and the Bloch–Kato subgroup
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
.

Our main result reveals a new structure on the Galois cohomology of rather general symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{R}}
-representations
{\mathcal{T}}
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
: the existence of a functorial decomposition

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})=H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\oplus H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})

(1.1)

into free
{\mathcal{R}}
-submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
of rank one which are Lagrangian respect to the symmetric Tate pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
, mirroring an intrinsic symplectic structure (see Theorem
1.3
). Here
{\mathcal{R}}
is any commutative Noetherian local complete
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra with finite residue field,
{\mathcal{T}}
a symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{R}}
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two with
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{{\mathcal{T}}})=0
for

\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}:={\mathcal{T}}\otimes{\mathcal{R}}/\mathfrak{m}_{{\mathcal{R}}}
, and functoriality refers to compatibility of
(
1.1
) with respect to variation of the pairs
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
.
A key feature of the local sign decomposition (
1.1
) is that for de Rham representations
T
precisely one of the submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
equals the Bloch–Kato subgroup
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
, the sign being determined by its completed
\varepsilon
-constant

\hat{\varepsilon}
:
an amalgamation of the

\varepsilon
-constant and Hodge–Tate weights.
In light of its functoriality,
the local sign decomposition
encodes variation of Bloch–Kato subgroups in a
p
-adic family
{\mathcal{T}}
of symplectic self-dual de Rham representations
T
, such as over a universal local deformation ring.
It is rooted in the principle of Kato’s local
\varepsilon
-conjecture
[
48
]
and relies on aspects of the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence
[
25
]
.

The local sign decomposition has diverse arithmetic consequences. This includes compatibility of
the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant and
\hat{\varepsilon}
-constants for rank two
p
-adic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
(see Theorem
1.10
)
and the
p
-parity conjecture (see Theorem
1.12
).
The compatibility
links Bloch–Kato subgroups of congruent de Rham representations with their
\hat{\varepsilon}
-constants, answering a question of Mazur and Rubin
[
MR
]
.
In turn we establish new cases of
the
p
-parity conjecture for Hilbert modular newforms for supercuspidal primes
p
.
This gives a new perspective on the work of Mazur–Rubin
[
MR
]
and Nekovář
[
NekMRl
,
NekMRtame
]
:
the local sign decomposition (
1.1
) is a categorification - an étale cohomological incarnation - of the numerical results of
[
MR
,
NekMRl
,
NekMRtame
]
.
As a concrete application, it significantly generalizes their results.

A special case of the local sign decomposition for a family of induced Galois representations
gives a new proof of Rubin’s 1987 conjecture
[
Ru
]

on the structure of anticyclotomic local units over the unramified quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
(see Theorem
1.13
), as well as a formulation and proof of its analogue over ramified quadratic extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
(see Theorem
1.18
). While the former
was resolved
[
BKO21
]
in 2021, the latter had not been conjectured.
The ramified case exhibits drastic variation of
\varepsilon
-constants and new phenomena abound, for the study of which Rubin-type conjecture lays foundation.

The local sign decomposition is interlaced with Iwasawa theory, which
seeks to study the arithmetic of a motive
M
as it deforms in a
p
-adic family
{\mathcal{M}}
.
One aims to formulate an analog of the Bloch–Kato conjecture for the family
{\mathcal{M}}
itself - an Iwasawa main conjecture.
It ought to account for the variation of Bloch–Kato subgroups over geometric specializations of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
However,
these subgroups typically
do not interpolate over a
p
-adic family, such as a non-ordinary family. In contrast our signed subgroups
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
do.
This leads to an analogue of the Bloch–Kato Selmer group for rank two
p
-adic symplectic self-dual families

{\mathcal{M}}
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
-representations
and a framework for integral Iwasawa theory at primes of non-semistable reduction.
As an illustration, we introduce an integral
p
-adic
L
-function for the anticyclotomic deformation of a CM elliptic curve for primes
p
ramified in the CM field, the first example of its kind (see Theorem
1.21
).

1.2.
Local sign decomposition

1.2.1.
Set up

Let
p
be an odd prime. Let
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
be an algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a continuous
{\mathcal{R}}
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, where
{\mathcal{T}}
is a free
{\mathcal{R}}
-module of finite rank and

{\mathcal{R}}
a commutative topological
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra
satisfying either of the following conditions:

i)

{\mathcal{R}}
is a
\mathrm{Jac}({\mathcal{R}})
-adically complete Noetherian semi-local ring such that

{\mathcal{R}}/\mathrm{Jac}({\mathcal{R}})
is a finite ring.

ii)

{\mathcal{R}}
is a finite product of finite extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

For example
{\mathcal{R}}
can be (the integer ring of) a finite field extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, a finite field of characteristic
p
or (a quotient of) the power series ring
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}[\![X_{1},\cdots,X_{r}]\!]
.
For the first example we use the notation
(R,T)
instead of
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
to emphasize that it does not correspond to a family of representations.

An
{\mathcal{R}}
-representation
{\mathcal{T}}
is symplectic self-dual if there exists a skew-symmetric and
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-equivariant pairing

\langle\;,\;\rangle:{\mathcal{T}}\times{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{R}}(1)
such that

{\mathcal{T}}\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{R}}(1))
for
{\mathcal{R}}(1):={\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}(1)
.
The arithmetic of the following Galois representations will be the focus of this paper.

Definition 1.1
.

A symplectic self-dual pair
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
is generic
if it satisfies the following.

i)

H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathfrak{m}})=0
if
{\mathcal{R}}
is semi-local,
{\mathfrak{m}}
any maximal ideal and
\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathfrak{m}}:={\mathcal{T}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}}}{\mathcal{R}}/{\mathfrak{m}}
.

ii)

H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=0
if
R:={\mathcal{R}}
a product of finite field extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
T:={\mathcal{T}}
.

We refer to Examples
1
and
2
in §
2.2
.
For any generic pair
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
, the Galois cohomology
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
is a free
{\mathcal{R}}
-module of rank
{\mathrm{rank}}_{{\mathcal{R}}}{\mathcal{T}}
. It commutes with base change for
{\mathcal{R}}
and the associated Tate pairing over
{\mathcal{R}}
is perfect.

de Rham representations

The seminal works of Fontaine
[
33
]
and Bloch–Kato
[
5
]
suggest that de Rham representations are geometric and akin to arithmetic phenomena.
They are defined over (the integer ring of) a finite field extension
2
2

2

In particular, they correspond to the notation
(R,T)
as above and not a family of representations
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
.
of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
We now describe some arithmetic invariants attached to symplectic self-dual de Rham representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.

For such a representation
V
,
let

\varepsilon_{p}(V)\in\{\pm 1\}

denote the
\varepsilon
-constant of the associated Weil–Deligne
representation (cf. §
2.5
).

For a de Rham
representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, let
\Gamma(V)\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
be the associated
\Gamma
-factor (cf. §
2.6.1
).

It is a
p
-adic analogue of the archimedean
\Gamma
-factor.
In the symplectic self-dual case we have
\Gamma(V)\in\{\pm 1\}
and
in the rank two case

\Gamma(V)=(-1)^{k-1}

where
(k,1-k)
are the Hodge–Tate weights of
V
with
k>0
(cf. Lemma
2.8
). Here and throughout the paper, we normalize the Hodge–Tate weights so that the
p
-adic cyclotomic character has Hodge–Tate weight
1
.

Definition 1.2
.

For a symplectic self-dual de Rham representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, the completed
\varepsilon
-constant
is defined by

\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V):=\Gamma(V)\varepsilon_{p}(V)\in\{\pm 1\}.

For a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-stable lattice
T\subset V
, we define
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(T)
as
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)
.

The notion of completed
\varepsilon
-constant will recur through the paper.

A fundamental invariant associated to a de Rham representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
is the Bloch–Kato subgroup

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V):=\mathrm{Ker}\left(H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}B_{\rm{crys}})\right).

For a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-stable lattice
T\subset V
, define
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=\iota^{-1}(H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V))
where
\iota:H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
is the natural map.

1.2.2.
Main result

We consider the arithmetic of symplectic self-dual representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two. The central result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
p
be an odd prime.
For all generic symplectic self-dual pairs
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations of rank two,
there is a functorial decomposition

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})=H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\oplus H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})

into free
{\mathcal{R}}
-submodules of rank one satisfying the following.

1)

The
{\mathcal{R}}
-submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})

are Lagrangian
3
3

3

That is, they are maximal isotropic submodules (cf. Definition
2.15
). Note that we use the terminology ‘Lagrangian’ though the Tate pairing is
not
skew symmetric.

with respect to the symmetric Tate pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
.

2)

For any morphism
{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{R}}
-representations, the canonical morphism induces
{\mathcal{R}}
-module homomorphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}).

3)

For any continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
{\mathcal{R}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
, the canonical morphism induces
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}
-module isomorphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\cong H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}}\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}).

In other words, the decomposition is compatible with base change.

4)

If
{\mathcal{T}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}=:T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham, then

H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(T)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T),

where
H^{1}_{\pm 1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T):=H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Moreover, the properties 3)-4) characterize the decomposition uniquely.

The above local sign decomposition is an étale manifestation of symplectic self-duality.
We emphasize its categorical nature: it is functorial and applies over rather general
{\mathcal{R}}
, for example over finite fields of characteristic
p
as well as universal local deformation rings (cf. Example
2
).
In view of the de Rham property 4) and the base change property 3) it encodes the variation of Bloch–Kato subgroups over any generic
p
-adic symplectic self-dual family of rank
2
in terms of
\hat{\varepsilon}
-constants of its de Rham specializations.

The uniqueness
is determined by
the variation over all generic symplectic self-dual
pairs of rank two,
while the de Rham property applies over specific base: (integer ring of) finite extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
We refer to §
5.2.2

for some examples of signed submodules.

Remark 1.4
.

i)

If
{\mathcal{R}}
is local or a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
then
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
apparently has zero or two Lagrangians.
For de Rham representations
T
, there are exactly two Lagrangians since

H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is a Lagrangian.
By Theorem
1.3
, the same holds for any generic
{\mathcal{T}}
,
i.e.

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
is a metabolic space.

ii)

Theorem
1.3
excludes non-generic representations and the prime
p=2
.
A variant of the local sign decomposition also holds for these excluded cases (see Theorem
3.5
).
Moreover, for odd primes
p
, non-generic representations seem to exhibit an integral decomposition mirroring the local sign decomposition (see §
F.3
for the de Rham and mod
p
cases).

1.2.3.
The signed submodules and universal norms

For any pair
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
as in §
1.2.2
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
define

Z_{\varepsilon}({\mathcal{T}}):=\{v\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\,|\,s(v)\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})\;\text{for any de Rham specialization $T_{s}$
with $\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(T_{s})=-\varepsilon\cdot 1$}\}.

A universal norm is an element of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
whose specialization corresponding to any de Rham specialization
T_{s}
lies in the Bloch–Kato subgroup
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
,
and
the universal norm subgroup
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})

is the set of all universal norms.

Proposition 1.5
.

For any generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
of rank two,
we have the inclusion

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})\subseteq Z_{\pm}({\mathcal{T}}).

It is an equality if
{\mathcal{R}}
contains Zariski dense specializations
s
with
T_{s}
de Rham and

\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(T_{s})=\mp 1
.
Moreover, the equality for both signs holds if and only if

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})=\{0\}.

Along with the Zariski density of signed crystalline points on a local deformation ring (cf. Theorem
A.2
), we obtain the following.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
be the universal framed deformation of a generic mod
p
representation
\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
. Then
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box})=0
.

1.3.
Applications

1.3.1.
Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constants, epsilon constants and the parity conjecture

Backdrop

We first introduce the parity conjecture and Mazur–Rubin local constants (cf.
[
MR
,
NekMRl
]
).

Let
M
be a pure motive over a number field
F
with coefficients in a number field
L_{M}
.
For a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
L_{M}
above
p
, let
V:=M_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the
p
-adic representation of
G_{F}:={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}/F)
associated to
M

with coefficients in
L:=L_{M,\mathfrak{p}}
.
Suppose that
M
is symplectic self-dual, i.e.
there exists an isomorphism

c:M\cong M^{\vee}(1)
of pure motives satisfying the equality
-c=c^{\vee}(1):M=(M^{\vee}(1))^{\vee}(1)\cong M^{\vee}(1)
.

Let
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F,V)
be the Block–Kato Selmer group associated to
V

and put
4
4

4

Note that
H^{0}(F,V)=0
since
M
is pure and self-dual. We still include the general definition since it will be used for geometric Galois representations.

\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)=\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\dim_{L}H^{0}(F,V).

Let
\varepsilon(V)\in\{\pm 1\}
be the associated global epsilon constant.
In fact
\varepsilon(V)
is defined for any geometric
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{F}
(cf. Definition
F.1
).
By the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture, such a
V
arises from a motive over
F
.
Conjecturally,
\varepsilon(V)
is the sign of the functional equation of the
L
-function
L(V,s)
.

We have the following mod
2
analogues of the Bloch–Kato conjecture.

Conjecture 1.7
.

(
p
-parity conjecture)
For any geometric symplectic self-dual
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{F}
,
we have

\varepsilon(V)=(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)}.

Conjecture 1.8
.

(relative
p
-parity conjecture)
Let
V
and
V^{\prime}
be any two geometric symplectic self-dual
p
-adic representations of
G_{F}
.
Then

(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V^{\prime})}=\varepsilon(V)/\varepsilon(V^{\prime}).

(1.2)

The relative
p
-parity conjecture implies that the
p
-parity conjecture for
V
and
V^{\prime}
are equivalent, allowing flexibility.
In practice, one seeks to study it for congruent or
p
-adic families of Galois
representations.
Suppose that
V
and
V^{\prime}
are residually symplectically isomorphic, i.e. there exist
G_{F}
-stable
symplectic self-dual lattices

T\subset V
and
T^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
such that

\overline{T}:=T/\mathfrak{p}T
and
\overline{T}^{\prime}:=T^{\prime}/\mathfrak{p}T^{\prime}

are symplectically isomorphic
over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}
.
Then Mazur and Rubin
[
MR
]
proved that there exist local invariants
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
such that

\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V^{\prime})\equiv\sum_{v:\text{finite}}\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})\quad\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2.

(1.3)

So the left-hand side of
(
1.2
)
is a product of local signs called arithmetic local constants, defined as follows.
For a finite place
v
, define
Selmer structures
\mathscr{F}_{v}
and
\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}

on
H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})
by

\mathscr{F}_{v}:=\mathrm{Im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})\right)

and

\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T^{\prime}})\cong H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})\right).

Then
\mathscr{F}_{v}
and
\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}
are Lagrangian subspaces,
whose relative position is measured by the following.

Definition 1.9
.

The Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})

is defined by

\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime}):=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\left(\mathscr{F}_{v}/\mathscr{F}_{v}\cap\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}\right)\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2\quad\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}.

In light of Conjecture
1.8
and (
1.3
),
Mazur and Rubin
[
MR
, p. 580]
predicted
5
5

5

They did not state a precise conjecture.

a link between the local constants
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
and

\varepsilon_{v}(V)/\varepsilon_{v}(V^{\prime})
for any finite place
v
.

(MR)

They proved some results towards the question (
MR
) for elliptic curves
[
MR
, §5-6]
, with applications to large Selmer ranks. (Subsequently, the Mazur–Rubin local constants found numerous arithmetic applications, such as
[
MR1
,
MR2
,
54
]
.)
A few years later,
Nekovář proved that

\varepsilon_{v}(V)/\varepsilon_{v}(V^{\prime})=(-1)^{\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})}

(1.4)

for
G_{F_{v}}
-representations
V,V^{\prime}

which are residually symplectically isomorphic
if
v\nmid p

[
NekMRl
]
or
if
v|p
and
V,V^{\prime}
become Barsotti–Tate over a tamely ramified abelian extension
[
NekMRtame
]
.
This compatibility
was a key to his
proof of the
p
-parity conjecture for
certain abelian varieties
and
Hilbert modular forms.

Now we describe our results towards the above problems.

A compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
p
be an odd prime.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be symplectic self-dual de Rham
representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank
2

which are residually symplectically isomorphic.
Put
V_{i}=T_{i}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then

\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{1})}{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{2})}=(-1)^{\delta_{p}(T_{1},T_{2})}.

(1.5)

This completely answers the Mazur–Rubin question (
MR
) for rank two representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
It is a
p
-local analogue of the relative parity Conjecture
1.8
.
For generic representations, this compatibility is a simple consequence of the functoriality of the local sign decomposition.
So, the latter may be viewed as an absolute version of the Mazur–Rubin constant. For non-generic representations, the compatibility is approached via this viewpoint (cf. §
F.3
).

Note that the compatibility (
1.5
) differs from (
1.4
)
if
the Hodge–Tate weights of
V
and
V^{\prime}
differ.
This was speculated by Nekovář, but neither did he state a precise conjecture

[
NekMRtame
, p. 2]
nor did the completed
\varepsilon
-constant appear in the prior results.
Theorem
1.10
is
the first general compatibility result for non-semistable Galois representations or general Hodge–Tate weights. The prior results rely on explicit description of the corresponding
\varepsilon
-constants
6
6

6

which is so far only available for trianguline or mildly ramified representations
and
p
-adic tools such as Breuil–Kisin modules
[
NekMRtame
]
.

Relative
p
-parity conjecture

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
F
be a number field and
p
an odd prime totally split in
F
.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be geometric symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-representations of
G_{F}
of rank two
which are residually symplectically isomorphic, where
L
is a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Put
V_{i}=T_{i}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then the relative
p
-parity Conjecture
1.8
is true for
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, i.e.

(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V_{1})-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V_{2})}=\varepsilon(V_{1})/\varepsilon(V_{2}).

The prior works of Nekovář
[
NekMRsome
,
NekMRl
,
NekMRtame
]
and Pottharst–Xiao
[
PX
]
primarily assume
V_{i}
to be trianguline at primes above
p
, in which case the
\varepsilon
-constants admit an elementary description.

p
-parity conjecture for Hilbert modular forms

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
F
be a totally real field and
p
an odd prime totally split in
F
.
Let
f

be a Hilbert modular newform over
F
of weight
k=\sum_{\sigma}k_{\sigma}\sigma\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}[{\mathrm{Hom}}(F,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}})]
and trivial central character.
Put
k_{0}=\max_{\sigma}k_{\sigma}
.
Let

\rho_{f}:G_{F}\to{\mathrm{Aut}}_{L}(V_{f})

be an associated

p
-adic Galois representation.
Suppose that
the residual representation
\overline{T}_{f}
is irreducible.
Then the
p
-parity Conjecture
1.7
is true for

V_{f}(k_{0}/2)
, i.e.

{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=0}L(V_{f}(k_{0}/2),s)\equiv\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}(F,V_{f}(k_{0}/2))\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2.

The above result allows
f
to be supercuspidal at primes above
p
and
k
arbitrary, complementing prior results towards the
p
-parity conjecture over totally real fields (see also Proposition
F.17
).

For elliptic newforms and non-Eisenstein primes
p
, i.e. primes for which the residual representation is ireducible, the
p
-parity conjecture is a theorem of Nekovář
[
NekMRsome
]
, the case of elliptic curves also due to Dockchitser brothers
[
32
]
.
If
F\neq{\mathbb{Q}}
, then
the parallel weight two and the
p
-ordinary parallel weight cases are
due to Nekovář

[
NekMRsome
,
NekMRtame
,
NekSC
]
,
the latter under some hypotheses.
The higher weight finite slope case
is due to Johansson–Newton
[
JN
]
,
which exploits the geometry of Hilbert modular eigenvariety.
Their work excludes supercuspidal primes
and requires an additional condition if
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
is odd

[
JN
, Rem. 1.2.4]
.

1.3.2.
Rubin’s conjecture

While exploring anticyclotomic Iwasawa theory of CM elliptic curves at primes
p
inert in the CM field, Rubin found a new structure on the module of twisted local units along the anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of the unramified quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and proposed it as a conjecture
[
Ru
]
.
Conditional on it, he envisioned an integral Iwasawa theory for this conjugate symplectic self-dual deformation, which is non-trianguline.

The conjecture

Let
p
be an odd prime and

K
the unramified quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

with integer ring
{\mathcal{O}}
.
Let
{\mathscr{F}}
be the Lubin–Tate formal group over
{\mathcal{O}}

for the uniformizing parameter
\pi:=-p
.
For
n\geq-1
, write
K_{n}=K({\mathscr{F}}[\pi^{n+1}])

and
K_{\infty}=\cup_{n}K_{n}
.
The Galois action on the
\pi
-adic Tate module
T_{\pi}{\mathscr{F}}

defines a conjugate symplectic self-dual character

\psi:{\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\infty}/K)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Aut}(T_{\pi}{\mathscr{F}})\cong{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
(cf. Definition
G.9
).

Let
K_{\infty}^{\rm ac}\subset K_{\infty}
be the anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of
K
and put
\Gamma={\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\infty}^{\rm ac}/K)
.
Define the Iwasawa algebra

\Lambda_{2}={\mathcal{O}}[\![{\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\infty}/K)]\!]\text{ and }\Lambda={\mathcal{O}}[\![\Gamma]\!]
.
Denote by
\Lambda^{\iota}
the
\Lambda
-representation of
G_{K}
of rank one, on which the
G_{K}
-action is given by
the character
G_{K}\to\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda^{\times}

sending
g\in G_{K}
to the image
[g^{-1}]
of
g^{-1}
in
\Gamma\subseteq\Lambda^{\times}
.
For a
\Lambda_{2}
-module
M
,
put
M^{*}=M\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}T_{\pi}{\mathscr{F}}^{\otimes-1}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}}(T_{\pi}{\mathscr{F}},M)
and let the Galois group act diagonally on it.

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, let
U_{n}
be the group of principal units in
K_{n}^{\times}
and
put
{U}_{\infty}^{*}=\varprojlim_{n}\,(U_{n}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathcal{O}})^{*}
.
Let

V_{\infty}^{*}

be the projection of
U_{\infty}^{*}
to the anticicyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-line,
which is a free
\Lambda
-module of rank
2
.
For a finite order character
\chi:\Gamma\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
,
let
\delta_{\chi}:V_{\infty}^{*}\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}

be the Coates–Wiles logarithmic derivative as in
[
15
, §2.1.1]
and
let
\Xi^{\pm}
denote the set of such
characters with conductor even or odd power of
p
respectively.

An insight of Rubin is to introduce
the
\Lambda
-submodules

\displaystyle V^{*,\pm}_{\infty}:=\{v\in V_{\infty}^{*}\;|\;\delta_{\chi}(v)=0\quad\text{for every $\chi\in\Xi^{\mp}$}\}.

He showed that
V^{*,\pm}_{\infty}\cong\Lambda
and

V^{*,+}_{\infty}\cap V^{*,-}_{\infty}=\{0\}
.
In 1987, Rubin proposed the following conjecture.

Theorem 1.13
.

(Rubin’s conjecture)
As
\Lambda
-modules, we have

V_{\infty}^{*}=V^{*,+}_{\infty}\oplus V^{*,-}_{\infty}.

The conjecture was resolved
in 2021 by us
[
BKO21
]
based on global as well as local tools.

Rubin’s conjecture and local sign decomposition

Proposition 1.14
.

For the Lubin–Tate character
\psi:G_{K}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
as above,
put
T_{\psi}={\mathcal{O}}(\psi)
and

\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}(T_{\psi}^{\otimes-1}(1)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}\Lambda^{\iota})
.
Then
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi}
is a generic symplectic self-dual

\Lambda
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
and there is a canonical isomorphism

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi})\cong V_{\infty}^{*}
.
Under this isomorphism, the local sign decomposition as in Theorem
1.3

coincides with Rubin’s decomposition as in Theorem
1.13
.

We refer to the proof of Corollary
G.27
for the above connection.

The local sign decomposition gives a new perspective on Rubin’s conjecture and
leads to refinements
(see Corollary
G.27
).
In retrospect the following variation of
\varepsilon
-constants initiates the conjecture.

Fact 1.15
.

For
\chi\in\Xi^{\pm}
, we have

\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(\mathrm{Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}(\psi\chi))=\pm 1.

Our approach also gives a proof of the generalization of
Rubin’s conjecture to conjugate symplectic self-dual Galois characters
\psi
over
K
which are ramified
or
with Hodge–Tate weights

(k+1,-k)
for
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
(cf.
[
Kazim
]
), which remained open
(see Theorem
G.29
and
Corollary
G.30
).

1.3.3.
An analogue of Rubin’s conjecture over ramified quadratic extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

Set-up

Let
p
be an odd prime. In this subsection
K
denotes a ramified quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
\omega_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
the associated quadratic character over
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote the integer ring of
K
.

Let
\psi
be a conjugate symplectic self-dual Galois character over
K
with Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
and (additive) conductor one
7
7

7

There are exactly two such
\psi
as explicitly constructed in §
G.2.3
.
.
It arises from a CM elliptic curve with CM by an order of an imaginary quadratic field in which
p
ramifies.
Let
L
be the extension of
K
generated by the image of
\psi
.
Put
T_{\psi}={\mathcal{O}}_{L}(\psi)
and let
\Lambda:={\mathcal{O}}_{L}[\![\Gamma]\!]
denote the anticyclotomic Iwasawa algebra for
\Gamma:={\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\infty}^{\rm ac}/K)
.

We have the following notable anticyclotomic variation of local
\varepsilon
-constants at
p

(cf. Proposition
G.7
).

Fact 1.16
.

Let
\chi
be a finite order anticyclotomic character over
K
of order
p^{n}>1
.
Then for
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
,

\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi^{a}))=\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi)),\quad\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi))=\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi^{-p})).

The above variation contrasts the unramified case, where the
\varepsilon
-constant only depends on the order of
\chi

(cf. Fact
1.15
). Note also that

{\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi)
is symplectic self-dual since
\psi\chi
is conjugate symplectic self-dual, and so the
\varepsilon
-constant
{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi))
is canonically defined, independent of the choice of additive character and embedding of its coefficient field into
{\mathbb{C}}
, unlike the case of
\psi\chi
(cf. §
2.5.2
).

Definition 1.17
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, define a partition of the set
\Xi_{n}
of anticyclotomic characters over
K
of order
p^{n}
by

\Xi_{n}=\Xi_{\psi,n}^{+}\cup\Xi_{\psi,n}^{-}=\{\chi\,|\,\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi^{-1}))=+1\}\cup\{\chi\,|\,\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}T_{\psi}(\chi^{-1}))=-1\}.

For a given
\psi
, the above partition is canonically defined in view of the preceding paragraph.
Note that
|\Xi_{\psi,n}^{+}|=|\Xi_{\psi,n}^{-}|
by Fact
1.16
. For
\cdot\in\{\emptyset,+,-\}
, put
\Xi^{\cdot}_{\psi}=\cup_{n}\Xi_{\psi,n}^{\cdot}
.
Consider the anticyclotomic deformation

\mathbb{T}_{\psi}=T_{\psi}^{\otimes-1}(1)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}\Lambda^{\iota}

where
\Lambda^{\iota}
is as in §
1.3.2
.
Note that
{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi}
is a
\Lambda
-conjugate symplectic self-dual
G_{K}
-representation of rank one.
Define

H^{1}_{\pm}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi})=\{v\in H^{1}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi})\;|\;\exp^{*}_{\chi}v=0\;\text{if $\chi\in\Xi^{\mp}_{\psi}$}\}

and likewise
H^{1}_{\pm}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\subset H^{1}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
where
\exp^{*}_{\chi}
is the composite of the dual exponential map and

H^{1}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\phi})=\varprojlim_{m}H^{1}(K_{m},T_{\phi}^{\otimes-1}(1))\to H^{1}(K_{n},T_{\phi}^{\otimes-1}(1))\to H^{1}(K_{n},T_{\phi}^{\otimes-1}(1))^{\chi^{-1}}=H^{1}(K,T_{\phi}^{\otimes-1}(1)(\chi))

where the first arrow is defined using Shapiro’s lemma, and the second is given by
x\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{n}/K)}x^{\sigma}\chi(\sigma)
.

Result

Theorem 1.18
.

Let
p
be an odd prime and
K
a ramified quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Let
\psi
be a conjugate symplectic self-dual Galois character over
K
of conductor one with Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
and
L
the finite extension of
K
generated by its image.
If
p=3
, suppose that either
K={\mathbb{Q}}_{3}(\sqrt{3})
or
K={\mathbb{Q}}_{3}(\sqrt{-3})
and
\psi\equiv\hskip-10.0pt/\ 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ {{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}}
.
Then the following holds.

i)

The
\Lambda
-modules
H^{1}_{\pm}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi})
are free of rank one.

ii)

The
\Lambda
-submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi})\subset H^{1}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi})
are Lagrangian.

iii)

As
\Lambda
-modules, we have

H^{1}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi})={H}^{1}_{+}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi})\oplus{H}^{1}_{-}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\psi}).

Moreover, in the excluded case
8
8

8

In this case
H^{1}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi})
is not a free
\Lambda
-module and a different formulation is necessary.
that
p=3
,
K={\mathbb{Q}}_{3}(\sqrt{-3})
and
\psi\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ {{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}}
, the assertions as in parts i)-iii) hold for the
\Lambda\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-modules
H^{1}_{\cdot}(K,{\mathbb{T}}_{\psi}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

The above result
can be interpreted in terms of local units
as in Rubin’s conjecture
(cf. Theorem
1.13
), yet we present the above formulation
to highlight
the general viewpoint of this paper.
It initiates the development of integral anticyclotomic Iwasawa theory of CM elliptic curves at primes
p
ramified in the CM field. As an illustration, we
construct an integral
p
-adic
L
-function
(see Theorem
1.21
).

We refer to Theorems
G.25
and

G.36
for
\psi
with arbitrary conductor and Hodge–Tate weights.

1.3.4.
A new example of a bounded
p
-adic
L
-function
for a symplectic self-dual deformation: anticyclotomic CM
p
-adic
L
-function for ramified (non-semistable) primes

Backdrop

The Hasse–Weil
L
-function is associated to any motive over a number field, a function of complex variable. An important problem is to find its
p
-adic analogue.

Despite constructions of
p
-adic
L
-functions in various settings, the conjectural framework remains elusive in general,
for example non-trianguline deformations.
The conjectural framework is known for
cyclotomic deformations of a motive for semistable
p
(cf.
[
74
,
76
]
) and for
Panchishkin deformations (cf.
[
38
]
), the former being trianguline at
p
.
The framework relies on the existence and properties of a family of isotropic subspaces of an appropriate size
in
p
-local Galois cohomology groups, and the properties are reflected in the corresponding
p
-adic
L
-functions.
In both cases, such an isotropic subspace is defined as the Galois cohomology group of a
p
-local subrepresentation, more precisely, a sub

(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over the Robba ring arising from a triangulation of the given family.
Since the subrepresentation is in general defined only over the rigid analytic space associated to the family,
the corresponding
p
-adic
L
-function typically has unbounded denominators
9
9

9

One may seek their bounded counterpart. As such, the signed Iwasawa theory initiated by Pollack
[
77
]
and the second-named author
[
55
]
sometimes works well.
. As for Panchishkin deformations, their definition itself assumes the existence of such a subrepresentation over a formal scheme, and
the conjectural
p
-adic
L
-function is expected to have bounded denominators.

For symplectic self-dual deformations, the authors expect that Lagrangian subspaces
manifest the aforementioned subspaces,
and the local sign decomposition is the first step towards a new class of
p
-adic
L
-functions.
Note also that the
\varepsilon
-constant variation, which is mild for semistable or Panchishkin deformations but wild in general, is an obstruction to the existence of a bounded
p
-adic
L
-function: for geometric specializations with global
\varepsilon
-constant
-1
, the central
L
-values
vanish.

As an application of the local sign decomposition, we construct a
bounded

p
-adic
L
-function for
the anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-deformation of a CM elliptic curve for primes
p
ramified in the CM field.
This conjugate symplectic self-dual deformation is non-trianguline at
p
and the variation of
\varepsilon
-constants is wild.

The anticyclotomic CM deformation at ramified primes

Iwasawa theory of CM elliptic curves began with the seminal work of Coates and Wiles
[
28
]
. On the analytic side, Katz
[
50
]
pioneered the construction of pertinent
p
-adic
L
-functions. The theory exhibits peculiar features along the anticyclotomic direction.

Let
E
be a CM elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}

and
{\mathcal{K}}
the CM field.
Suppose that
E
has CM by
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Let
p
be an odd prime and
{\mathcal{K}}_{\infty}^{\rm ac}
the anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of
{\mathcal{K}}

with the
n
-th layer
{\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\rm ac}
. The behavior of
the Mordell–Weil rank of
E({\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\rm ac})
is elemental to Iwasawa theory.
For
p
split in
{\mathcal{K}}
,
there exists a constant
c
such that for
n\gg 0
we have

\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\,E({\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\rm ac})=\frac{1-\varepsilon(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}})}{2}\cdot p^{n}+c.

(1.6)

In contrast if
p
is inert in
K
,
Greenberg
[
37
,
39
]
noticed that
global epsilon constants vary along
{\mathcal{K}}_{\infty}^{\rm ac}
and

\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\,E({\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\rm ac})-\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\,E({\mathcal{K}}_{n-1}^{\rm ac})=\varepsilon_{n}p^{n-1}(p-1)

for
n\gg 0
,
where
\varepsilon_{n}\in\{0,1\}
depending on the parity of
n
. Rubin initiated the study of Iwasawa theory
[
Ru
,
BKO21
]
related to this phenomenon.
Finally, if
p
is ramified in
{\mathcal{K}}
, then global epsilon constants vary drastically
(see Fact
1.19
) and

\mathrm{rank}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\,E({\mathcal{K}}_{n}^{\rm ac})=\frac{p^{n}-1}{2}+c

for
n\gg 0
(cf. Theorem
H.4
).
So new points of infinite order appear in basically every layer of
{\mathcal{K}}_{\infty}^{\rm ac}
.
Note that the variation of Mordell–Weil ranks in the above three cases is markedly different.

A basic problem is to develop anticyclotomic CM Iwasawa theory at ramified primes reflecting the above phenomena. As a first step, we construct an integral
p
-adic
L
-function.

Set-up

For an elliptic curve
E
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with CM by an order of
an imaginary quadratic field
\mathcal{K}
,
let
\phi
be the associated Hecke character over
{\mathcal{K}}
so that
L({\bf\phi},s)=L(E_{/{\mathbb{Q}}},s)
.

Let
p
be an odd prime ramified in
{\mathcal{K}}
and
K
the completion of
{\mathcal{K}}
at the prime above
p
.
Fix embeddings
\iota_{\infty}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
and
\iota_{p}:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
.
Let
{\phi}
also denote the associated
p
-adic Galois character over
{\mathcal{K}}
.
Put

\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{K}[\![\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{K}_{\infty}^{\rm ac}/\mathcal{K})]\!]
.
As a reflection of
the variation of local epsilon constants at
p
(cf. Fact
1.16
),
we have the following notable variation of the global epsilon constants
\varepsilon(\phi\chi)

along
{\mathcal{K}}_{\infty}^{\rm ac}
.

Fact 1.19
.

Let
\chi
be a finite order anticyclotomic character over
{\mathcal{K}}
of order
p^{n}>1
.
Then for
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
,

\varepsilon(\phi\chi^{a})=\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\varepsilon(\phi\chi),\quad\varepsilon(\phi\chi)=\varepsilon(\phi\chi^{-p}).

Definition 1.20
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, define a partition of the set
\Xi_{n}
of anticyclotomic characters over
{\mathcal{K}}
of order
p^{n}
by

\Xi_{n}=\Xi_{\phi,n}^{+}\cup\Xi_{\phi,n}^{-}=\{\chi\,|\,\varepsilon(\phi\chi^{-1})=+1\}\cup\{\chi\,|\,\varepsilon(\phi\chi^{-1})=-1\}.

Note that
|\Xi_{\phi,n}^{+}|=|\Xi_{\phi,n}^{-}|
by Fact
1.19
.
For
\cdot\in\{\emptyset,+,-\}
, put
\Xi^{\cdot}_{\phi}=\cup_{n}\Xi_{\phi,n}^{\cdot}
.
If
\chi\in\Xi^{-}_{\phi}
, then
L(\phi\chi^{-1},1)=0
since
\varepsilon(\phi\chi)=-1
. A natural problem:
to construct a
p
-adic
L
-function interpolating algebraic part of the
L
-values
L(\phi\chi^{-1},1)
for
\chi\in\Xi_{\phi}^{+}
.

Put

\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\phi}:={\mathrm{sgn}}({\varepsilon}({\phi})/\varepsilon_{p}({\rm Ind}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\phi_{p})),\qquad{\mathbb{T}}_{\phi}=T_{{\phi}}^{\otimes-1}(1)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\Lambda^{\iota}.

Result

Theorem 1.21
.

Let
E
be a CM elliptic curve defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with CM by an order of an imaginary quadratic field
{\mathcal{K}}
. Let
\phi
be the associated Hecke character.
Let
p\geq 3
be a prime ramified in
{\mathcal{K}}
and
K
the completion of
{\mathcal{K}}
at the prime above
p
.
If either
p\geq 5
or
p=3
and
{\phi}\equiv\hskip-10.0pt/\ 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ {{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}}
on
G_{K}
(Case I), then let
v_{\varepsilon}
be a basis of the
\Lambda
-module
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\phi})
. In the excluded case that
p=3
and
{\phi}\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ {{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}}
on
G_{K}
(Case II),
let
v_{\varepsilon}
be a basis of the
\Lambda\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-module
H^{1}_{\varepsilon}(K,\mathbb{T}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Then there exists a
p
-adic
L
-function

\mathscr{L}_{p,v_{\varepsilon}}(E)\in\begin{cases}\Lambda\qquad&(\text{Case I})\\
\;\Lambda\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}\qquad&(\text{Case II})\end{cases}

such that for
\chi\in\Xi^{+}_{\phi}
we have

\chi^{-1}(\mathscr{L}_{p,v_{\varepsilon}}(E))=\frac{1}{\delta_{v_{\varepsilon}}^{\omega_{E}}(\chi)}\cdot\frac{L({\phi}\chi^{-1},1)}{\Omega_{\infty}}

where
\exp^{*}_{\chi}(v_{\varepsilon})=\delta_{v_{\varepsilon}}^{\omega_{E}}(\chi)\cdot\omega_{E}
for
\omega_{E}
the Néron differential of a minimal Weierstrass model over
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
, and

\Omega_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is the CM period (cf. §
H.3.3
).

In the above interpolation formula the non-vanishing of
\delta_{v_{\varepsilon}}^{\omega_{E}}(\chi)
is a
consequence of Theorem
1.18

(cf. Corollary
3.2
).

Remark 1.22
.

In case II the integral elliptic unit main conjecture is subtle to formulate
[
46
]
and so is an integral refinement of

\mathscr{L}_{p,v_{\varepsilon}}(E)
.
The
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{K}}
-equivariant framework
[
46
]
may shed some light on the latter.

1.4.
Idea of the proof: local sign decomposition

The basic idea is to construct an involution
w_{\mathcal{T}}
on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
for generic symplectic self-dual pairs
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
of rank two so that
its eigenspace decomposition yields the local sign decomposition.

We present two methods for the construction of
w_{\mathcal{T}}
:

•

Colmez’s
w
-operator in the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence,

•

Kato’s local epsilon conjecture at
p
.

In the following, we outline the construction via Colmez’s
w
-operator and refer to §
4.2
for the approach via Kato’s local
\varepsilon
-conjecture. Here we only remark that the latter implies the existence of a symplectic structure on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathcal{T}})
. Both methods are useful in verifying the properties in Theorem
1.3
.

For simplicity of notation, suppose that
{\mathcal{R}}
is a complete local Noetherian ring with finite residue field.
Put

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}:=\varprojlim_{n}({\mathcal{R}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{R}}^{n}[\![X]\!][\frac{1}{X}])
.
Let

D:=D({\mathcal{T}})
be the étale
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{R}}
associated to
({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{T}})
and

\psi
be the left inverse of
\varphi
.
Then we have

D^{\psi=1}\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{{\mathcal{T}}}),

where the latter denotes the cyclotomic Iwasawa cohomology
[
22
]
.

Colmez
[
25
]
defined an operator

w:D^{\psi=0}\rightarrow D^{\psi=0,\iota},
by

w(x):=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\!\!\ \mathrm{mod}\ p^{n}}(1+X)^{1/i}\sigma_{-1/i^{2}}(\varphi^{n}\psi^{n}(1+X)^{-i}x).

where
\sigma_{a}(1+X)=(1+X)^{a}
and

\iota
is the
\Gamma
-twist arising from
\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma,\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{-1}
.
The
w
-operator corresponds
to the action of

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
on the
R[\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})]
-module
\Pi(D)
corresponding to
D
via the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence (cf.
[
25
]
).
Let
\delta_{D}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow{\mathcal{R}}^{\times}
be the character corresponding to
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{-1}\mathrm{det}_{{\mathcal{R}}}{\mathcal{T}}
by local class field theory. We remark that
\delta_{D}=\mathbf{1}
in our self-dual case. Using
D
and
w
, Colmez defined an
R[\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})]
-module
D\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, a sub
R[\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})]
-module
10
10

10

Note that the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-stability of
D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}
is a deep result of Colmez.

D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}
and

\Pi(D):=D\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}/D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}.

Moreover, he proved that (under some assumptions) there exists a natural
{\mathcal{R}}[\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})]
-linear isomorphism

D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}\cong\Pi(D)^{*}\otimes\delta_{D}\circ\mathrm{det}.

By definition of
D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}
, one has a natural
\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cong\Gamma
-linear map

(D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1})^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow D^{\psi=1}\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T})

for
\alpha_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.
We show that this map induces

(D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1})^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\gamma-1)\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{{\mathcal{T}}})/(\gamma-1)\cong H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{{\mathcal{T}}})

under our generic assumption (see Theorem
4.23
). Note that the action of
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
on
D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1}
naturally induces an action on the subquotient
(D^{\natural}\boxtimes_{\delta_{D}}\mathbf{P}^{1})^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\gamma-1)
under our self-dual assumption. This induced action finally yields an involution
w_{\mathcal{T}}
on
H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{{\mathcal{T}}})
through the above isomorphim. Namely, the involution
w_{\mathcal{T}}
is defined as the Galois theoretic counterpart of the automorphic action of
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
on
\Pi(D)^{*}
.

A link between the
w
-operator and the
p
-adic Hodge theoretic invariants like local
\varepsilon
-constant,

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}
and Hodge–Tate weights, is due to the third-named author
[
NaKato
]
.
It is based on Colmez’s
p
-adic Kirillov model and
Emerton’s local-global compatibility. The link leads to a connection between the local sign decomposition, Bloch–Kato subgroups and the completed
\varepsilon
-constant.

We give two different constructions of the involution
w_{\mathcal{T}}
as both approaches seem to be important for generalizations of the local sign decomposition.

1.5.
Vistas

Iwasawa theory

The proof of Rubin’s conjecture
[
BKO21
]
led to development of anticyclotomic CM Iwasawa theory at inert primes
[
15
,
16
,
18
,
10
,
17
]
. In the same vein we expect the ramified analogue of Rubin’s conjecture as in Theorem
1.18
to be ancillary to the case of ramified primes.
In a follow-up
[
13
]
we formulate and prove anticyclotomic CM main conjecture for such primes
p
of additive reduction.
Moreover, we begin the study of
p
-adic Waldspurger-type formulas
for
\mathscr{L}_{p,v_{\varepsilon}}(E)
(cf.
[
1
,
56
,
15
]
).

Applications of Theorem
1.18
to non-CM anticyclotomic Iwasawa theory as in
[
8
,
9
]
will also be explored.
In a different direction we plan to study the existence of a bounded
p
-adic
L
-function and Iwasawa theory
for the universal symplectic self-dual deformation over deformation ring of a mod
p
symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
. It will exploit the local sign decomposition and

[
63
,
27
]
.

Higher rank case

A basic problem is to find analogue of the local sign decomposition for general
self-dual Galois representations.
In a sequel to this paper
we will propose a new framework for the arithmetic of symplectic self-dual Galois representations of general rank and an analogue.

1.6.
Contents

Part
I
focuses on the local sign decomposition. It is local, and considers
p
-adic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two. Section
2
recalls basic notions. The main result is formulated in section
3
, followed by its proof in section
4
. Then
section
5
describes
a characterization of the signed submodules appearing in the local sign decomposition. It partly relies on the Zariski density of crystalline points with a given sign on universal local deformation rings, which is shown in Appendix
A
.

Part
II
presents some local and global applications. Section
F
establishes the compatibility of the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constants and
\hat{\varepsilon}
-constants, and some results towards the
p
-parity conjecture. Then a formulation and proof of Rubin-type conjectures over quadratic extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is given in section
G
. Based on it, section
H
describes a construction of the
p
-adic
L
-function

\mathscr{L}_{p,v_{\varepsilon}}(E)
.

Part I
Local sign decomposition

2.
Background

2.1.
Notation

Throughout, we fix a prime
p
.

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
with residue field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Let
K_{0}
denote the maximal unramified subfield. For a seaprable closure
\overline{K}
of
K
, let
K_{0}^{\rm un}
denote the maximal unramified field extension of
K_{0}
in
\overline{K}
. For a field
F
, define
\overline{F}
analogously and set
G_{F}:={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{F}/F)
.

Let
W_{K}
be the Weil group of
K
, i.e.
the subgroup of
G_{K}

consisting of elements whose images in
G_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
are integral powers of the geometric Frobenius.
It is a topological group such
that the inertia group
I_{K}
is open, and the relative topology
on
I_{K}
from
W_{K}
is the Krull topology.
Let
v_{K}:W_{K}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
be the valuation that sends the geometric Frobenius to
1
.
Let
\omega_{1}
denote the unramified character of
W_{K}

taking the value
q^{-1}
on any geometric Frobenius.

We normalize the Hodge–Tate weights so that the
p
-adic cyclotomic character over
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
has Hodge–Tate weight
1
.

2.2.
Symplectic self-dual Galois representations

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Let
T
be a continuous
R
-representation
11
11

11

In the rest of the paper we use the uniform notation
(R,T)
unlike the introduction.
of
G_{K}
, where
T
is a free
R
-module and

R
a commutative topological
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra
satisfying either of the following:

i)

R
is a
\mathrm{Jac}(R)
-adically complete Noetherian semi-local ring such that

R/\mathrm{Jac}(R)
is a finite ring.

ii)

R
is a finite product of finite extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
R
-representation
T
is symplectic self-dual if there exists a skew-symmetric and
G_{K}
-equivariant pairing

\langle\;,\;\rangle:T\times T\rightarrow R(1)

such that

T\cong{\rm Hom}_{R}(T,R(1))
for
R(1):=R\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}(1)
.

Example 1
.

a)

The
p
-adic Tate module
T=T_{p}E
of an elliptic curve
E
defined over
K
is symplectic self-dual with
R={\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
.

b)

For an
R
-representation
T
of rank two, it is symplectic self-dual if and only if
{\rm det}_{R}T
is the
p
-adic cyclotomic character
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
over
R
.

c)

For
any smooth proper variety
X
defined over
K
of dimension
2n+1
, the pair
(
R={\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
T=H^{2n+1}_{\text{ \rm\'{e}t}}(X_{\overline{K}},{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(n+1))
) is symplectic self-dual.

d)

Any cupsidal automorphic representation over a number field which is regular, algebraic and self-dual typically gives rise to symplectic self-dual Galois representations.
For example, let
f
be an elliptic newform of weight
k\geq 2
and trivial Neben-type,
L
the completion of its Hecke field at a prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
above
p
and

V_{\mathfrak{p}}(f)
the associated

G_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
-representation with Hodge–Tate weights

(1-k,0)
.
Then the pair (
R=L
,
T=V_{\mathfrak{p}}(f)(\frac{k}{2}))
is symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation.

e)

The induction of conjugate symplectic self-dual representations is self-dual. For example, let
K
be
a quadratic extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

and
T
a conjugate symplectic self-dual
R
-representation
12
12

12

This corresponds to replacing
T\times T
in Definition
2.1
with
T\times T^{c}
for
T
a representation of
G_{K}
,
c\in{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
the non-trivial element and
T^{c}
the inner conjugate.
of
G_{K}
.
Let
K_{\infty}
be the anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-extension of
K
and
\Lambda_{R}=R[\![{\mathrm{Gal}}(K_{\infty}/K)]\!]
the Iwasawa algebra.
Then the pair
(\Lambda_{R},{\mathrm{Ind}}_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}(T\otimes_{R}\Lambda_{R}))

is symplectic self-dual.
A concrete example arises from anticyclotomic
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-deformation of a conjugate symplectic self-dual
p
-adic Galois character over
K
.

Rank two case

This paper considers symplectic self-dual pairs
(R,T)
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations of rank two.
The above examples a), b), d) and e) are of rank two.
We explicate the prototypical ones:

Example 2
.

b1)

Any two dimensional Galois representation
\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Aut}}_{{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{T})
over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}
of characteristic
p
with
\det_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{T}
being the mod
p
cyclotomic character is symplectic self-dual.

b2)

Any rank two
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation
(R,T)
with
\det_{R}T=\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}
is symplectic self-dual, where
R
is the integer ring of a
p
-adic local field and
T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
de Rham.

b3)

For
\overline{\rho}
as in b1), let
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
be the universal framed deformation ring with determinant
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
the universal deformation (cf.
[
M
, Cor. in §23, §24]
). Then the pair
(R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box},{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box})
is symplectic self-dual.

2.3.
Symplectic self-dual Weil–Deligne representations

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

A Weil–Deligne representation
W
of
K

is a pair
(\rho,N)
where

\rho:W_{K}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(W)

is a finite dimensional continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-representation on
W
and

N
a nilpotent endomorphism of
W
satisfying

\rho(g)N\rho(g)^{-1}=\omega_{1}(g)N\qquad(g\in W_{K}).

Definition 2.2
.

A Weil–Deligne representation
W

is symplectic self-dual if there exists a non-degenerate skew-symmetric form
B
on
W

satisfying

B(gm,gn)=B(m,n)\quad(g\in W_{K}),\qquad B(Nm,n)+B(m,Nn)=0.

More generally,
W
is essentially self-dual of weight
t\in{\mathbb{Z}}
if

W\otimes\omega_{1}^{t/2}
is self-dual.

If
\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}W=2
, then it is symplectic self-dual if and only if
\det W=1
.

2.4.
p
-adic Galois representations and Weil–Deligne representations

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

For any prime
\ell\neq p
, by fixing an isomorphism
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
,
we can associate a Weil–Deligne representation of
W_{K}
over
{\mathbb{C}}

with an
\ell
-adic representation of
G_{K}
simply by Grothendieck’s monodromy theorem.
Moreover, there is an equivalence of categories between
\ell
-adic representations of
G_{K}
and
\ell
-integral Weil–Deligne representations of
W_{K}
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
(cf.
[
86
, §1]
).
The case
\ell=p
is more complicated.
Fontaine’s
D_{\rm{pst}}
-functor as recalled below associates
a Weil–Deligne representation to a
p
-adic de Rham representation.
The association is far from being a bijection and in particular, it does not encode the Hodge filtration.
Though we describe the properties of
symplectic self-dual Galois and Weil–Deligne representations in parallel,
we suggest the reader to keep an eye on the type of representation.
As such, we use the letter
W
to denote
Weil–Deligne representations and
V
for
p
-adic Galois representations.

Let
L
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Let
V
be a
p
-adic representation of
G_{K}
with coefficients in
L
.
Fix an algebraic closure
\overline{K}
of
K
.

For a finite extension
K^{\prime}/K
in
\overline{K}
, put

D_{\mathrm{pst}}^{K^{\prime}}(V):=(\mathbf{B}_{\rm{st}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}V)^{G_{K^{\prime}}}

where
\mathbf{B}_{\rm{st}}
is the
p
-adic semi-stable period ring of Fontaine.
This is a
(K^{\prime}_{0}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}L)
-vector space
with operators
\varphi
and
N
satisfying
N\circ\varphi=p\varphi\circ N
.
Furthermore, if
K^{\prime}/K
is normal, the natural action of
G_{K}
on
\mathbf{B}_{\rm{st}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}V
defines a
K^{\prime}_{0}
-semiliner action of

\mathrm{Gal}(K^{\prime}/K)
on
D_{\mathrm{pst}}^{K^{\prime}}(V)
that commutes with
\varphi
and
N
.
In other words,
D_{\mathrm{pst}}^{K^{\prime}}(V)
is a
(\varphi,N,G_{K^{\prime}/K})
-module.
Put

D_{\mathrm{pst}}(V):=\varinjlim_{K^{\prime}/K}D_{\mathrm{pst}}^{K^{\prime}}(V)

where
K^{\prime}
runs through all finite extensions of
K
.
Then
D_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)
is
(K_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}L)
-module
with the operator
\varphi
and
N
satisfying
N\circ\varphi=p\varphi\circ N
, and
a natural
K_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}
-semi-linear action of the Weil group
W_{K}
that commutes with
\varphi
and
N
.
If
V
is de Rham, hence, potentially semi-stable,
then we have
\dim_{K_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}}D_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}V
.

For
\sigma\in W_{K}
and
w\in D_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)
,
define a
K_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}
-linear action of
\sigma
on
w
by

\sigma\cdot w:=\sigma\varphi^{fv_{K}(\sigma)}w

where
f=[K_{0}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]
and
v_{K}
is as in §
2.1
.
Consider the map

K_{0}^{\rm{un}}\otimes L\rightarrow\overline{K}\otimes L=\prod_{\tau:L\hookrightarrow\overline{K}}\overline{K}.

Pick an embedding
\tau:L\hookrightarrow\overline{K}
and an isomorphism

\iota:\overline{K}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
.
Then the above
K_{0}^{\mathrm{un}}
-linear action defines
a continuous
{\mathbb{C}}
-representation

\rho_{\iota\circ\tau}:W_{K}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(D_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)\otimes_{\iota\circ\tau}{\mathbb{C}}).

Definition 2.3
.

A Weil–Deligne representation
associated to a
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{K}
and the embedding
\iota\circ\tau:L\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is given by

\mathrm{WD}(V)_{\iota\circ\tau}=(\rho_{\iota\circ\tau},N).

We often simply denote it by
\mathrm{WD}(V)
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
V
be a symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{K}
.
Then the associated Weil–Deligne representation
\mathrm{WD}(V)_{\iota\circ\tau}

is essentially symplectic of weight
-1
.

Proof.

By construction, we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{WD}(V^{*}(1))\cong\mathrm{WD}(V^{*})\otimes\omega_{1}\cong\mathrm{WD}(V)^{*}\otimes\omega_{1}.

The assertion follows from this.
∎

We have the following compatibility between the Weil–Deligne and induction functors.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
K
and
L
be
p
-adic local fields, and
\overline{K}
an algebraic closure.
Let
K^{\prime}\subset\overline{K}
be a finite extension of
K
and
V
a
p
-adic representation of
G_{K^{\prime}}
over
L
.
Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{WD}(\mathrm{Ind}_{K^{\prime}/K}V)\cong\mathrm{Ind}_{K^{\prime}/K}\mathrm{WD}(V).

Proof.

This follows from definitions, whose details we leave to the interested reader.
∎

2.5.
Epsilon constants

Let
K
be a local field.

For a Weil–Deligne representation
W
of
W_{K}
,
a non-trivial additive character
\xi:K\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
and
a Haar measure
dx
, let
13
13

13

Throughout Part
I
, we use the notation
\varepsilon(\cdot)
instead of
\varepsilon_{p}(\cdot)
as in §
1
or part
II
.

\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}

be the associated Deligne–Langlands epsilon constant (cf.
[
T
,
TLC
]
).

If
W
arises from a motive over a number field,
then
\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx)
is conjecturally related to
local component of the conjectural functional equation of the associated
L
-function.

2.5.1.
Self-dual epsilon constants

A non-trivial additive character
\xi
on
K
defines the
Pontryagin duality

K\cong K^{\vee},y\mapsto(x\mapsto\xi(xy)).

Let
dx_{\xi}
be the Haar measure on
K
which is self-dual with respect to
\xi
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If a Weil–Deligne representation
W
is essentially symplectic self-dual of weight
-1
, then

\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi})\in\{\pm 1\}

and
it does not depend on the choice of
\xi
.

Proof.

This is well-known. We include a proof just to confirm our normalization.

Another additive character is of the form
\xi_{a}(x):=\xi(ax)
for some
a\in K^{\times}
.
Let
d
be the dimension of
W
.
Since
\det W=\omega_{1}^{\frac{d}{2}}
and
dx_{\xi_{a}}=\omega_{1}(a)^{\frac{1}{2}}dx_{\xi}
,
by (3.4.3) and (3.4.4) of
[
T
]
, we have

\varepsilon(W,\xi_{a},dx_{\xi_{a}})=\det W(a)\,\omega_{1}(a)^{-d}\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi_{a}})=\det W(a)\,\omega_{1}(a)^{-d}\,\omega_{1}(a)^{\frac{d}{2}}\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi})=\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi}).

Also by (3.4.7) of
[
T
]
, we have

\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi})\varepsilon(W^{*}(\omega_{1}),\xi_{-1},dx_{\xi})=1.

Since
W\cong W^{*}(\omega_{1})
, it follows that
\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi})^{2}=1
.
∎

From now,
we denote the epsilon constant
\varepsilon(W,\xi,dx_{\xi})
simply by
\varepsilon(W)
.

2.5.2.
Epsilon constants for
symplectic self-dual de Rham representations

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and

V
a symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{K}
with coefficients in a
p
-adic local field
L
.
Then, the epsilon constant

\varepsilon(\mathrm{WD}(V)_{\iota\circ\tau})\in\{\pm 1\}

does not depend on the choice of embeddings
\iota
and
\tau
as in Definition
2.3
by Rohrlich
[
Ro
]
.
This is the epsilon constant associated with
V
, which we
simply denote by
\varepsilon(V)
.

2.6.
Gamma constants and completed epsilon constants

Since the Weil–Deligne
representation
\mathrm{WD}(V)
does not encode
the Hodge filtration of a de Rham representation
V
, neither does
the epsilon constant
\varepsilon(V)
.
On the other hand, if
V
arises from a motive over a number field, the archimedean epsilon constants are
determined by the associated Hodge realization. We introduce a modification of the epsilon constant
\varepsilon(V)
by a
p
-adic counterpart of the archimedean epsilon constant.

2.6.1.
Gamma constants

Kato and Perrin-Riou independently defined the following
\Gamma
-constant for a de Rham representation
V
encoding its

p
-adic Hodge theoretic invariants.

Definition 2.7
.

For a de Rham
L
-representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
over a
p
-adic local field
L
, the
\Gamma
-constant is defined by

\Gamma(V):=\prod_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Gamma^{*}(r)^{-\dim_{L}{\rm gr}^{-r}D_{{\rm dR}}(V)}

where

\Gamma^{*}(r):=\begin{cases}(r-1)!\quad&(r\geq 1)\\
\frac{(-1)^{r}}{(-r)!}\quad&(r\leq 0).\end{cases}

If
V
arises from a motive over a number field, then the
\Gamma
-constant is related to the associated archimedean epsilon constants.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(k_{0},k_{1},\cdots,k_{n-1},k_{n})
be Hodge–Tate weights of
a de Rham
L
-representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
without counting multiplicity
so that
k_{0}<k_{1}<\cdots<k_{n-1}<k_{n}
.
If
V
is symplectic self-dual, then
n
is odd,

k_{i}=1-k_{n-i}
for
0\leq i\leq n
and

\displaystyle\Gamma(V)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{i=0}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}k_{i}\dim_{L}{\rm gr}^{-k_{i}}D_{{\rm dR}}(V)}=(-1)^{\sum_{i>0}i\dim_{L}{\rm gr}^{i}D_{{\rm dR}}(V)}.

Proof.

Note that the Hodge–Tate weights of
V^{*}(1)
without counting multiplicity are the following tuple of integers in increasing order

1-k_{n}<1-k_{n-1}<\cdots<1-k_{1}<1-k_{0}.

Hence
V^{*}(1)\cong V
implies that
k_{i}=1-k_{n-i}
for
0\leq i\leq n

and
\dim_{L}{\rm gr}^{-k_{i}}D_{{\rm dR}}(V)=\dim_{L}{\rm gr}^{-k_{n-i}}D_{{\rm dR}}(V)
.

In particular,
n
is odd as otherwise
k_{n/2}\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For
i<n/2
, we have

\Gamma^{*}(k_{i})\Gamma^{*}(k_{n-i})=\frac{(-1)^{k_{i}}}{(-k_{i})!}(k_{n-i}-1)!=(-1)^{k_{i}}

since
k_{i}\leq 0<k_{n-i}
.
The assertion follows.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
V
be a two dimensional symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}

such that
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=\{0\}
.
Let
(k,1-k)
be the Hodge–Tate weights of
V
for an integer
k\geq 1
.
Then
\Gamma(V)=(-1)^{k-1}
and

\mathrm{dim}_{L}(H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))=1
.

Proof.

The assertion on the
\Gamma
-constant is a special case of Lemma
2.8
.
As for the latter, note that

\mathrm{dim}_{L}(\mathrm{Fil}^{i}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))=\begin{cases}0&(k\leq i),\\
1&(-k<i<k),\\
2&(i\leq-k).\end{cases}

In particular, we have

\mathrm{dim}_{L}(H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))=\mathrm{dim}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))=1.

∎

2.6.2.
Completed epsilon constants

The following notion is ancillary to the paper.

Definition 2.10
.

For a symplectic self-dual de Rham representation
V
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
,
define the completed epsilon constant
\hat{\varepsilon}(V)
by

\hat{\varepsilon}(V):=\Gamma(V)\varepsilon(V)\in\{\pm 1\}.

The sign of
\hat{\varepsilon}(V)
will often be referred to as the sign of
V
.

2.7.
Galois cohomology of generic representations

In this subsection we present basic properties of Galois cohomology of generic
p
-adic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.

Definition 2.11
.

A
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representation
(R,T)
is generic if

i)

H^{i}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{\mathfrak{m}})=0
for
R

a
\mathrm{Jac}(R)
-adically complete Noetherian semi-local ring with

R/\mathrm{Jac}(R)
finite,
i\in\{0,2\}
,
{\mathfrak{m}}
any maximal ideal of
R
and
\overline{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}:=T\otimes_{R}R/{\mathfrak{m}}
,

ii)

H^{i}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=0
for
R
a product of finite field extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
i\in\{0,2\}
.

Otherwise, we say that
(R,T)
is non-generic or anomalous
14
14

14

Indeed, for an elliptic curve
E
over
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

with good reduction at
p
, the
p
-adic Tate module is anomalous if and only if
p
is an anomalous prime.
.

In the case i) the generic assumption implies that

\mathrm{rank}_{R/{\mathfrak{m}}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}_{{\mathfrak{m}}})={\mathrm{rank}}_{R}T
, and the Tate dual
T^{*}(1)
satisfies the same by Tate duality and the Euler–Poincare characteristic formula.
In particular, for a symplectic self-dual representation,
the generic condition is equivalent to

H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}_{{\mathfrak{m}}})=0.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank
n
.

1)

We have
H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0
for
i=0,2
.

2)

For any finite
R
-module
M
, the canonical map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}M)

is an
isomorphism.

3)

The
R
-module
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
is free of rank
n
.

4)

For any ideal
I
of
R
, the canonical map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}R/I\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R/I)

is
an isomorphism.

5)

Suppose that
p
is odd. Let
\gamma
be a topological generator of
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

and put
H^{1}_{\rm Iw}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n}}),T)
.
Then
the canonical map

H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)/(\gamma-1)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

is
an isomorphism.

6)

The Tate pairing, which we denote by

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow R,\qquad(x,y)\mapsto\{x,y\}_{T}

is a perfect pairing. It induces a symmetric pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Proof.

Note that 1) is clear, and 4) is a special case of 2).

As for 2), it is trivial for
M=R^{m}
. For a general
M
, take an exact sequence

0\rightarrow N\rightarrow R^{m}\rightarrow M\rightarrow 0

of
R
-modules. It induces the exact sequence

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}N\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}R^{m}\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow 0

(2.1)

and

0\rightarrow T\otimes_{R}N\rightarrow T\otimes_{R}R^{m}\rightarrow T\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow 0.

In turn, the latter induces the exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}N)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R^{m})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}M)\rightarrow 0

(2.2)

since
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}M)=H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}N)=0
. By
considering the canonical map from (
2.1
) to

(\ref{Appendix, equation, (ii)})
,
it follows that the canonical map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}M)

is surjective. Since
M
is an arbitrary
R
-module, this surjectivity also holds for
N
. Then, considering the
canonical map from (
2.1
) to

(\ref{Appendix, equation, (ii)})
again,
we deduce that
the canonical map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}M)

is an isomorphism by a simple diagram chase.

Henceforth, we suppose that

R
is a
\mathrm{Jac}(R)
-adically complete Noetherian semi-local ring with

R/\mathrm{Jac}(R)
finite. A slight variant of the following arguments applies if
R
is a product of finite field extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, which we leave to the interested reader.

We prove 3) by induction on the length of
R
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-module.
We may assume that
R
is local.
When
R=\mathbb{F}
is a finite field of characteristic
p
, note that
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})=n
by the Euler–Poincaré formula.
For a local
R
with residue field
{\mathbb{F}}
, take a non-zero ideal
I
of
R
such that
\mathfrak{m}_{R}I=0
.
The exact sequence

0\rightarrow I\rightarrow R\rightarrow R/I\rightarrow 0,

induces the following exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}I\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R/I)\rightarrow 0,

where we note that
T\otimes_{R}I=\overline{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}I
since
\mathfrak{m}_{R}I=0
. The latter implies that

\mathrm{length}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T))=n\cdot\mathrm{length}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(R)

since
\mathrm{length}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R/I))=n\cdot\mathrm{length}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}(R/I)

by the induction hypothesis. Moreover, by the induction hypothesis, we have an isomorphism

(R/I)^{n}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R/I)=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}R/I.

Take an
R
-linear lift

f:R^{n}\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

of the above isomorphism. It is surjective by Nakayama’s lemma.
Hence, considering the lengths of both sides, it follows that
f
is an isomorphism.

We now consider 5). In view of the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)/(\gamma-1)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\gamma=1}\rightarrow 0,

it suffices to show that
H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\gamma=1}=0
, which is equivalent to

H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\Delta=1}=0
,
where
\Delta\subset\Gamma
denotes the torsion subgroup.
Let
\gamma_{0}
be a topological generator of the free part
\Gamma_{\mathrm{free}}
of
\Gamma
.
By 1), we have

H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\Delta=1}/(\gamma_{0}-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)/(\gamma-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0.

Since
H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\Delta=1}
is a finitely generated (torsion)
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![\Gamma_{\mathrm{free}}]\!]\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\![[\gamma_{0}]-1]\!]
-module, Nakayama’s lemma implies
that
H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{\Delta=1}=0
.

For 6), we may assume that
R
is local with residue field
{\mathbb{F}}
. It suffices to show that the
R
-linear map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),R),\qquad x\mapsto[y\mapsto\{x,y\}_{T}]

is bijective. Since both the sides are free
R
-modules of the same rank, it suffices to show it is surjective.
In view of Nakayama’s lemma, it suffices to show that the surjectivity holds after tensoring with
\mathbb{F}
over
R
.
By 4), the latter map is given by

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{*}(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}),\mathbb{F}):x\mapsto[y\mapsto\{x,y\}_{\overline{T}}],

which is bijective by Tate duality.
The induced pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is symmetric by
[
18
, Prop. 3.4]
.
∎

For a symplectic self-dual pair
(R,T)
we set

H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}):=H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p},\qquad R[1/p]:=R\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}.

In the anomalous case we have the following variant of Proposition
2.12
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
(R,T)
be a symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank
n
.
Assume that

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0.

1)

We have
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0
and the
R[1/p]
-module
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is locally free of rank
n
.

2)

For any continuous
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, we have
