H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0

for
i\in\{0,2\}
, and
the canonical map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{R[1/p]}R^{\prime}[1/p]\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})

is
an isomorphism.

3)

The Tate pairing

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow R[1/p],\qquad(x,y)\mapsto\{x,y\}_{T}

is perfect, and it induces a symmetric pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proof.

By the assumption, the perfect complex
C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is quasi-isomorphic to
a bounded complex
P^{\bullet}
of finite projective
R[1/p]
-modules such that
P^{i}=0
for
i\geq 2
. In view of the Tate duality and the self-duality of
(R,T)
, the complex

C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}

is also quasi-isomorphic to

Q^{\bullet}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(P^{\bullet},R[-2])

whose non-zero terms are in degree
[1,m]
for some
m\geq 1
.

Since
H^{i}(Q^{\bullet})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{i}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0
for
i\geq 2
, a standard argument shows that

H^{1}(Q^{\bullet})
is projective and
Q^{\bullet}
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex
H^{1}(Q^{\bullet})[-1]
. Hence, the proposition follows from the base change
properties, the Tate duality and the Euler–Poincaré formula for

C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
∎

To verify the assumption of the above proposition, a useful criterion is the following.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
(R,T)
be a symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation.
Then

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0
if and only if

H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{s})=0
for all the
p
-adic representations
T_{s}
obtained from a base change of

T\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

We leave proof of the above elementary lemma to the interested reader.

2.8.
Lagrangian submodules

Let
R
be a commutative Noetherian ring.

Let
M
be a finitely generated projective
R
-module with a pairing
(\;,\;)

that induces an
R
-module isomorphism
M\cong\mathrm{Hom}(M,R)
.

Definition 2.15
.

An
R
-submodule
N
of
M
is Lagrangian
15
15

15

We emphasize that the pairing
(\;,\;)
is allowed to be symmetric.

if

i)

N
and
M/N
are projective,

ii)

N
is isotropic, and

iii)

the pairing
(\;,\;)

induces
R
-module isomorphisms
N\cong\mathrm{Hom}(M/N,R)
.

We have the following preliminary regarding Langrangian submodules of a rank two module.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
R
be
a commutative Noetherian local ring such that
2\in R^{\times}
.
Let
M
be a free
R
-module of rank two with a symmetric pairing
(\;,\;)

which induces an isomorphism
M\cong\mathrm{Hom}(M,R)
of
R
-modules.
Then the number of Lagrangian
R
-submodules of
M
is zero or two.

Proof.

Since
M
is of rank two, Lagrangian submodules
are of the form
Rv
such that
(v,v)=0
, and
Rv
and
M/Rv
are both free.

Given such an element
v
.
Put
k:=R/\mathfrak{m}_{R}
and
\overline{M}:=M\otimes_{R}k
.
Since
M/Rv
is free,

k\overline{v}
is also a Lagrangian submodule of
\overline{M}
.
Take
\overline{w}\in\overline{M}
such that

(\overline{v},\overline{w})=1

and a lift
w\in M
of
\overline{w}
.
Then by Nakayama’s lemma,
\{v,w\}
is a basis of
M
such that
({v},{w})\in R^{\times}
.
If necessary, replacing
w
by

w-\frac{(w,w)}{2(v,w)}v
, we may assume that
(w,w)=0
. Then
Rw
defines another Lagrangian.

Now pick a Lagrangian
N
.
It is of the form
N=Rx
, write

x=av+bw

for
a,b\in R
.
Then
(x,x)=0
implies that
ab=0
.
Since the pairing induces the isomorphism
N\cong\mathrm{Hom}(M/N,R)
, at least one of
a
or
b
must be a unit of
R
.
Hence, we have
N=Rv
or
N=Rw
.
∎

As for this paper, a key example of Lagrangians arises from generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations
T
, whose cohomology
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is endowed with a symmetric Tate pairing.

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
p
be an odd prime and
T
a generic symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two.
Then
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
has exactly two Lagrangian submodules with respect to the Tate pairing, one of them being the Bloch–Kato subgroup
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Proof.

Since
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is free, one has

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\cap H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)

and
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)[1/p]
.
Therefore, we obtain

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)^{\perp}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\cap H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)^{\perp}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\cap H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T).

∎

Our main theorem shows the existence of a Lagrangian for any generic symplectic self-dual representation of rank two,
and so that of exactly two Lagrangians when
R
is local.
We canonically label these submodules and for de Rham representations, it is linked with Bloch–Kato subgroups
via completed epsilon constants.

3.
Main result

The central result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
p
be an odd prime.
For all generic symplectic self-dual pairs
(R,T)
of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations of rank two
there is a functorial decomposition

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\oplus H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)

into free
R
-submodules of rank one with the following properties.

1)

The
R
-submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)

are Lagrangian
with respect to the symmetric Tate pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

2)

For any morphism
T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
of
R
-representations, the canonical morphism induces
R
-module homomorphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T^{\prime}).

3)

For any continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, the canonical morphism induces
R^{\prime}
-module isomorphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}\cong H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}).

In other words the decomposition is compatible with base change.

4)

If
T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham, then

H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}(T)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T),

where
H^{1}_{\pm 1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T):=H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Moreover, the properties 2)-4) characterize the decomposition uniquely.

The following is an immediate application of Theorem
3.1
,
which will be used in our construction of
p
-adic
L
-function at additive primes (cf. Theorem
H.15
).

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank two.
Let
v_{\epsilon}
be a basis of
H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)

for
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Suppose that
V_{s}
is a de Rham specialization of
T
with sign

\hat{\varepsilon}(V_{s})=\epsilon
.
Then we have

\exp^{*}_{V_{s}}(v_{\epsilon})\not=0

for
\exp^{*}_{V_{s}}
the dual exponential map.

Remark 3.3
.

The above non-vanishing is a generalization of
[
BKO21
, Cor. 2.2]
, which is a consequence of the proof of Rubin’s conjecture.

If we use the Lagrangian propery 1) in addition to the properties 3) and 4),
the uniqueness of the local sign decomposition is relatively easily proven.

Proposition 3.4
.

The local sign decomposition as in Theorem
3.1
is uniquely determined
by the properties 1), 3) and 4).

Proof.

In light of the base change property 3) of the local sign decomposition and completeness of
R
, we may assume that
R
is an Artinian semi-local ring. Since
R
is then a finite product of Artinian rings, we may further assume that
R
is local.
In light of the base change property 3) we finally reduce to the case that
T
is the universal framed deformation

\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{T}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
of a residual representation
\overline{\rho}
whose determinant is the cyclotomic character (see b3) of Example
2
).

Suppose that
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{T})
has two decompositions

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{T})=Rv_{+}\oplus Rv_{-}=Rw_{+}\oplus Rw_{-}

satisfying the properties 1), 3) and 4) of Theorem
3.1
.
Since there are only two Lagrangian submodules by Lemma
2.16
,
it follows that
Rv_{+}=Rw_{+}
or
Rv_{+}=Rw_{-}
.

Suppose that
Rv_{+}=Rw_{-}
and then
Rv_{-}=Rw_{+}
.
Let
s
be a crystalline specialization of
{\mathbb{T}}
(cf. Theorem
A.1
), and

T_{s}
denotes the specialization of

\mathbb{T}
at
s
.
Put
\epsilon=\hat{\varepsilon}(T_{s})
. Then both

R\,s(v_{-\epsilon})
and
R\,s(w_{-\epsilon})
equal

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
by the property 4).
Therefore, we obtain
R\,s(v_{+})=R\,s(v_{-})
, a contradiction.
∎

For either the anomalous case or the prime
p=2
, we have the following variant of the local sign decomposition.
Recall that by definition,

H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}):=H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
for
i=0,1,2
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
p
be any prime.
Let
(R,T)
be a symplectic self-dual pair of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations of rank two. Assume that

H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=0.

Then
there is a functorial decomposition

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})=H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\oplus H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})

into locally free
R[1/p]
-submodules of rank one with the following properties.

1)

The
R[1/p]
-submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})

are Lagrangian
with respect to the symmetric Tate pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

2)

For any continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, the canonical morphism induces
R^{\prime}[1/p]
-module isomorphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{R[1/p]}R^{\prime}[1/p]\cong H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

In other words the decomposition is compatible with base change.

3)

If
(L,V)
is any de Rham specialization of
(R,T)
, then

H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}(V)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V),

4.
Construction of the local sign decomposition

The main idea is to construct an involution
w_{T}
on the Galois cohomology
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
whose eigenspace decomposition leads to the local sign decomposition as in Theorem
3.1
.

In this section, we introduce two methods for the construction of
w_{T}
.
The first is based on Kato’s local epsilon conjecture for rank two representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
as established by the third-named author and Jacinto
[
NaKato
,
45
]
. For generic symplectic self-dual representations,
the conjecture
leads to a symplectic pairing on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
,
whose comparison with the symmetric Tate pairing yields the involution
w_{T}
.
Since it relies on the abstract symplectic pairing, this definition of
w_{T}
is not constructive,
while our second method is:
we show that
w_{T}
is essentially
Colmez’s operator
w_{*}
in
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-theory.
Both constructions are elemental to
properties of the local sign decomposition.

Throughout, we fix a prime
p
.

4.1.
Kato’s local epsilon conjecture

This subsection describes the eponymous conjecture and some results towards it following
[
48
,
62
,
NaKato
]
.

4.1.1.
Set-up

Let
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
be an algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}:={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.
Let
W_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\subset G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
be the Weil group of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and

{\mathrm{rec}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}\simeq W_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}^{\rm ab}

be the reciprocity map of local class field theory normalized so that
{\mathrm{rec}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}(p)
is a lift of the geometric Frobenius
{\rm Fr}_{p}\in G_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
. Let
I_{p}\subset W_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
be the inertia subgroup.

Let
T
be a continuous
R
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank
r_{T}
, where

R
is a commutative topological
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra
satisfying either of the following:

i)

R
is a
\mathrm{Jac}(R)
-adically complete Noetherian semi-local ring such that

R/\mathrm{Jac}(R)
is a finite ring.

ii)

R
is a finite product of finite extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

In the second case we often denote
R
by
L
. Let
T^{*}(1):={\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(T,R)(1)
denote the Tate dual of
T
.

For an
R
-representation
T
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
,
denote the complex of continuous cochains of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
with values in
T
by
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cont}}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)
, i.e.

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cont}}^{i}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T):=\{c:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{i}\rightarrow T|\text{ continuous maps}\}

for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geqq 0}
with the usual boundary maps.
Applying the determinant functor of Knudsen–Mumford
[
53
]
to the perfect complex

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cont}}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)

of
R
-modules
leads to the graded invertible
R
-module

\Delta_{R,1}(T):=\mathrm{Det}_{R}(\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cont}}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)).

It is is of degree
-r_{T}

by the Euler–Poincaré formula.

For
a\in R^{\times}
(resp.
a\in\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}
if
R=L
), put

R_{a}:=\{x\in W(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}R\mid(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id}_{R})(x)=(1\otimes a)\cdot x\},

which is an invertible
R
-module. For
T
as above, we often regard
\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)
as a continuous homomorphism
\mathrm{det}_{R}(T):G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{ab}}\rightarrow R^{\times}
.
Define a constant

a(T):=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(p))\in R^{\times},

and
a graded invertible
R
-module

\Delta_{R,2}(T):=(\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)\otimes_{R}R_{a(T)},r_{T}).

For a graded invertible
R
-module
\mathcal{P}=(P,r)
, put
\mathcal{P}^{\vee}:=(P^{*},r)
. For a finite projective
R
-module
M
,
define a trivialization (Zariski locally) by

\mathrm{det}_{R}(M)\otimes_{R}\mathrm{det}_{R}(M^{*})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R:(e_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{n})\otimes(e_{n}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{1}^{*})\mapsto 1

for
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}\}
a (local) basis of
M
and
\{e_{1}^{*},\cdots,e_{n}^{*}\}
its dual basis, by which we identify
\mathrm{det}_{R}(M^{*})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(\mathrm{det}_{R}(M))^{*}
.

Definition 4.1
.

For an
R
-representation
T
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
,
the local fundamental line
\Delta_{R}(T)
is defined by

\Delta_{R}(T):=\Delta_{R,1}(T)\boxtimes\Delta_{R,2}(T).

The local fundamental line is compatible with the following functorial operations.

1)

For any continuous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism

R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, the canonical morphism induces an
R^{\prime}
-module isomorphism

\Delta_{R}(T)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R^{\prime}}(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}).

2)

For any exact sequence
0\rightarrow T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}\rightarrow T_{3}\rightarrow 0
of

R
-representations of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, the canonical morphism induces an
R
-module isomorphism

\Delta_{R}(T_{2})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T_{1})\boxtimes\Delta_{R}(T_{3}).

3)

There exists a canonical
R
-module isomorphism

\Delta_{R}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T^{*})^{\vee}\boxtimes(R(r_{T}),0)

defined as the product of the following two isomorphisms

\Delta_{R,1}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R,1}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee},

induced by the Tate duality
\mathrm{C}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{cont}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(\mathrm{C}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{cont}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},T^{*}(1)),R[-2]),
and the isomorphism

\Delta_{R,2}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R,2}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee}\boxtimes(R(r_{T}),0)

defined by

x\otimes y\mapsto[z\otimes w\mapsto y\otimes w\otimes z(x)]

for

x\in\mathrm{det}_{R}(T),y\in R_{a(T)},z\in\mathrm{det}_{R}(T^{*}(1))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(\mathrm{det}_{R}(T))^{*}(r_{T})
and

w\in R_{a(T^{*}(1))}
.
Here we note that
R_{a(T)}\otimes_{R}R_{a(T^{*}(1))}=R
since
a(T)\cdot a(T^{*}(1))=1
.

For all pairs
(R,T)
as above and
\zeta\in\varprojlim_{n}\mu_{p^{n}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
,
Kato’s local epsilon conjecture
[
48
]
posits the existence of a compatible family of canonical trivializations of local fundamental lines

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T),

referred to as Kato’s local epsilon isomorphisms.
A characterizing property is that
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T)
interpolates the de Rham epsilon isomorphisms

\varepsilon^{\mathrm{dR}}_{L,\zeta}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L}(V)

for all de Rham pairs
(R,T)=(L,V)
,
as defined by Kato and recalled in §
4.1.2
below.

We first recall epsilon constants defined for representations of the Weil–Deligne group
{}^{\prime}W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
Let
C
be a field of characteristic zero which contains all the
p
-power roots of unity.
For a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis
\zeta=\{\zeta_{p^{n}}\}_{n\geq 0}\in\Gamma(C,\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)):=\varprojlim_{n\geqq 0}\mu_{p^{n}}(C)
, define an additive character

\psi_{\zeta}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}\rightarrow C^{\times}\text{ by }\psi_{\zeta}(\frac{1}{p^{n}})=\zeta_{p^{n}}.

Let
\rho=(M,\rho)
be a smooth
C
-representation of the Weil group
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, namely
M
is a finite dimensional
C
-vector space with a
C
-linear smooth (i.e. locally constant) action
\rho
of
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
The theory of local constants associates to it the epsilon constant

\varepsilon(\rho,\psi,dx)\in C^{\times}

(cf.
[
Del
, Thm. 6.5]
and §
2.5
), which depends on the choices of an additive character
\psi:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow C^{\times}
and a
C
-valued
Haar measure
dx
on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.
In this article we consider this constant only for the pair
(\psi_{\zeta},dx)
such that
\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}dx=1
(note that
dx
is self-dual with respect to
\psi_{\zeta}
), and denote it by

\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta):=\varepsilon(\rho,\psi_{\zeta},dx)

for simplicity. We note that this constant satisfies

f(\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta))=\varepsilon(\rho\otimes_{C,f}C^{\prime},f(\zeta))\in C^{\prime}
for any field homomorphism
f:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
, and

\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta^{a})=\mathrm{det}(\rho)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(a))\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta)
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
by Lemma 4.3 and Theorem 6.5 (b) of
[
Del
]
.

Let
M=(M,\rho,N)
be a
C
-representation of the Weil-Deligne group
{}^{\prime}W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, i.e.
\rho:=(M,\rho)
is a smooth
C
-representation of
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
with a
C
-linear endomorphism
N:M\rightarrow M
such that

\widetilde{\mathrm{Fr}_{p}}\circ N=p^{-1}\cdot N\circ\widetilde{\mathrm{Fr}_{p}}

for any lift
\widetilde{\mathrm{Fr}_{p}}\in W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
of the geometric
Frobenius
\mathrm{Fr}_{p}\in G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Its epsilon constant is defined by

\varepsilon(M,\zeta):=\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta)\cdot\mathrm{det}_{C}(-\mathrm{Fr}_{p}|M^{I_{p}}/(M^{N=0})^{I_{p}}).

4.1.2.
de Rham epsilon isomorphism

For any de Rham

L
-representation
V
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
,
we recall Kato’s definition
[
48
]
of de Rham
\varepsilon
-isomorphism

\varepsilon^{\mathrm{dR}}_{L,\zeta}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L}(V)
.

Fix a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis
\zeta=\{\zeta_{p^{n}}\}_{n\geq 0}\in\Gamma(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1))
.
Let
V
be a de Rham
L
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
By
the
p
-adic local monodromy theorem and Fontaine’s functor
D_{\mathrm{pst}}(-)
,
we have an associated functorial Weil–Deligne
L\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{{\mathrm{ur}}}
-representation

W(V)=(W(V),\rho,N)

(cf. §
2.4
).

Let
I_{L}
denote the set of embedding of
L
into
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Definition 4.2
.

We define the epsilon constant
\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta)
of
W(V)
as

\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta):=(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta))_{\tau\in I_{L}}\in(L\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})^{\times},

where
\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta)\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
is the epsilon constant of
W(V)_{\tau}:=W(V)\otimes_{L\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}},\tau\otimes\mathrm{id}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

for all
\tau
.

The following lemma seems to be well-known, but we couldn’t find a published reference for it.
Put
L_{\infty}=L\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})\subset L\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

We have

\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta)\in(L_{\infty}^{\times})^{I_{p}=\mathrm{det}W(V)}.

In particular, if
\mathrm{det}(W(V))
is unramified, then
\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta)\in L^{\times}
.

Proof.

We first note that the image of the character
\mathrm{det}(W(V))
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\mathrm{ab}}
is a subgroup of
L^{\times}\subset(L\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}})^{\times}
, and so the notation
(L_{\infty}^{\times})^{I_{p}=\mathrm{det}W(V)}
in the lemma is well defined.
Indeed one has
\mathrm{det}(W(V))=W(\mathrm{det}(V))
and any de Rham character
\chi:G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow L^{\times}
(e.g.
\mathrm{det}(V)
) is of the form

\chi=\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{k}\mu_{\lambda}\eta
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mu_{\lambda}
the unramified character such that
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\lambda\in L^{\times}
,
\eta:\Gamma\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow L^{\times}
a finite order character. So
W(\chi)=\mu_{\lambda}\eta\cdot|-|_{p}^{k}
, and the image is clearly a subgroup of
L^{\times}
.

As for the lemma, it suffices to show

\sigma(\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta))=\mathrm{det}(W(V))(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma))\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta)

for any
\sigma\in W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.
Note that

\sigma((\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta))_{\tau\in I_{L}})=(\sigma(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\sigma^{-1}\circ\tau},\zeta)))_{\tau\in I_{L}}\\
=(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\sigma\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\tau},\sigma(\zeta)))_{\tau\in I_{L}}=(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma)}))_{\tau\in I_{L}},

where the second equality follows from the base change property
f(\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta))=\varepsilon(\rho\otimes_{C,f}C^{\prime},f(\zeta))\in C^{\prime}
for any field homomorphism
f:C\rightarrow C^{\prime}
.
By the formula
\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta^{a})=\mathrm{det}(\rho)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(a))\varepsilon(\rho,\zeta)
for
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
,
we obtain

\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma)})=\mathrm{det}(W(V)_{\tau})(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma))\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta)=\tau(\mathrm{det}(W(V))(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma)))\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta).

Therefore, it follows that

(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma)}))_{\tau\in I_{L}}=\mathrm{det}(W(V))(\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\sigma))(\varepsilon(W(V)_{\tau},\zeta))_{\tau\in I_{L}}.

∎

Put

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V):=(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V)^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}

on which the Frobenius
\varphi
naturally acts,
and

D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V):=(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V)^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}},\ \ \mathrm{Fil}^{i}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V):=(t^{i}\mathbf{B}^{+}_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V)^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\ \text{ and }\ t_{V}:=D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V).

Based on the above preliminaries, first define an isomorphism

\theta_{L}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L,1}(V)\boxtimes\mathrm{Det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))

as the one induced by the following exact sequence of
L
-vector spaces

0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\xrightarrow{(a)}D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\oplus t_{V}\xrightarrow{(b)}\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\\
\xrightarrow{(c)}D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V^{*})^{*}\oplus\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\xrightarrow{(d)}D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V^{*})^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow 0.

(4.1)

Here the map (a) is the sum of
1-\varphi:D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\rightarrow D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
and the canonical map
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\rightarrow t_{V}
, and the maps
(b) and (c) are defined by using the Bloch–Kato’s exponential map and its dual,
and the map (d) is the dual of (a) for
V^{*}(1)
(see
[
48
,
35
,
62
]
for the precise definition).

We set
t_{\zeta}:=\mathrm{log}[\zeta(\bmod p)]\in\mathbf{B}^{+}_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
h_{M}:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}r\cdot\mathrm{dim}_{L}\mathrm{gr}^{-r}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)
. By Lemma
4.3
, the map

L_{a_{p}(T)}\otimes_{L}\mathrm{det}_{L}(V)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{det}(V):x\mapsto\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{L}(W(V),\zeta)}\cdot\frac{1}{t_{\zeta}^{h_{V}}}\cdot x,

induces an isomorphism

L_{a_{p}(T)}\otimes_{L}\mathrm{det}_{L}(V)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)).

We denote its inverse by

\theta_{\mathrm{dR},L}(V,\zeta):\mathrm{Det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L,2}(V).

Definition 4.4
.

The de Rham
\varepsilon
-isomorphism

\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L}(V)

is defined as the following composite

\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\xrightarrow{\Gamma(V)\cdot\theta_{L}(V)}\Delta_{L,1}(V)\boxtimes\mathrm{Det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\theta_{\mathrm{dR},L}(V,\zeta)}\Delta_{L,1}(V)\boxtimes\Delta_{L,2}(V)=\Delta_{L}(V).

4.1.3.
The conjecture

In the early 90’s Kato proposed the following fundamental (cf.
[
48
, Conj. 1.8]
,
[
35
, Conj. 3.4.3]
, and
[
62
, Conj. 3.8]
).

Conjecture 4.5

(Kato’s local epsilon conjecture)
.

For all triples
(R,T,\zeta)
as in §
4.1.1
such that
T
is an
R
-representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
\zeta
a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-basis of
\Gamma(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1))
,
there exists a unique compatible family of isomorphisms

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T),

satisfying the following.

1)

For any continuous
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
, we have

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T)\otimes\mathrm{id}_{R^{\prime}}=\varepsilon_{R^{\prime},\zeta}(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})

under the canonical isomorphism

\Delta_{R}(T)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R^{\prime}}(T\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}).

2)

For any exact sequence
0\rightarrow T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}\rightarrow T_{3}\rightarrow 0
of
R
-representations of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, we have

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T_{2})=\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T_{1})\boxtimes\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T_{3})

under the canonical isomorphism

\Delta_{R}(T_{2})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T_{1})\boxtimes\Delta_{R}(T_{3}).

3)

For any
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta^{a}}(T)=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(a))\cdot\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T).

4)

We have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Delta_{R}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{R}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee}\boxtimes(R(r_{T}),0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee}\boxtimes[\mathbf{e}_{r_{T}}\mapsto 1]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(-1))\cdot\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{R}\boxtimes{\bf 1}_{R}}

5)

For any triple
(L,V,\zeta)
such that
V
is de Rham,
we have

\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}(V)=\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V).

Remark 4.6
.

The above property 4) corrects a typo
in the property (4) of
[
NaKato
, Conj. 2.1]
:
on the left vertical map therein, the author wrote

\varepsilon_{R,-\zeta}(T)(:=\varepsilon_{R,\zeta^{-1}}(T))
instead of
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T)
.
We also note that the commutative diagram in property 4) is the same as the following

\textstyle{\Delta_{R}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{R}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee}\boxtimes(R(r_{T}),0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T^{*}(1))^{\vee}\boxtimes[\mathbf{e}_{r_{T}}\mapsto 1]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{1}_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{R,\zeta^{-1}}(T)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{R}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}_{R}}

in view of the property 3).

4.1.4.
Results

The following is a main result of
[
48
,
NaKato
,
45
]
.

Theorem 4.7
.

i)

For any odd prime
p
, Kato’s local epsilon Conjecture
4.5
is true for all pairs
(R,T)
such that
\mathrm{rank}_{R}(T)\leq 2
. Namely,
there exists a unique compatible family of isomorphisms

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)

satisfying the properties 1), 3), 4), 5) of Conjecture
4.5
and also the property 2) in the rank two case
with
T_{1}
and
T_{3}
of rank one.

ii)

If
p=2
, then a similar result holds after inverting
p
, i.e. we have compatible families of isomorphisms

\varepsilon_{R[1/p],\zeta}(T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}):\mathbf{1}_{R[1/p]}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}

satisfying
the properties 1), 3), 4), 5) of Conjecture
4.5
.

Remark 4.8
.

We describe some of the prior results towards Conjecture
4.5
as in Theorem
4.7
.

i)

The rank one case was originally proved by Kato
[
48
]
(see also
[
88
,
62
]
).

ii)

The rank two case was proved by the third-named author
[
NaKato
]
for almost all pairs
(R,T)
, and completed by Jacinto
[
45
]
building on
[
NaKato
]
.
More precisely, the isomorphism
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)
was defined in
[
NaKato
]
for all
pairs
(R,T)
such that
\mathrm{rank}_{R}(T)=2
under the assumption: the universal (framed or versal) deformation
ring corresponding to
\overline{T}=T\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{m}_{R}
is reduced and
p
-torsion free for
R
local, and contains a Zariski dense subset consisting of crystalline points in every irreducible component.
These properties are established for all residual representations
\overline{T}
by the recent work of Böckle–Iyenger–Paškūnas
[
BIP
]
, hence we obtain the isomorphism
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)
for all pairs
(R,T)
such that
\mathrm{rank}_{R}(T)=2
.

iii)

In
[
NaKato
]
, property 5) was proved when the
Hodge–Tate weights of
V
are
(k_{1},k_{2})
satisfying
k_{1}\leq 0
and
k_{2}\geq 1
. Subsequently, the general case
was
established in
[
45
]
. For our application to the local sign decomposition,
the former suffices.

4.2.
Definition of
w_{T}
via Kato’s local epsilon isomorphism

In this subsection we define an involution
w_{T}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
for
generic symplectic self-dual pairs
(R,T)
of rank two using
Kato’s epsilon
isomorphism
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)
as in Theorem
4.7
.
In the anomalous case (including the prime
p=2
), we similarly define an involution
w_{T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p})
via the epsilon
isomorphism
\varepsilon_{R[1/p],\zeta}(T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}):\mathbf{1}_{R[1/p]}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}.

4.2.1.
General rank two case

Let
(R,T)
be a generic rank two representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, not necessarily symplectic self-dual.

Recall that
H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0
for
i\in\{0,2\}
and

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
is a free
R
-module of rank two. Thus, we have

\Delta_{R,1}(T)=(\wedge^{2}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T))^{-1}
as an underlying invertible
R
-module, and to give an

R
-module
isomorphism

\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)=\Delta_{R,1}(T)\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)

is equivalent to give an
R
-module
isomorphism

\wedge^{2}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R,2}(T).

Moreover, the latter is equivalent to giving an
R
-bilinear skew-symmetric perfect pairing

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow\Delta_{R,2}(T).

For a fixed
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-base
\zeta\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
,
we denote by

[\ ,\ ]_{T,\zeta}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow\Delta_{R,2}(T)

the pairing corresponding to the local epsilon isomorphism

\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)

as in Theorem
4.7
.
We also define

\{\ ,\}_{T}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow\Delta_{R,2}(T):(x\otimes y,z)\mapsto\{x,z\}_{T}\cdot y.

Lemma 4.9
.

There exists an
R
-module morphism

w_{T,\zeta}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)

uniquely characterized by the equality

\{w_{T,\zeta}(x),y\}_{T}=[x,y]_{T,\zeta}\in\Delta_{R,2}(T)

for any
x,y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
.
Moreover, for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have

w_{T,\zeta^{a}}=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\sigma_{a})w_{T,\zeta}

Proof.

The existence is a simple consequence of part 6) of Proposition
2.12
.
The rest follows from property 3) of Conjecture
4.5

since
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta^{a}}(T)=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\sigma_{a})\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T)
.
∎

Applying this construction to
T^{*}(1)
for
\zeta^{-1}
instead of
\zeta
, we obtain an
R
-module morphism

w_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T^{*}(1)).

The following compatibility for
w_{T,\zeta}
and
w_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}
involves a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{can}:\Delta_{R,2}(T^{*}(1))\otimes\Delta_{R,2}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R(2)

defined by the tensor products of the canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{det}_{R}(T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(\mathrm{det}_{R}(T))^{*}(2)\otimes_{R}\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R(2)
and
R_{a(T^{*}(1))}\otimes_{R}R_{a(T)}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R:y\otimes z\mapsto yz
.

Lemma 4.10
.

The composite

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\xrightarrow{w_{T,\zeta}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)\\
\xrightarrow{-w_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\Delta_{R,2}(T)}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)\\
\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{can}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)(2)\xrightarrow{(-)\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-2}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

is the identity.

Proof.

This lemma follows from the compatibility of local epsilon isomorphism with Tate duality.

Fix a basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
and
\{e\}
of
\Delta_{R,2}(T)
such that

[e_{1},e_{2}]_{T,\zeta}=e.

Let
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
be the dual basis of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))
with respect to
the Tate pairing
\{\ ,\ \}_{T}
, i.e.

\{e_{i}^{*},e_{j}\}_{T}=\begin{cases}0&(i\neq j)\\
1&(i=j)\end{cases},

and
e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2}\in\Delta_{R,2}(T^{*}(1))
be the basis such that

\mathrm{can}(e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2}\otimes e)=\zeta^{\otimes 2}.

Then we have

w_{T,\zeta}(e_{1})=e_{2}^{*}\otimes e,\ \ w_{T,\zeta}(e_{2})=-e_{1}^{*}\otimes e

since

\{e_{2}^{*}\otimes e,e_{1}\}_{T}=0=[e_{1},e_{1}]_{T,\zeta},\ \ \{e_{2}^{*}\otimes e,e_{2}\}_{T}=e=[e_{1},e_{2}]_{T,\zeta}

and

\{-e_{1}^{*}\otimes e,e_{1}\}_{T}=-e=[e_{2},e_{1}]_{T,\zeta},\ \ \{-e_{1}^{*}\otimes e,e_{2}\}_{T}=0=[e_{2},e_{2}]_{T,\zeta}.

By the property 4) of Conjecture
4.5
and Theorem
4.7
,
we have

[e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}]_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}=-e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2}.

Similarly, we obtain

w_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}(e^{*}_{1})=e_{2}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2},\ \ w_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}(e^{*}_{2})=-e_{1}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2}

since

\{e_{2}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2},e_{1}^{*}\}_{T^{*}(1)}=0=[e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{1}]_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}},\ \ \{e_{2}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2},e^{*}_{2}\}_{T^{*}(1)}=-e^{*}\zeta^{2}=[e^{*}_{1},e^{*}_{2}]_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}

and

\{-e_{1}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2},e^{*}_{1}\}_{T^{*}(1)}=e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2}=[e^{*}_{2},e^{*}_{1}]_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}},\ \ \{-e_{1}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2},e^{*}_{2}\}_{T^{*}(1)}=0=[e^{*}_{2},e^{*}_{2}]_{T^{*}(1),\zeta^{-1}}.

Hence, the composite in the Lemma sends

e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}^{*}\otimes e\mapsto(e_{1}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2})\otimes e\mapsto e_{1}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes 2}\mapsto e_{1}

and

e_{2}\mapsto-e_{1}^{*}\otimes e\mapsto-(-e_{2}\otimes e^{*}\zeta^{\otimes 2})\otimes e\mapsto e_{2}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes 2}\mapsto e_{2},

concluding the proof.
∎

4.2.2.
Rank two self-dual case

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two.

We have
\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R(1)

and in turn,

\Delta_{R,2}(T)=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)

since
a(T)=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(p))=\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\mathrm{rec}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(p))=1
.
Hence,

\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(T,\mathrm{det}_{R}(T))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}T^{*}(1)\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(R)(-1),

where
\Delta_{R,2}(R)(-1)
is regarded as an
R
-module.
By its composition with the following isomorphism

T\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(T,\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)):x\mapsto[y\mapsto x\wedge y],

we obtain an isomorphism

T\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}T^{*}(1)\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(R)(-1),

(4.2)

by which we identify both sides.

Define an
R
-module morphism

w_{T}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

as the composite

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\xrightarrow{w_{T,\zeta}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-1}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

where the last isomorphism is induced by (
4.2
).

Lemma 4.11
.

The morphism
w_{T}
is an involution and it is independent of the choice of
\zeta
.

Proof.

The second assertion
holds since for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, we have

w_{T,\zeta^{a}}\otimes(\zeta^{a})^{\otimes-1}=\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)(\sigma_{a})w_{T,\zeta}\otimes a^{-1}\zeta^{\otimes-1}=w_{T,\zeta}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-1}.

As for the first, it is a consequence of Lemma
4.10
since the isomorphism (
4.2
) is symplectic.
∎

Definition 4.12
.

If
p
is an odd prime and
T
is generic, then the signed submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
are defined by

H^{1}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)^{w_{T}=\pm 1}.

If either
p=2
or
T
is anomalous, then we similarly define
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

Basic properties of the signed submodules are given by
Proposition
4.14
below, which relies on the following preliminary.

Lemma 4.13
.

Define a pairing

\{-,-\}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1)

as the composite

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{*}(1))\otimes_{R}\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\\
\xrightarrow{(x\otimes z,y)\mapsto\{x,y\}_{T}\otimes z}\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1),

where the first morphism is induced by (
4.2
).
This pairing is symmetric and satisfies

\{w_{T}(x),y\}=[x,y]_{T,\zeta}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-1}

(4.3)

for any
x,y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
.

Proof.

The last equality immediately follows from the definition of

w_{T,\zeta}
and
w_{T}
.

It remains to show that the pairing
\{-,-\}
is symmetric.
By definition, it equals the composite

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}T)\xrightarrow{H^{2}([x\otimes y\mapsto x\wedge y])}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathrm{det}_{R}(T))\\
\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},R(1))\otimes\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1).

Here the second morphism is induced by the morphism
x\otimes y\mapsto x\wedge y
for
x,y\in T
.
The preceding composite is symmetric since so is the composite

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{R}T)\xrightarrow{H^{2}([x\otimes y\mapsto x\wedge y])}H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathrm{det}_{R}(T)).

∎

Proposition 4.14
.

i)

Let
p
be an odd prime and
(R,T)
a generic symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two.
Then we have a decomposition

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\oplus H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

into free
R
-submodules of rank one.
Moreover, the submodules
H^{1}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
are Lagrangian with respect to the pairing

\{-,-\}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow\Delta_{R,2}(T)(-1)

as in Lemma
4.13
.

ii)

Let
p
be a prime and
(R,T)
a symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two such that

H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}=0.

Then
we have a decomposition

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}=H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\oplus H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})

into Lagrangian locally free
R[1/p]
-submodules of rank one.

Proof.

In the following we consider part i), and an analogous argument applies for part ii).

Note that
w_{T}
is an involution by Lemma
4.11
and
2\in R^{\times}
,
so the existence of the decomposition follows.
Since
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
is a free
R
-module of rank two,
to show that
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
are of rank one, it suffices to show that

w_{T}
is not a scalar morphism, i.e.
w_{T}\not=\pm\mathrm{id}
.

We assume that
w_{T}=\pm\mathrm{id}
and deduce a contradiction below. It considers the case
w_{T}=\mathrm{id}
and
an analogous argument applies if
w_{T}=-\mathrm{id}
.

We have

\{x,y\}=\{w_{T}(x),y\}=[x,y]_{T,\zeta}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-1}

for arbitrary
x,y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
. Then it follows that

[x,y]_{T,\zeta}=0
since the pairing
[\,,\,]_{T,\zeta}
is skew-symmetric and
\{\,,\,\}
symmetric.
This contradicts the fact that the pairing
[\,,\,]_{T,\zeta}
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{det}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R,2}(T)
.

Finally, we show the Lagrangian property.
For any
x_{\pm}\in H^{1}_{\pm}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
,
we have

\{x_{\pm},x_{\pm}\}=\pm\{w_{T}(x_{\pm}),x_{\pm}\}=\pm[x_{\pm},x_{\pm}]_{T,\zeta}\otimes\zeta^{\otimes-1}=0

since
[\,,\,]_{T,\zeta}
is skew-symmetric.
On the other hand, if
x_{\pm}
is a basis, then
\{x_{+},x_{-}\}\in R^{\times}
since the pairing is non-degenerate.
Hence, the proof concludes.
∎

4.3.
Local sign decomposition for de Rham representations

This subsection gives a description of the local sign decomposition as in §
4.2
for de Rham representations.

Let
V
be a two dimensional de Rham
L
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
Suppose that it is symplectic self-dual with Hodge Tate weights
k_{1}\leq k_{2}
and generic, i.e.
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=0
. Then we have

(k_{1},k_{2})=(1-k,k)

for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Moreover,

\mathrm{dim}_{L}(H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))=\mathrm{dim}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))=1

and
\Gamma(V)=(-1)^{k-1}

by Corollary
2.9
.
Recall that
\varepsilon_{L}(W(V))\in\{\pm 1\}
and it is independent of the choice of
\zeta
.

Since we assume that
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=0
and so
H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\simeq H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)^{*}=0
,
the de Rham epsilon isomorphism
\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L}(V)

is induced by an isomorphism

\wedge^{2}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L,2}(V)

whose explicit definition is given in the proof of Proposition
4.17
below. To give such an isomorphism is equivalent to giving an

L
-linear skew-symmetric pairing

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\times H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow\Delta_{L,2}(V).

Proceeding as in §
4.2.2
, the de Rham epsilon isomorphism leads to such an involution,
which we denote by

w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).

Here we note that
w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}
satisfies the same property as in Lemma
4.10
since

\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V)
is also compatible with Tate duality by Lemma 3.7 (2) of

[
62
]
.

Definition 4.15
.

The signed submodules
H^{1}_{\pm,{\rm dR}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
are defined by

H^{1}_{\pm,\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V):=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)^{w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}=\pm 1}

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
V
be a two-dimensional de Rham
L
-representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
which is symplectic self-dual and generic.
Then we have a decomposition

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{+,{\rm dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\oplus H^{1}_{-,{\rm dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

into
L
-vector subspaces of dimension one.

Proof.

This follows just as in the proof of Proposition
4.14
.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

For
\epsilon:=-\Gamma(V)\varepsilon(W(V))
,
we have

H^{1}_{\epsilon,\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).

Proof.

It suffices to show that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\subset H^{1}_{\epsilon,\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
since both are one dimensional.

The de Rham epsilon isomorphism
\varepsilon_{L,\zeta}^{\mathrm{dR}}(V):\mathbf{1}_{L}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L}(V)
is induced by the
following composite of isomorphisms

\mathrm{det}_{L}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\otimes_{L}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)/H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))\\
\xrightarrow{\Gamma(V)\cdot\mathrm{exp}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{exp}^{*}}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\otimes_{L}\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\xrightarrow{\overline{x}\otimes y\mapsto-x\wedge y(=y\wedge x)}\mathrm{det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\\
\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathrm{det}(V))=\frac{1}{t}\Delta_{L,2}(V)\xrightarrow{y\mapsto\varepsilon_{L}(W(V))ty}\Delta_{L,2}(V)

Here we note that
\overline{x}\otimes y
is sent to
y\wedge x=-x\wedge y
since the natural isomorphism is given by

\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\otimes_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)):y\otimes\overline{x}\mapsto y\wedge x.

By the canonical isomorphism
V\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}V^{*}(1)\otimes_{L}\Delta_{L,2}(V)(-1)
,
we identify the above composite

(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\otimes_{L}\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{det}_{L}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))\\
\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathrm{det}_{L}(V))=\frac{1}{t}\Delta_{L,2}(V)

with

(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V^{*}(1))/\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V^{*}(1))\otimes_{L}\Delta_{L,2}(V)(-1))\otimes_{L}\mathrm{Fil}^{0}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\\
\xrightarrow{(f\otimes z)\otimes y\mapsto-f(y)\otimes z}D_{\mathrm{dR}}(L(1))\otimes_{L}\Delta_{L,2}(V)(-1)=\frac{1}{t}L(1)\otimes_{L}\Delta_{L,2}(V)(-1)=\frac{1}{t}\Delta_{L,2}(V).

Since

\{\mathrm{exp}_{V}(a),b\}_{V}=[a,\mathrm{exp}^{*}(b)]_{\mathrm{dR}}

for any
a\in D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V^{*}(1))
,
b\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
,
it follows that the above composite

\mathrm{det}_{L}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L,2}(V)

maps
x\wedge y
, for
x\in H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V),y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
, to

-\Gamma(V)\varepsilon_{L}(W(V))\{x,y\}\otimes\zeta.

On the other hand, by the definition of
w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}
, this isomorphism is also defined by

\mathrm{det}(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{L,2}(V):x\wedge y\mapsto\{w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}(x),y\}\otimes\zeta

for any
x,y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
.
Therefore, we obtain

\{w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}(x),y\}=-\Gamma(V)\varepsilon_{L}(W(V))\{x,y\}

for
x\in H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
and
any
y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
. We conclude that

w_{V}^{\mathrm{dR}}(x)=-\Gamma(V)\varepsilon_{L}(W(V))x

for
x\in H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
,
which implies that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\subset H^{1}_{\epsilon,\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
.

∎

4.4.
Construction of
w_{T}
via Colmez’s operator
w

In this subsection we give an explicit construction of the involution
w_{T}
using Colmez’s operator
w_{*}
on the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module
D=D(T)
associated to
a rank two generic symplectic self-dual pair
(R,T)
.

The involution corresponds to the action of
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
on
the associated representation
\Pi_{p}(D)
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
by the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence.
This construction of
w_{T}
is essentially the same as in §
4.2
since Kato’s epsilon isomorphism
\varepsilon_{R,\zeta}(T):\mathbf{1}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Delta_{R}(T)
in Theorem

4.7
is itself defined
[
NaKato
,
45
]
using Colmez’s operator
w_{*}
.

Throughout this subsection, we fix an odd prime
p
.

4.4.1.
Backdrop

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
R
is Artinian local. Indeed, the results in this subsection follow
for a general
R
from the Artinian local case by taking the limit.

Set
\Gamma:={\mathrm{Gal}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(\mu_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, and let
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}:\Gamma\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
be
the
p
-adic cyclotomic character.
For
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
, define

\sigma_{b}\in\Gamma
such that
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}(\sigma_{b})=b
.

Define a topological ring
\mathcal{E}_{R}=R(\!(X)\!)
such that
R[\![X]\!]
is open in
\mathcal{E}_{R}
and the induced topology on
R[\![X]\!]
coincides with the
X
-adic topology. The ring
\mathcal{E}_{R}
is equipped with actions of the Frobenius

\varphi
and
\Gamma:=\mathrm{Gal}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, which act as
R
-algebra continuous homomorphisms characterized by

\varphi(X)=(1+X)^{p}-1,\ \ \gamma(X)=(1+X)^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}(\gamma)}-1\ \ (\gamma\in\Gamma).

Let
D=D(T)
be the étale
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module
associated to
T
by the covariant Fontaine functor
[
33
]
, namely
D
is a finite free
\mathcal{E}_{R}
-module equipped with a Frobenius structure

\varphi:\varphi^{*}(D)=D\otimes_{\mathcal{E}_{R},\varphi}\mathcal{E}_{R}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}D

and a commuting continuous semilinear
\Gamma
-action.
Let
\psi
be the left inverse of
\varphi
on
D
defined by

\psi:D\rightarrow D:x=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}(1+X)^{i}\varphi(x_{i})\mapsto x_{0}.

We have the following key link between the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module
D
and the Iwasawa cohomology
H^{1}_{\rm Iw}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T):=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n}}),T)
.

Proposition 4.18
.

There exists a canonical
R[\![\Gamma]\!]
-module isomorphism

D^{\psi=1}\cong H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}).

In particular, we have a canonical
R
-module isomorphism

D^{\psi=1}/(\gamma-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}),

where
\gamma
is
a topological generator of
\Gamma
.

This proposition is the content of
[
22
]
and ‘in particular’ part follows from part 5) of Proposition
2.12
.

Colmez defined

[
25
, II.1]

the
\Gamma
-equivariant
R
-linear operator

w_{*}:D^{\psi=0}\rightarrow D^{\psi=0,\iota}
by

w_{*}(x):=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\!\!\ \mathrm{mod}\ p^{n}}(1+X)^{1/i}\sigma_{-1/i^{2}}(\varphi^{n}\psi^{n}((1+X)^{-i}x)).

(4.4)

Here

D^{\psi=0,\iota}
is
D^{\psi=0}
but the
\Gamma
-action is
twisted by the involution
\iota:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma,\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{-1}
.
The operator
w_{*}
is related to the action of
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
on the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-representation
associated to
T
under the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence, and a key to
Colmez’s construction
[
25
]
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
-representations from
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules.

The operator
w_{*}
also underlies the third-named author’s proof
[
NaKato
,
45
]
of Kato’s local epsilon conjecture for rank two representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
In fact, for de Rham representations, he related the action of
w_{*}
to the corresponding epsilon constants.

To relate
D^{\psi=1}
and
D^{\psi=0}
, consider the map

1-\varphi:D^{\psi=1}\rightarrow D^{\psi=0}.

Its kernel is
D^{\varphi=1}

and the image is denoted by

\mathscr{C}(D):=(1-\varphi)D^{\psi=1}.

Our strategy to construct an involution on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
: Colmez’s operator
w_{*}
on
D^{\psi=0}
indues an involution on
D^{\psi=1}/(\gamma-1)
(see Proposition
4.18
).

In fact, if
T
is an irreducible
L
-representation where
L
is a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, then

D^{\varphi=1}=0
and
Colmez
[
25
, Prop. V.2.1]

showed
16
16

16

We have
\delta_{D}(p)=1
in the self-dual case.
that
w_{*}(\mathscr{C}(D))\subset\mathscr{C}(D)
.
We generalize Colmez’s proof of Proposition V.2.1 in
[
25
]
to families of Galois representations and reducible cases (see §
4.4.3
).

4.4.2.
Preliminaries

Following Colmez, put

D\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}:=\{x=(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in D^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\psi(x^{(n+1)})=x^{(n)}(n\geq 0)\},

D\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}(=D\boxtimes_{\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{P}^{1}):=\{(z_{1},z_{2})\in D^{2}\mid w_{*}((1-\varphi\psi)(z_{1}))=(1-\varphi\psi)(z_{2})\}.

We also consider

P(\mathbb{Q}_{p}):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbb{Q}_{p}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\subset B(\mathbb{Q}_{p}):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}&\mathbb{Q}_{p}\\
0&\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\subset\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Recall that
D\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
D\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}
are equipped with an action of
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

respectively with the trivial central character since
T
is assumed to be symplectic self-dual
[
25
, II.1]
. For example, we have

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(\sigma_{a}(x^{(n)}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ (a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}),

\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(x^{(n+1)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},

\begin{pmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(x^{(n-1)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},

where
x^{(-1)}:=\psi(x^{(0)})
, and

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\cdot(z_{1},z_{2})=(z_{2},z_{1}),

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot(z_{1},z_{2})=(\sigma_{a}(z_{1}),\sigma_{a}^{-1}(z_{2}))\ (a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}),

\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot(z_{1},z_{2})=(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})

with
\psi(z_{1}^{\prime})=z_{1},z_{2}^{\prime}=\psi(z_{2})
.

The following is the content of
[
25
, Prop. II.1.14]

Proposition 4.19
.

There exists a canonical
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant map

\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}:D\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\rightarrow D\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}:z\mapsto\left(\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}p^{n}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot z\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

such that

\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})=\{(0,z)\mid z\in D^{\mathrm{nr}}\}

for
D^{\mathrm{nr}}:=\cap_{n=1}^{\infty}\varphi^{n}(D)
.

We equip
D^{\mathrm{nr}}
with the structure of a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
-module by
17
17

17

Note that
\varphi:D^{\mathrm{nr}}\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{nr}}
is an isomorphism.

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot z=\sigma_{a}^{-1}(z)\ (a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}),\ \ \begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot z=\psi(z)(=\varphi^{-1}(z)),\ \ \begin{pmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot z=\varphi(z).

Then the map

D^{\mathrm{nr}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}):z\mapsto(0,z)

is
a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant isomorphism.

Let

D^{\sharp}
be the largest compact
\psi
-stable sub
R[\![X]\!]
-module of
D
on which
\psi
is surjective,

D^{\natural}
be the smallest compact sub
R[\![X]\!]
-module of
D
which is stable by

\psi
and generates
D
as an
R(\!(X)\!)
-module (see I.3.2 of
[
25
]
).

Definition 4.20
.

For
\circ\in\{\sharp,\natural\}
, define

D^{\circ}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}:=\{(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in D\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\mid x^{(n)}\in D^{\circ}\ (n\in\mathbb{N})\},

D^{\circ}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}:=\{z\in D\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\mid\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(z)\in D^{\circ}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\}.

Note that the above
R
-submodules are
B(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-stable. In fact, we have the following.

Theorem 4.21

(Colmez)
.

For
\circ\in\{\sharp,\natural\}
, the
R
-submodules

D^{\circ}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\subset D\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}
are
stable by
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the
stability holds for a universal (framed) deformation
{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
over a universal (framed) deformation ring
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
by the proof of
[
25
, Prop. II.2.15]
.

For the universal case, it is proved in
[
25
, Thm. II.3.3]
under the
assumption that the corresponding
universal (framed) deformation
ring is reduced and
p
-torsion free, and contains
a Zariski dense subset consisting of crystalline points in every irreducible component of its generic fiber.
These properties are established
for arbitrary universal (framed) deformation
rings by the recent work
[
BIP
]
of Böckle–Iyenger–Paškūnas.
∎

For
\alpha_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, we have a

\Gamma\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
-equivariant isomorphism

D^{\psi=1}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\left(D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}:x\mapsto(x)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

(4.5)

since
(D^{\sharp})^{\psi=1}=D^{\psi=1}
by Proposition II. 4.2 of
[
24
]
, via which we identify both sides.

As composite of
(\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})^{\alpha_{p}=1}:\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}

and the canonical inclusion

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\hookrightarrow\left(D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}=D^{\psi=1}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

we obtain a
\Gamma\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
-equivariant morphism

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T).

Taking its quotients by
\sigma_{a}-1
for a topological generator
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
yields an
R
-module morphism

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)/(\gamma-1)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

by part 5) of Proposition
2.12
. A key result below is that it is an isomorphism (see Theorem
4.23
).

We begin with a preliminary.

Lemma 4.22
.

For a topological generator
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
,
we have

(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1,\sigma_{a}=1}=((D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1})/(\sigma_{a}-1)=0,

(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))/(\sigma_{a}-1)=(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))^{\sigma_{a}=1}=0.

Proof.

Define a complex

C^{*}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}}):[D^{\mathrm{nr}}\xrightarrow{(1-\varphi)\oplus(1-\sigma_{a})}D^{\mathrm{nr}}\oplus D^{\mathrm{nr}}\xrightarrow{(1-\sigma_{a},\varphi-1)}D^{\mathrm{nr}}]

of degree
[0,2]
. Denoting its cohomology by
H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})
,
we have

H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1,\sigma_{a}=1},\ \ H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=(D^{\mathrm{nr}})/(1-\varphi,1-\sigma_{a}).

Moreover, we have a canonical short exact sequence

0\rightarrow(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})\rightarrow(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))^{\sigma_{a}=1}\rightarrow 0.

Hence it suffices to show that
H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
for all
i
. Since
D^{\mathrm{nr}}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module of finite length
18
18

18

Recall that
R
is Artinian local by assumption.

by Proposition I.3.3 of
[
25
]
, we have

\sum_{i=0}^{2}(-1)^{i}\mathrm{length}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}}))=0.

Therefore, it suffices to show that

H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
for
i\in\{0,2\}
.

For
i=0
, we have

H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1,\sigma_{a}=1}=D^{\varphi=1,\sigma_{a}=1}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0.

Hence, it remains to show that
H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
.

When
\overline{D}=D(\overline{T})
is absolutely irreducible,
we have
D^{\mathrm{nr}}=0
since
\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}}=0
. Since the functor
D\mapsto D^{\mathrm{nr}}
is left exact by Proposition I.3.3 of
[
25
]
, it follows that
H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
.

When
\overline{D}
is not absolutely irreducible,
it sits in the
following exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta_{1})\rightarrow\overline{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta_{2})\rightarrow 0

for some characters

\delta_{1},\delta_{2}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
such that
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}=\overline{\chi}_{p}
, the latter being mod
p
cyclotomic character. Here, for any character
\delta:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
,

\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta)=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\delta}
is a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}

defined by

\varphi(e_{\delta})=\delta(p)e_{\delta},\gamma(e_{\delta})=\delta(\overline{\chi}_{p}(\gamma))e_{\delta}.

Since
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta)^{\mathrm{nr}}=\mathbb{F}(\delta)
for any character
\delta:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
,
we obtain an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{F}(\delta_{1})\rightarrow\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}(\delta_{2})

(4.6)

of
\mathbb{F}[\varphi,\Gamma]
-modules. Hence, the set of Jordan–Hölder factors of
D^{\mathrm{nr}}
is a subset of

\{\mathbb{F}(\delta_{1}),\mathbb{F}(\delta_{2})\}
.

If
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\not=\mathbf{1}
, then

H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
since
H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F}(\delta))=0
for any
\delta\not=\mathbf{1}
.
If
\delta_{1}
or
\delta_{2}
equals
\mathbf{1}
, then the other character is
\overline{\chi}_{p}
.
As
T
is generic, we may assume that
\delta_{1}=\overline{\chi}_{p}
.
Then the exact sequence (
4.6
) becomes

0\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}(1)\rightarrow\overline{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{F}}\rightarrow 0,

which is non-split since
H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\overline{D})=H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})=0
by the generic assumption.
We claim that the natural inclusion
\mathbb{F}(1)\rightarrow\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}}
is bijective. If not, then we have
the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathbb{F}(1)\rightarrow\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\rightarrow 0.

Taking the associated long exact sequence for
H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(-)
,
we obtain the following exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F}(1))\rightarrow H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}})\rightarrow H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F}(1))\rightarrow\cdots.

Since
H^{i}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F}(1))=0
for all
i
, it follows that

H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F})=\mathbb{F},

which contradicts our assumption that
H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}})=H^{0}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\overline{D})=0
.
Therefore, we have
\mathbb{F}(1)=\overline{D}^{\mathrm{nr}}
. Hence we conclude that
H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(D^{\mathrm{nr}})=0
since

H^{2}_{\varphi,\sigma_{a}}(\mathbb{F}(1))=0
.
∎

Theorem 4.23
.

For a topological generator
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
,
the map

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
4.18
and (
4.5
),
it suffices to show that the maps

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)

induced by
\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow\left(D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)

induced by the inclusion
\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\hookrightarrow\left(D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}
are isomorphisms.

As for the latter map, we utilize the following
\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
-equivariant exact sequence

0\rightarrow D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\hookrightarrow D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\xrightarrow{(x^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto x^{(0)}}D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}\rightarrow 0,

as in Proposition I.3.10 of
[
25
]
, where
\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
acts on
D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}
by
19
19

19

Note that
\psi
is bijective on
D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}
.

\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot\overline{x}=\overline{\sigma_{a}(x)}\ (a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}),\ \ \begin{pmatrix}p^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\cdot\overline{x}=\overline{\psi(x)}

for

\overline{x}\in D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}
.
Since
\alpha_{p}-1
is surjective on
D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
by
[
25
, Prop. I.3.16 (i)]
, we obtain the following

\Gamma\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
-equivariant
short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow\left(D^{\sharp}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow(D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural})^{\psi=1}\rightarrow 0.

Hence, it suffices to show that

((D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural})^{\psi=1})^{\sigma_{a}=1}=(D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural})^{\psi=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)=0
.
Since there exists a perfect pairing

\{\ ,\}:D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}\times D^{\mathrm{nr}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}

such that

\{\sigma_{a}(x),y\}=\{x,\sigma_{a}^{-1}(y)\}\text{ and }\{\psi(x),y\}=\{x,\varphi(y)\}

for
x\in D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural}
and

y\in D^{\mathrm{nr}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}D^{*}(1)^{\mathrm{nr}}
(cf.
[
25
, Prop. I.3.4 (ii)]
), we obtain

(((D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural})^{\psi=1})^{\sigma_{a}=1})^{\vee}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))/(\sigma_{a}-1)=0

and

((D^{\sharp}/D^{\natural})^{\psi=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1))^{\vee}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))^{\sigma_{a}=1}=0

by Lemma
4.22
.

It remains to show that
\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)
is an
R
-module isomorphism. We note that
the map
\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
induces the following
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant exact sequence

0\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{nr}}\xrightarrow{z\mapsto(0,z)}D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\rightarrow 0

by (the same proof as in) Remarque II.2.3 (ii) of
[
25
]
. In turn we obtain the following
\Gamma
-equivariant exact sequence

0\rightarrow(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1}\rightarrow(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1})^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi).

Denote the image of
(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p})^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi)
by
M
. Since

M^{\sigma_{a}=1}\subset(D^{\mathrm{nr}}/(1-\varphi))^{\sigma_{a}=1}=0

by Lemma
4.22
,
it follows that
M^{\sigma_{a}=1}=0
, and so
M/(\sigma_{a}-1)=0
.
In view of this vanishing and the fact that
(D^{\mathrm{nr}})^{\varphi=1}/(1-\sigma_{a})=0
(cf. Lemma
4.22
), it follows that
the map

\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)

is bijective by a simple diagram chase.
∎

4.4.3.
The involution

In this subsection we construct the sought after involution on
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Put
w=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
Since
\alpha_{p}^{-1}\cdot w=\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}\cdot w\cdot\alpha_{p}
,
we have
20
20

20

Note that
D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}
has trivial central character.

\alpha_{p}^{-1}(w(x))=\begin{pmatrix}p&0\\
0&p\end{pmatrix}(w(\alpha_{p}(x)))=w(\alpha_{p}(x))=w(x)

for any
x\in\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}=\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}^{-1}=1}
.

Hence
w
induces an involution

w:\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}

which satisfies
w(\sigma_{a}(x))=\sigma_{a}^{-1}(w(x))
. In turn, it induces an
R
-module involution

w:\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1)\rightarrow\left(D^{\natural}\boxtimes\mathbf{P}^{1}\right)^{\alpha_{p}=1}/(\sigma_{a}-1).

Theorem 4.24
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank two. Then
the above involution
w
is compatible with Colmez’s operator
w_{*}:D^{\psi=0}\rightarrow D^{\psi=0}
as in (
4.4
).
Moreover, it induces an
R
-module involution

w^{\prime}_{T}:H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

by Theorem
4.23
,
which coincides with the involution
w_{T}

as in §
4.2.2
.

Proof.

The compatibility with
w_{*}
follows from the proof of Proposition V.2.1 of
[
25
]
.

By §3.2.2 and pp. 313-314 of
[
NaKato
]
, we see that
w^{\prime}_{T}
also satisfies the characterization
property (
4.3
) of
w_{T}
. Hence, the two constructions coincide.
∎

4.5.
Proofs of Theorems
3.1
and
3.5

Proof.

The existence of the local sign decomposition as in Theorem
3.1
is a consequence of
the above two different constructions of the involution
w_{T}
, which coincide by Theorem
4.24
.

The functorial property 2) is clear from the second construction of the local sign decomposition since Colmez’s operator
w_{*}
is functorial.
The property 3) follows from part 4) of Proposition
2.12
and the functorial property of
w_{*}
, or, from the property 1) of Conjecture
4.5
which is
proved in Theorem
4.7
.
The first property follows from Propositions
4.14
.

As for the fourth property, note that
H^{1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}(V)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)=H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
by Proposition
4.17
. For a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-stable lattice
T\subset V
, we have
H^{1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}(V)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\subset H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
. Since both are Lagrangian
R
-submodules of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
of rank one, we conclude that

H^{1}_{\hat{\varepsilon}(V)}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
by
Lemma
2.16
.

Similarly,
the involution
w_{T\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
defined in §4.2 and Proposition
2.13
lead to Theorem
3.5
.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

The above verification of the de Rham property 4)
relies on Kato’s local epsilon conjecture.
It is based on
[
NaKato
, Prop. 3.14]
,
which was the key and the deepest part of the third-named author’s proof of the conjecture for rank two representations.

5.
Characterization of the signed submodules

In this section, we give a characterization of the signed submodules appearing in the local sign decomposition
via de Rham specializations (see Proposition
5.2
), and an application:
the vanishing of the universal norm subgroup over a universal local deformation ring (see Theorem
5.5
). It relies on Zariski density of crystalline points with a given sign on the deformation ring as established in appendix
A
. We also give some examples of the signed submodules for reducible representations (see Proposition
5.8
).

The characterization will be used in our proof of Rubin-type conjectures (see section
G
), and the reducible example will be used in our compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants for non-generic representations (see §
F.3
).

In this section we fix an odd prime
p
.

5.1.
de Rham characterization and universal norms

The characterization of signed submodules
as in Proposition
3.4

employs one de Rham specialization and rigidity of Lagrangian submodules of modules of rank two
(cf. Lemma
2.16
).
In this subsection we show that sufficiently many de Rham specializations also characterize
the decomposition without the Lagrangian property.

5.1.1.
The characterization

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation.
For a de Rham specialization
s
given by
R\rightarrow R_{s}
, put
T_{s}:=T\otimes_{R}R_{s}
.

Definition 5.1
.

For
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
define an
R
-submodule
Z_{\varepsilon}(T)\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
by

Z_{\varepsilon}(T):=\{v\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\,|\,s(v)\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})\;\text{for any de Rham specialization $s$
with $\hat{\varepsilon}(T_{s})=-\varepsilon\cdot 1$}\}.

The universal norm subgroup
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is defined by
21
21

21

If
T
is de Rham, then
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
coincides with the Bloch–Kato subgroup.

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T):=Z_{+}(T)\cap Z_{-}(T),

i.e. it consists of elements
v
whose any de Rham specialization
s(v)
belongs to
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank two and

\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
.
Suppose that the de Rham specializations with sign
-\varepsilon
are Zariski dense for the pair
(R,T)
.
Then

Z_{\varepsilon}(T)=H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T).

In particular, if the
\varepsilon
-de Rham specializations are Zariski dense for any
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
then
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=\{0\}
.

The proof of Proposition
5.2
in the next subsection yields the following.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
S
be a subset of the set of specializations of
R
.
For
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
put

Z_{S,\varepsilon}(T):=\{v\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\,|\,s(v)\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})\;\text{for any de Rham specialization $s\in S$
with $\hat{\varepsilon}(T_{s})=-\varepsilon\cdot 1$}\}.

Suppose that
S
contains
Zariski dense de Rham specializations with sign
-\varepsilon
.
Then

Z_{S,\varepsilon}(T)=Z_{\varepsilon}(T)=H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T).

5.1.2.
Proof of Proposition
5.2

Proposition 5.4
.

i)

We have
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\subset Z_{\pm}(T)
.

ii)

The natural map induces surjective
R
-module morphisms

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\rightarrow Z_{\pm}(T)/H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T).

In particular, if the universal norm subgroup
H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is trivial, then

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=Z_{\pm}(T)
.

Proof.

i) Suppose that
\hat{\varepsilon}(T_{s})=-\varepsilon
. Then we have

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})=H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
by part 4) of Theorem
3.1
. Hence
x\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
implies that
s(x)\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
, concluding the proof.

ii) We only discuss the plus case.
Take an element
x\in Z_{+}(T)
, and consider the decomposition
x=x_{+}+x_{-}
arising from the local sign decomposition.
By part i), we have

x_{-}=x-x_{+}\in Z_{+}(T)\cap H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\subset Z_{+}(T)\cap Z_{-}(T).

Hence
x\equiv x_{+}
modulo a universal norm.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Fix an
R
-basis
v_{\pm}
of
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Let
x\in Z_{\varepsilon}(T)
and consider its local sign decomposition

x=x_{+}+x_{-}=r_{+}v_{+}+r_{-}v_{-}

for
r_{\pm}\in R
. Let
s
be a de Rham specialization with sign
-\varepsilon
.
Then we have
s(x)\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})=H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})
, and so

s(x_{-\varepsilon})=s(x-x_{\varepsilon})\in H^{1}_{-\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})\cap H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{s})=\{0\}.

Hence, it follows that

r_{-\varepsilon}=0
by the Zariski density assumption.
We then have
x=x_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
, concluding the proof.

The vanishing of
H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
follows from the property
H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)\cap H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=\{0\}
of the local sign decomposition.
∎

5.1.3.
An example: universal deformations

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Aut}}_{{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{T})
be a symplectic self-dual two-dimensional representation over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}
of characteristic
p
.
Let
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
be a universal framed deformation ring classifying framed lifts of
\overline{\rho}
with determinant
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}
and
{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
the universal deformation.
Then for
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
we have

Z_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box})=H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}).

In particular,

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{\mathbb{T}}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box})=0,

and the local sign decomposition
is uniquely determined by the properties 2)-4) of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof.

In light of the Zariski density of crystalline points with a given sign on
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box}
as established in Theorem
A.2
, the assertion is a consequence of Proposition
5.2
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Nekovář conjectured that the universal norm subgroup is torsion for cyclotomic deformation of a non-ordinary representation, as later shown by Perrin-Riou
[
75
]
and Berger
[
3
]
.
The above setting of universal deformations complements the
(conjectures in the)
literature.

5.2.
A signed submodule of reducible representations

5.2.1.
A preliminary

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank
2n
.
Suppose that
T
has a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-subrepresentation
T_{1}
that
is a Lagrangian
R
-submodule
with respect to the symplectic pairing associated with
(R,T)
.
Put
T_{2}:=T/T_{1}
.
Suppose that either

H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})=0
, or
R
is a PID and
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{2})
is free.
Then the image of the natural map

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)

is a Lagrangian submodule of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

Proof.

First, we show that the image is an isotropic submodule.

In view of
the Lagrangian property and self-duality, we have
T_{1}^{*}(1)\cong T_{2}
.
This leads to the following commutative diagram,
which is induced by the cup product and the symplectic self-duality:

\textstyle{(\;,\;):\;H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}
\textstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R}
