\textstyle{(\;,\;):\;H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}
\textstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}
\textstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{R.}

Then for
x\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})
and
y\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
,
we have
(p_{1}(x),y)=(x,p_{2}(y))
. Hence,
(p_{1}(x),p_{1}(x))=0
.

Assume that
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})=0
. Then as in the proof of part 3) of Proposition
2.12
,

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{2})
is a free
R
-module of rank
n
.
We also have
H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{1})=0
.
Hence, the cokernel of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is free of rank
n
.
Next, assume that
R
is a PID and
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{2})
is free.
Then, the cokernel of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is free.
Since
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\subset H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=0
, the Euler–Poincaré formula shows that
the image of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
has free rank
n
.
Hence, it
is a direct summand of rank
n
and the proof concludes.
∎

5.2.2.
A signed submodule

The purpose of this subsection is to prove the following.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
T
be a generic symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}

of rank
2
with coefficients in
{\mathcal{O}}
, the integer ring of a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Suppose that there exists an exact sequence

0\rightarrow T_{1}\rightarrow T\rightarrow T_{2}\rightarrow 0

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations
for
T_{i}
(
i=1,2
) free of rank
1
and
the Hodge–Tate weight of
T_{1}
positive.

i)

Suppose that
T_{2}
is the trivial representation
\mathcal{O}
. Then the natural map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

induces an
{\mathcal{O}}
-module isomorphism

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})/\mathrm{Im}(\delta)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

where
\delta:H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})
is the boundary map. In particular, if
T
is crystalline, then
the natural map induces

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})
, and
if
T
is non-crystalline, then

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
.

ii)

Suppose that
T_{2}
is a non-trivial representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
Write the character associated to
\overline{T}_{1}
as
\omega^{r}\mu

for
\omega
the Teichmüller character,
\mu
unramified and put
\epsilon=(-1)^{r}
. Then we have

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

and
the natural map

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\epsilon}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

is injective and of finite index. It is surjective if and only if
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})=0
.

Proof.

i) Assume that
T_{2}=\mathcal{O}
, and so
T_{1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
by the symplectic self-duality.

Since
T_{1}
is of rank one, it is Lagrangian for the symplectic pairing on
T
.
Thus,

X:=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})/\mathrm{Im}(\delta)

is a Lagrangian
\mathcal{O}
-submodule of

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
by Lemma
5.7
.
Note that
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O})
is free.
Hence, in light of the local sign decomposition for
T
and Lemma
2.16
, it suffices to show that

X\subseteq H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

or equivalently

X\cap H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0

in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
.

Let
z\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))
be the extension class
corresponding to the given one

\alpha:=[0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow T\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\rightarrow 0].

Note that
\mathrm{Im}(\delta)=\mathcal{O}z.

Then taking the long exact sequences associated to
\alpha
and
\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\alpha
, and letting
V=T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we obtain the following
commutative diagram with exact horizontal maps:

\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{O}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}}
\textstyle{D_{\mathrm{crys}}(L)=L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}L(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{3}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V).}

Note that
V
is crystalline or non-crystalline semistable with Hodge–Tate weights
(0,1)

since it is an extension of semistable representations and

H^{1}_{\rm g}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))
.
(cf.
[
2
, §6]
.)

We first assume that
V
is crystalline. Then

\mathcal{O}z\subseteq H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))

and the map
\delta_{3}
is injective since
\delta_{1}
is surjective.
The above inclusion is in fact an equality

\mathcal{O}z=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))

as
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathcal{O}z
is torsion-free since so is
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
.
Note that the inverse image of
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
by the map
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
is

H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))
since
\delta_{3}
is injective. Therefore, we obtain

X\cap H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1)))\cap H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=0

in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
as
H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
by the property 4) of Theorem
3.1
. Here we note that

\hat{\varepsilon}(V)=\varepsilon(V)=1
since
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)=\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
is unramified as a representation of
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
N=0
. We now show that

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2}).

The intersection of the kernel of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})
and

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
is
0
by what we have just shown.
Hence, it induces an injective map
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})

of rank
1
modules.
Since the cokernel of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})
is contained
in the free module
H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})
and

\mathrm{Im}\,H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=\mathrm{Im}\,H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
, it follows that the map

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})
is surjective.

It remains to consider the case where
V
is non-crystalline semistable. Then we have

\mathcal{O}z\cap H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))(=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{can})\cap H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1)))=0,

hence we obtain the following inclusions

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathrm{Im}(\delta)=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathcal{O}z\supseteq H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))\subseteq H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T),

where the last equality holds by the property 4) of Theorem
3.1
.
Here we note that
\hat{\varepsilon}(V)=\varepsilon(V)=-1
since
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)=\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{ur}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)
is unramified as a representation of
W_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and

\mathrm{det}(-\mathrm{Fr}_{p}\mid\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)/\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(V)^{N=0})=\mathrm{det}(-\mathrm{Fr}_{p}\mid\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{pst}}(L))=-1.

Since
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathcal{O}z
is torsion-free and
V
is non-crystalline,
we have
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))=\mathcal{O}z+H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))
.
Hence,
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathcal{O}z=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1)).

This implies the equality

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))/\mathrm{Im}(\delta)=H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)

since both are
Lagrangian submodules of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.

ii) Write the character associated with
T_{1}
as
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}^{m}\omega^{n}\mu\chi
for

\mu
unramified and
\chi
of finite
p
-power order.
Then
22
22

22

Note that there is no contribution from the monodoromy part.
we have
\varepsilon(V)=\omega^{n}(-1)
and

\Gamma(V)=(-1)^{m-1}
, implying

\hat{\varepsilon}(V)=(-1)^{m+n-1}=(-1)^{r-1}.

Therefore,
we have

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
by the property 4) of Theorem
3.1
.

Since
T_{2}
is non-trivial, the natural map
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
is injective and
the image is a submodule of
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
of finite index by an argument in the above part i).

If
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})=0
, then the quotient is contained in
the free module
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{2})
, hence,
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1})=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
.
Suppose that
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})\not=0
.
Then
\overline{T}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}
and
\overline{T}_{1}\cong\mathbb{F}(1)
, and
the residual exact sequence is of the form

0\rightarrow\mathbb{F}(1)\rightarrow\overline{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\rightarrow 0.

(5.1)

We also have
r\equiv m+n\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
, and hence
\hat{\varepsilon}(V)=+1
.
Since the map

H^{1}_{\rm{g}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbb{F}(1))

is surjective,
there exists
an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(1)\rightarrow T^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}\rightarrow 0

whose extension class is a lift of that of the residual sequence (
5.1
) and
T^{\prime}
de Rham.
Hence, by part i) and the base change property of the local sign decomposition,
the image of

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})

is equal to
H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
.

Suppose that the image of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T})
is isomorphic to

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T})=H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T})
. Then again by the base change property,
the image of

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})

equals
H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

In particular, the proposition relates the Bloch–Kato subgroup of
T
with that of
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
. The various possibilities do not seem to be discussed in the literature.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\overline{T}
be a generic symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}

of rank
2
with coefficients in a finite field
\mathbb{F}
.
Suppose that
\overline{T}
has a subrepresentation
\overline{T}_{1}
of rank
1

with character
\omega^{r}\mu
for

\mu
unramified, and put
\epsilon=(-1)^{r}
.

i)

If
\overline{T}_{1}\cong\mathbb{F}(1)
, then

H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})=\mathrm{Im}\,H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})
.

ii)

If
\overline{T}_{1}
is not isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}(1)
, then
H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})
.

Proof.

This follows from the above proof of Proposition
5.8
by choosing an appropriate lift.
For example, in the case i), we choose
T^{\prime}
as in the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
(R,T)
be a generic symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation of rank two.
Suppose that
T
is decomposable, i.e.

T=T_{1}\oplus T_{2}

as a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation for
T_{i}
of rank one.
Write the character associated to
\overline{T}_{1}
as
\omega^{r}\mu
for

\mu
unramified,
and put
\varepsilon=(-1)^{r}
.
Then

H^{1}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{1}),\qquad H^{1}_{-\varepsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{2}).

Proof.

Since
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})=0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the submodules
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
and
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
are Lagrangian by
Lemma
5.7
. The sign of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
and
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})

is determined by Cororally
5.10
ii).
∎

Appendix A
Zariski density of crystalline points with a sign

A.1.
Setting

Let
p
be an odd prime. Let
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
be an algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}={\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and
I_{p}\subset G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
the inertia subgroup.

Let
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
denote
the
p
-adic cyclotomic character. We normalise the Hodge–Tate weights so that the one of
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
is
1
. Let
\omega:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the mod
p
reduction of
\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
.

A.2.
Existence of a lift

We consider the following existence of a lift of rank two, symplectic self-dual mod
p
representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
to characteristic zero. The proof is based on a suggestion of
V. Paškūnas, to whom we are grateful.

Theorem A.1
.

Let

\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})

be a continuous representation with
\det(\overline{\rho})=\omega

for
{\mathbb{F}}
a finite field of characteristic
p
.
Then there exists a continuous lift

\rho:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(W({\mathbb{F}}))

of
\overline{\rho}
such that

i)

\det(\rho)=\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
,

ii)

\rho
is crystalline,

iii)

The associated Hodge–Tate weights are
(k,1-k)
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
of a given parity,

iv)

\rho
is irreducible.

Proof.

We primarily follow the notation of
[
87
]
(see also
[
51
,
52
,
73
]
). In particular
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,1}
denotes the associated framed deformation ring, classifying framed deformations with determinant

\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
.

For an integer
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, put
\lambda_{k}=(k,1-k)
. Let

R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,{\rm cr},k}:=R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,1,{\rm cr}}(\lambda_{k},1\oplus 1,\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}})

be an associated crystalline deformation ring
[
51
]
:
the quotient of
R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,1}
, classifying crystalline deformations of
p
-adic Hodge type
(\lambda_{k},1\oplus 1,\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}})
. In particular the corresponding Hodge–Tate weights are
(k,1-k)
and the determinant

\chi_{{\mathrm{cyc}}}
. It suffices to show that the corresponding Hilbert–Samuel multiplicity
e(R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,{\rm cr},k}/\varpi)
is non-zero, where
\varpi
denotes a uniformiser of
W({\mathbb{F}})
. Indeed, then

R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,{\rm cr},k}
will be non-zero and being
p
-torsion free, its generic fiber will also be non-zero, leading to a desired lift satisfying the properties i)-iii).

The positivity of Hilbert–Samuel multiplicity will be shown
under the large weight hypothesis:

2k-2\geq 2(p^{2}-1),

(A.1)

which will be assumed henceforth.

By the Breuil–Mézard conjecture
[
87
, Thm. 1.2]
, we have

e(R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,{\rm cr},k}/\varpi)=\sum_{\overline{\sigma}}m_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\rm cr}(\lambda_{k})\mu_{\overline{\sigma}}(\overline{\rho}).

(A.2)

Here
\overline{\sigma}
varies over smooth irreducible
{\mathbb{F}}
-representations of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
,
m_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\rm cr}(\lambda_{k}):=m_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\rm cr}(\lambda_{k},1\oplus 1)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
denotes the
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
-multiplicity as in
[
87
, p. 2176]
,
and

\mu_{\overline{\sigma}}(\overline{\rho})\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
the Hilbert-Samuel multiplicity of a crystalline deformation ring as in
[
87
, Rem. 1.3]
. Note that

\mu_{\overline{\sigma}}(\overline{\rho})\neq 0\iff{\mathrm{Hom}}_{{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})}(\overline{\sigma},\beta(\overline{\rho}))\neq 0

(cf.
[
73
, Thm. 1.1]
),
where
\beta(\overline{\rho})
denotes the smooth
{\mathbb{F}}
-representation associated to
\overline{\rho}
by Colmez
[
23
]
. A necessary condition for this non-vanishing is that the central character of
\overline{\sigma}
equals
\mathbf{1}
. We recall that the Breuil–Mézard conjecture
[
BM
,
52
]
is unconditionally resolved for odd primes
p
by the work of Kisin
[
52
]
, Paškūnas
[
73
]
, Hu–Tan
[
44
]
and Tung
[
87
]
.

Put
\sigma_{k}={\rm Sym}^{2k-2}E^{2}\otimes\det^{1-k}
and let
\overline{\sigma}_{k}
denote the associated residual representation. In view of (
A.1
) and the proof of
[
72
, Lem. A.2]
, any smooth irreducible
{\mathbb{F}}
-representation
\overline{\sigma}
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
with trivial central character
appears as a subquoteint of the residual representation
\overline{\sigma}_{k}
with multiplicity at least two. That is, for such
\overline{\sigma}
, we have
m_{\overline{\sigma}}^{\rm cr}(\lambda_{k})\geq 2
.
Therefore, in view of (
A.2
) and the above paragraph, it follows that

e(R_{\overline{\rho}}^{\Box,{\rm cr},k}/\varpi)\geq 2,

(A.3)

completing the existence of a lift
\rho
satisfying the properties i)-iii).

Now we consider the property iv).
If it does not hold for any lift
\rho
satisfying i), ii) and iii) for a given parity, then any such lift is reducible.
On the other hand, the reducible locus has Hilbert–Samuel multiplicity one. Hence, in light of (
A.3
), the proof concludes.

∎

A.3.
Zariski density of crystalline points with a sign

An application of the existence of crystalline lift with a given sign
is the following Zariski density of such crystalline points.

Theorem A.2
.

Let

\overline{\rho}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{F}})

be a continuous representation with
\det(\overline{\rho})=\omega

for
{\mathbb{F}}
a finite field of characteristic
p
and
R^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}_{\overline{\rho}}
the associated universal framed deformation ring with determinant
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}
.
Then for each sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm\}
, the set
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
consisting of
x\in\mathrm{Spec}R^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}_{\overline{\rho}}[1/p]
whose
associated
p
-adic Galois representation
\rho_{x}
is crystalline with Hodge–Tate weights
(k,1-k)
for

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
with
(-1)^{k}=\varepsilon\cdot 1
is Zariski
dense in
\mathrm{Spec}R^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}_{\overline{\rho}}[1/p]
.

Proof.

The Zariski density of crystalline points assuming the existence of a lift as in Theorem
A.1

has already been proved in
[
7
]
, albeit without specifying the sign.
In the following outline we modify the argument of
loc. cit.
to account for the sign.
We primarily follow the notation of
[
7
]
.

First, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
is Zariski dense in the rigid generic fiber

\mathfrak{X}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
of the formal scheme
\mathrm{Spf}R^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}_{\overline{\rho}}
by

[
7
, Lem. 5.1]
. To show this density, it is enough to check that the proof of
[
7
, Cor. 4.3]
works also for
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
instead of
\mathcal{S}^{\psi}
therein.
Instead of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
in that proof, we consider
the subset
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{\overline{\rho}}^{\square,\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}\times_{\mathrm{Sp}L}\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{rig}}
corresponding to pairs

(\rho^{\prime},\theta)
such that
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
and
\theta
is crystalline.
Since
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
is non-empty by Theorem
A.1
,
so is
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}

as it contains a point corresponding to a pair
(\rho,\mathbf{1})
for

\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
.

Then the density of

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
follows by the proof of
[
7
, Cor. 4.3]
. Here we note that if we consider

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{cn}}_{\mathrm{cr}}
in that proof for a fixed
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
, then
any
\rho_{x}\otimes\theta
in the statement of
[
7
, Lem. 4.1]
belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}}
, i.e. the parity of Hodge–Tate weights of
\rho_{x}\otimes\theta
are the same as those of
\rho
since the Hodge–Tate weights of

\rho_{x}
are congruent to those of
\rho

\ \mathrm{mod}\ {dt}
, and those of

\theta
are congruent to
0\ \mathrm{mod}\ {t}
for
d=2
and
t=|\mu_{\mathrm{tor}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})|=p-1
. In particular, they are congruent
\ \mathrm{mod}\ {2}
since
p
is odd.
∎

Part II
Applications

Appendix F
Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constants, epsilon constants and the
p
-parity conjecture

In this section we consider some phenomena around the
p
-parity conjecture. The central result is a compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants for rank two symplectic self-dual de Rham representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
(see Theorem
F.7
). It leads to new cases of the relative
p
-parity conjecture (see Theorem
F.16
) as well as the
p
-parity conjecture for Hilbert modular forms over totally real fields (see Theorem
F.18
). Along the way, we study a local sign-like decomposition for non-generic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
(see Theorem
F.15
).

In this section we fix an odd prime
p
.

F.1.
Backdrop

This subsection introduces the
p
-parity conjecture and Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constants (cf.
[
MR
,
NekMRl
]
).

F.1.1.
p
-parity conjecture

Let
M
be a pure motive
23
23

23

in the naive sense
[
NekMRl
]
defined over a number field
F
with coefficients in a number field
L_{M}
.

For a prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
L_{M}
above
p
, let
V:=M_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the
p
-adic representation of
G_{F}
associated to
M

with coefficients in
L:=L_{M,\mathfrak{p}}
.
Suppose that
M
is symplectic self-dual, i.e.
there exists an isomorphism

M\cong M^{\vee}(1)

of pure motives inducing a skew-symmetric isomorphism
V\cong V^{\vee}(1)

of
L[G_{F}]
-modules.

Let
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F,V)
be the Block–Kato Selmer group associated to
V
and put

\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)=\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F,V).

Here we note that
\dim_{L}H^{0}(F,V)=0
since
M
is pure and self-dual.
For a place
v
of
F
, let
\varepsilon_{v}(V)\in\{\pm 1\}
be the local
\varepsilon
-constant,
defined using a non-trivial additive character
\psi
and a Haar measure that is
self-dual with respect to
\psi
(cf. Lemma
2.6
).

Definition F.1
.

The global
\varepsilon
-constant associated to
V
is defined as

\varepsilon(V):=\prod_{v}\varepsilon_{v}(V).

The
\varepsilon
-constant
\varepsilon_{v}(V)
for any finite place
v

and the archimedean component
\prod_{w|\infty}\varepsilon_{w}(V)

are determined by the local Galois representations
V|_{G_{F_{v}}}
and
V|_{G_{F_{w}^{\prime}}}
for all
w^{\prime}|p
, respectively.
More precisely, for an integer
m
and place
w^{\prime}
above
p
, put

h_{m}(V|_{G_{F_{w^{\prime}}}}):=\dim_{L}\mathrm{gr}^{m}D_{\rm{dR}}(V|_{G_{F_{w^{\prime}}}}),\quad g_{w^{\prime}}^{-}(V):=\sum_{m<0}m\,h_{m}(V|_{G_{F_{w^{\prime}}}}),\quad g_{w^{\prime}}^{+}(V):=\sum_{m>0}m\,h_{m}(V|_{G_{F_{w^{\prime}}}}).

Then the global
\varepsilon
-constant equals

\varepsilon(V)=\prod_{v\in S_{\mathrm{f}}}\varepsilon_{v}(V)\cdot\prod_{w\in S_{\infty}}\varepsilon_{w}(V)

where
S_{\rm f}
and
S_{\infty}
denote the set of finite and infinite places of
F
and
24
24

24

Fix an isomorphism

\lambda:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
. Then for each embedding
\iota_{v}:F\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
,
we can deduce Hodge theoretic information about a symplectic self-dual motive
M
defined over
F

at the archimedean place corresponding to
\lambda\circ\iota_{v}

from its
p
-adic realization with respect to
\iota_{v}
.
A subtlety is that there is a one to one correspondence
between archimedean and
p
-adic embeddings via
\lambda
,
but not between the archimedean and
p
-adic places of
F
, namely
\#S_{\infty}\not=\#S_{p}
in general.
Hence, one typically does not expect a finer term-by-term relation than (
F.1
).

\prod_{w\in S_{\infty}}\varepsilon_{w}(V):=(-1)^{\sum_{w^{\prime}\in S_{p}}g_{w^{\prime}}^{-}(V)}\cdot(-1)^{r_{2}(F)\dim_{L}\!V/2}

(F.1)

for
S_{p}
the set of places above
p
(cf.
[
65
]
).

We now let
V
to be a geometric
p
-adic representation of
G_{F}
defined over a
p
-adic local field
L
,
i.e. unramified outside finitely many places and de Rham at places above
p
.
Put

\chi_{\rm f}(F,V)=\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\rm f}(F,V)-\dim_{L}H^{0}(F,V).

Define
\varepsilon(V)
as in Definition
F.1
.

The Bloch–Kato conjecture suggests the following.

Conjecture F.2
.

(
p
-parity conjecture)
For any symplectic self-dual and geometric
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{F}
,
we have

\varepsilon(V)=(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)}.

F.1.2.
Relative
p
-parity conjecture

Conjecture F.3
.

(relative
p
-parity conjecture)
For any two symplectic self-dual and geometric
p
-adic representations
V
and
V^{\prime}
of
G_{F}
,
we have

(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V^{\prime})}=\varepsilon(V)/\varepsilon(V^{\prime}).

(F.2)

The above conjecture is evidently implied by the
p
-parity conjecture for
V
and
V^{\prime}
.
In practice, one first attempts to approach the relative
p
-parity conjecture for a
p
-adic family
{\mathcal{V}}
containing
V
, which is amenable to
p
-adic methods. Then one may aim to approach the
p
-parity conjecture for
V
via establishing it for a specific specialization of
{\mathcal{V}}
with simple arithmetic features, such as Selmer rank zero or one.

F.1.3.
Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constants

Let
V
and
V^{\prime}
be symplectic self-dual and geometric
p
-adic representations of
G_{F}

that are residually symplectically isomorphic, i.e.
they are deformations of a fixed representation of
G_{F}
over a finite field.

Then Mazur and Rubin
[
MR
, Thm. 1.4]
proved that

\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V^{\prime})\equiv\sum_{v:\text{finite}}\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})\quad\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2

(F.3)

for local constants
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
as recalled below.
So the left hand side of
(
F.2
)
is a product of local signs.

To introduce the invariant
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
,
let
T\subset V
and
T^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
be symplectic self-dual lattices such that

\overline{T}:=T/\mathfrak{p}T
and
\overline{T}^{\prime}:=T^{\prime}/\mathfrak{p}T^{\prime}

are symplectically isomorphic
over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}
.
For each finite place
v
, define
Selmer structures
\mathscr{F}_{v}
and
\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}

on
H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})

by

\mathscr{F}_{v}:=\mathrm{Im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})\right)

and

\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T^{\prime}})\cong H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})\right).

Here the last isomorphism arises from our assumption
\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}
,

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T)
is the Bloch–Kato subgroup if
v|p
, and otherwise

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(F_{v},T):=\mathrm{Ker}\left(H^{1}(F_{v},T)\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},V)/H^{1}(G_{F_{v}}/I_{v},V^{I_{v}})\right)

for
I_{v}
the inertia subgroup.
Then
\mathscr{F}_{v}
and
\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}
are Lagrangian subspaces of
H^{1}(F_{v},\overline{T})
.

Definition F.4
.

Let
v
be a finite place of
F
.

i)

For symplectic self-dual representations
T
and
T^{\prime}
of
G_{F_{v}}

over a complete Noetherian local ring
R
with residue field
{\mathbb{F}}

and an
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear symplectic isomorphism
\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}
of
G_{F_{v}}
-representations,
the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
is defined by

\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\left(\mathscr{F}_{v}/\mathscr{F}_{v}\cap\mathscr{F}_{v}^{\prime}\right)\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2\quad\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}.

ii)

More generally, for
Lagrangian submodules
L
and
L^{\prime}
of
H^{1}(F_{v},T)
and
H^{1}(F_{v},T^{\prime})
respectively, and
an
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear symplectic isomorphism
\overline{f}:\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}
of
G_{F_{v}}
-representations,
define

\delta_{v}(L,L^{\prime})=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}(\overline{L}/\overline{L}\cap\overline{f}(\overline{L}^{\prime}))\ \mathrm{mod}\ {2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}.

Note that the above invariants are defined for local representations, unlike the preceding set-up of the
p
-parity conjecture. Here is an example.

Example 3
.

Let
A
and
B
be elliptic curves defined over
F_{v}
such that
A[p]\cong B[p]
as
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}[G_{F_{v}}]
-modules.
Then we have

\delta_{v}(A,B)=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\overline{A(F_{v})}/(\overline{A(F_{v})}\cap\overline{B(F_{v})})\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2,

where
\overline{A(F_{v})}:=A(F_{v})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\overline{B(F_{v})}:=B(F_{v})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
denote the Kummer images in
H^{1}(F_{v},A[p])
, the latter via the given isomorphism
B[p]\cong A[p]
.

We now show that the local constants
do not depend on the choice of
an isomorphism
\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}
.

Proposition F.5
.

Let
F_{v}
be a non-archimedean local field and
{\mathbb{F}}
a field of odd characteristic
p>0
.
Let
V
be a symplectic self-dual representation of
G_{F_{v}}
over
{\mathbb{F}}
and
\langle\;,\;\rangle
the pairing on
V
.
Suppose that there exists a
G_{F_{v}}
-equivariant
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear isomorphism
f:V\rightarrow V
and
c\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
such that

\langle f(x),f(y)\rangle=c\langle x,y\rangle

for
all
x,y\in V
.
Let
f:H^{1}(F_{v},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(F_{v},V)
also denote the induced map.
Then for any Lagrangian subspace
L
of
H^{1}(F_{v},V)
with respect to the Tate pairing, we have

\delta_{v}(L,f(L))=0\in\mathbb{F}_{2}.

In particular, the invariant
\delta_{v}(L,L^{\prime})
as in Definition
F.4
and so the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
do not depend on the choice of
an
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear symplectic isomorphism
\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}
.

Proof.

First, note that the independence of local constants is a consequence of the asserted vanishing.
Indeed, let
f_{1},f_{2}:\overline{T}^{\prime}\cong\overline{T}

be two
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear symplectic isomorphisms
of
G_{F_{v}}
-representations, and
\delta_{v,1}(\cdot),\delta_{v,2}(\cdot)
the associated invariants. Then
f:=f_{2}\circ f_{1}^{-1}:\overline{T}\cong\overline{T}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-linear isomorphism, and we have

\delta_{v,2}(L,L^{\prime})=\delta_{v,2}(L,f(L))+\delta_{v,2}(f(L),f_{2}(L^{\prime}))=\delta_{v,1}(L,L^{\prime}).

Here the first equality follows from the transitivity of the local invariant
\delta_{v,2}
as in
[
54
, Cor. 2.5]
and the second from the asserted vanishing.

To approach the vanishing, we proceed via a number of reductions. First, we reduce to the case that
f\in\mathrm{Aut}_{{\mathbb{F}}}(V)
is diagonalizable.
Indeed, without loss of generality, we may assume that
{\mathbb{F}}
contains the eigenvalues of
f
.
Then the linear map
f^{p^{m}}
is diagonalizable
for a sufficiently large integer
m
: restricting
f
to a generalized eigenspace of
V
,
we have

f=\alpha I+N

for
\alpha I
a scalar map and
N
a nilpotent operator and so

f^{p^{m}}=\alpha^{p^{m}}I+N^{p^{m}}=\alpha^{p^{m}}I
for an integer
m
with
N^{p^{m}}=0
.
Note that replacing
f
with
f^{p^{m}}
does not affect the assertion since
for any integer
n
, we have

\delta_{v}(L,f^{2n}(L))=\delta_{v}(L,f^{n}(L))+\delta_{v}(f^{n}(L),f^{2n}(L))=\delta_{v}(L,f^{n}(L))+\delta_{v}(L,f^{n}(L))=0

and so

\delta_{v}(L,f^{2n+1}(L))=\delta_{v}(L,f(L))+\delta_{v}(f(L),f^{2n+1}(L))=\delta_{v}(L,f(L))+\delta_{v}(L,f^{2n}(L))=\delta_{v}(L,f(L)).

Hence, by replacing
f
with its odd power
f^{p^{m}}
, we suppose that
f
is diagonalizable.

Next, we reduce to the existence of an
f
-invariant Lagrangian subspace

L_{0}
of
H^{1}(F_{v},V)
.
Indeed, then

\delta_{v}(L,f(L))=\delta_{v}(L,L_{0})+\delta_{v}(L_{0},f(L))=\delta_{v}(L,L_{0})+\delta_{v}(f^{-1}(L_{0}),L)=\delta_{v}(L,L_{0})+\delta_{v}(L_{0},L)=0,

as desired. Here the first equality uses the transitive property
[
54
, Cor. 2.5]
of the invariant
\delta_{v}
.

We may suppose that
f
is an isometry.
Let

V=\oplus_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}}V_{\lambda}

be the eigenspace decomposition of
f
.
Since the
G_{F_{v}}
-action commutes with
f
, the
{\mathbb{F}}
-subspaces
V_{\lambda}\subset V
are stable under the
G_{F_{v}}
-action.
If
\lambda\mu\not=1
, then

V_{\lambda}
and
V_{\mu}
are orthogonal.
Let
\epsilon\in\{+1,-1\}
act on
{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
by
\epsilon\cdot x=x^{\epsilon}
,
and write
S={\mathbb{F}}^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
for the quotient space of this action.
For
\overline{\lambda}\not=\overline{1}\in S
,
put
V_{\overline{\lambda}}:=V_{\lambda}\oplus V_{\lambda^{-1}}
.
Then

V=V_{1}\oplus V_{-1}\oplus_{\overline{\lambda}\in S\setminus\{\overline{1}\}}V_{\overline{\lambda}}

is an orthogonal decomposition. Hence, the restriction of the pairing
\langle\;,\;\rangle

on
V
to the
{\mathbb{F}}
-subspaces

V_{\pm 1}
or
V_{\overline{\lambda}}
is non-degenerate.
Note that
H^{1}(F_{v},V_{\pm 1})
and
H^{1}(F_{v},V_{\overline{\lambda}})
are orthogonal to each other
with respect to the Tate pairing since
it is induced by composition of the cup product with the pairing on
V
.
Hence, the existence of a Lagrangian is further reduced to the case that
V=V_{\pm 1}
or
V=V_{\overline{\lambda}}
for
\overline{\lambda}\in S\setminus\{\overline{1}\}
.

If
V=V_{\overline{\lambda}}
for
\overline{\lambda}\in S\setminus\{\overline{1}\}
,
then
L_{0}=H^{1}(F_{v},V_{\lambda})
or
L_{0}=H^{1}(F_{v},V_{\lambda^{-1}})
is a desired
f
-invariant Lagrangian of
H^{1}(F_{v},V_{\overline{\lambda}})
.
As for the remaining case
V=V_{\pm 1}
, and
f
is simply multiplication by
\pm 1
.
Since
V
is symplectic self-dual, it is even dimensional, and then so is
H^{1}(F_{v},V)
by the Euler–Poicaré formula.
The Tate pairing on
H^{1}(F_{v},V)
is non-degenerate and symmetric, and the associated matrix can be diagonalized with non-zero diagonal entries.
By a scalar extension, we may assume that
{\mathbb{F}}
contains
square roots of
-1
and
the diagonal entries. Hence, we can transform the quadratic form associated with the Tate pairing
to a form with signature
(n,n)
for
n=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}H^{1}(F_{v},V)/2
and
the diagonal coefficients being
\pm 1
. Then the existence of a desired Lagrangian
L_{0}
is apparent.

∎

F.1.4.
A conjecture on the compatibility of local constants

We propose a conjecture on the compatibility of Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant and epsilon constants.

In the setting of the relative
p
-parity conjecture as in §
F.3
, recall that

(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V)-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V^{\prime})}

conjecturally equals

\varepsilon(V)/\varepsilon(V^{\prime})
.
Since the latter is a product of ratios of local
\varepsilon
-constants,
Mazur and Rubin
[
MR
]
surmised a connection between their invariant
\delta_{v}(T,T^{\prime})
and
the ratio
\varepsilon_{v}(V)/\varepsilon_{v}(V^{\prime})
.
Mazur–Rubin
[
MR
]
and Nekovář
[
NekMRl
,
NekMRtame
]
proved results toward it in various cases.
However, neither of them
proposed a conjecture on the compatibility of these local constants.

For symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations, we propose the following.

Conjecture F.6
.

(compatibility of local constants)
Let
p
be an odd prime.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
for
L
a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Suppose that
V_{i}:=T_{i}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Suppose also that

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are residually symplectically isomorphic.
Then

\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{1})}{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{2})}:=\frac{\Gamma(V_{1})\varepsilon_{p}(V_{1})}{\Gamma(V_{2})\varepsilon_{p}(V_{2})}=(-1)^{\delta_{p}(T_{1},T_{2})}.

(F.4)

In this section we prove the conjecture for rank two representations (see Theorem
F.7
).

F.2.
Compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants

The central result of this section is the following.

Theorem F.7
.

Let
p
be an odd prime.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two for
L
a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Suppose that
V_{i}=T_{i}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are residually symplectically isomorphic.
Then Conjecture
F.6
is true for
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, i.e.

\frac{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{1})}{\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{2})}=(-1)^{\delta_{p}(T_{1},T_{2})}.

Proof.

We first consider the generic case. That is, we assume that
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})=0
and and so
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})=0
.
Let
{\mathbb{F}}
denote the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
.

By the base change and de Rham properties of the local sign decomposition as in Theorem
3.1
, we have a canonical surjection

H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})=H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{i})}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\;\longrightarrow\;H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{i})}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathbb{F}}=H^{1}_{-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{i})}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
In light of the functoriality of the local sign decomposition for the isomorphism
\overline{T}_{1}\cong\overline{T}_{2}
, it thus follows that

\delta_{p}(T,T^{\prime})=\begin{cases}0\qquad\text{if \;$\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{1})=\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{2})$,}\\
1\qquad\text{if \;$\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{1})\not=\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V_{2})$.}\end{cases}

The assertion follows from this.

The non-generic or “anomalous” case is a consequence of Theorem
F.9
in the next subsection by the transitivity of the invariant
\delta_{p}
(cf.
[
54
, Cor. 2.5]
).

∎

Remark F.8
.

In light of the above proof, the local sign decomposition may be viewed as an absolute version of the Mazur–Rubin arithmetic local constant.

F.3.
The anomalous case

In this subsection we prove a compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants for non-generic representations of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, henceforth refereed to as anomalous. It relies on a local sign-like decomposition for anomalous representations, which may be viewed as the main result of the subsection.

F.3.1.
Compatibility of Mazur–Rubin and Deligne–Langlands local constants bis

Let
\overline{T}
be an anomalous symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation over a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}
of characteristic
p
, i.e.
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})\neq 0
.
Let
C
be the one-dimensional trivial subrepresentation of
\overline{T}
.

By definition, we have an exact

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations,
and so an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

(F.5)

Note that
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
is a four-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the integer ring of a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}

with uniformizer
\pi
and the residue field
{\mathbb{F}}
.
Suppose that
T
is a symplectic self-dual lift of
\overline{T}

with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}
such that
V:=T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham.
A main result of this subsection is the following.

Theorem F.9
.

Let
T
be a symplectic self-dual
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representation over
\mathcal{O}
of rank two.
Suppose that
V:=T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is de Rham and
the residual representation
\overline{T}
has a one-dimensional trivial representation
C
. Then we have

\delta_{p}(\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)},H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C))=-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)

where
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm f}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)}
denotes the residual image in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
.

The above compatibility implies the compatibility of local constants as in Theorem
F.7
for anomalous representations.

We approach Theorem
F.9
in the reducible and irreducible cases in the next two subsections. The strategy employs local sign decomposition for auxiliary generic representations, leading to a local sign-like decomposition
for anomalous de Rham representations (cf. Theorem
F.15
).

F.3.2.
The reducible case

Let
{T}
be a symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two over an integer ring
{\mathcal{O}}
of a
p
-adic local field with residue field
{\mathbb{F}}
. Put
V=T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.

Throughout §
F.3.2
we assume that

T
is anomalous, i.e.
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})\not=0
, and there is an extension

0\rightarrow T_{1}\rightarrow T\rightarrow T_{2}\rightarrow 0

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations for

T_{1}
of rank one. We may and do assume that the Hodge–Tate weight of
T_{1}
is positive.
Indeed, if the extension is non-split, then this holds,
and
if it is decomposable, this may be assumed
without loss of generality.

We approach Theorem
F.9
by analyzing the following three subcases,
the key tool being local sign decomposition.

Proposition F.10
.

Suppose that
\overline{T}_{1}\cong C
.
Then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)=-1
and

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{1})\cong\mathrm{Im}\,H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{1})=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}),

the isomorphism being induced by the map

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T})
.
In particular,
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)
.

Proof.

The sign
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)
is computed as in the proof of Proposition
5.8
ii).
(Note that
m+n\equiv 0\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2
in this case.)

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})=0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{3}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}V).}

We show that
\delta_{2}
is the zero map. If
V_{2}
is not crystalline, this is trivial
since
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V_{2})=0
. Suppose that
V_{2}
is crystalline, and
it is sufficient to show that
V
is crystalline.
Then
V_{1}
is crystalline
by the symplectic self-duality. Since
\overline{T}_{1}\otimes\overline{T}_{2}^{\otimes-1}\cong\mathbb{F}(-1)
,
the crystalline
representation
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}^{\otimes-1}
is isomorphic to neither
\mathcal{O}
nor
\mathcal{O}(1)
,
and has positive Hodge-Tate weight. Hence,

H^{1}_{\mathrm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V_{1}\otimes V_{2}^{\otimes-1})=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V_{1}\otimes V_{2}^{\otimes-1})
, and
this implies that the extension
V
is crystalline.

The
{\mathcal{O}}
-module
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{2})
is torsion-free by part 3) of Proposition
2.12
since
\overline{T}_{2}\cong\mathbb{F}(1)
.
Hence, the assertion follows.
∎

Proposition F.11
.

Suppose that
\overline{T}_{1}\cong\mathbb{F}(1)
and
T_{1}\not\cong\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)=+1
and

\delta_{p}(\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)},H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C))=-1.

Proof.

The sign
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)
is computed as before.

The residual exact sequence

0\rightarrow\overline{T}_{1}\rightarrow\overline{T}\rightarrow\overline{T}_{2}\rightarrow 0

splits since
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})\not=0
.
In particular, we have the exact sequence

0\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}_{2})\rightarrow 0.

Since
T_{1}
is neither isomorphic to

\mathcal{O}(1)
nor
\mathcal{O}
, the
{\mathcal{O}}
-module
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})
is free of rank one and equals

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})
.
So the image of
\overline{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})}

in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})
is a one-dimensional
subspace contained in
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
.
In particular,
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\not={H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\cap{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)}\not=0
.

Put
C_{0}=H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V/T)
.
The pull-back of the sequence

0\rightarrow T\rightarrow V\rightarrow V/T\rightarrow 0

by
C_{0}
yields the exact sequence

0\rightarrow T\rightarrow T^{\prime}\rightarrow C_{0}\rightarrow 0

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations.
This defines the residual exact sequence

0\rightarrow C\rightarrow\overline{T}\rightarrow\overline{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{C}_{0}\rightarrow 0.

In the following we simply denote the image of
\overline{T}\rightarrow\overline{T}^{\prime}
by
C(1)
.

First, assume
T^{\prime}
is anomalous. Then

0\rightarrow C(1)\rightarrow\overline{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{C}_{0}\rightarrow 0

splits. Hence, the sequence

0\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime})

is exact.
Therefore, the natural image of
C_{0}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)_{\mathrm{tor}}
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})

defines a one-dimensional subspace of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)\cap\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
.

Assume that
T^{\prime}
is generic. Hence, it has local sign decomposition as in Theorem
3.1
. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!f}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})\oplus H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!h}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime})\oplus H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime}).}

The image of
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)
by
f

is contained in
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})=H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})
.
Hence, the image of
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
by
h\circ g
is in

H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime})
. However,
the image of
h
is contained in
H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime})

by Corollary
5.10
.
Therefore, the image of
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
by
h\circ g

must be
0
, and so

g(\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)})
is contained in
the one-dimensional subspace
\ker(h)
.
Since
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}=2
, it follows that

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}

and
\ker(g)=H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)
have non-trivial intersection.
∎

Proposition F.12
.

Suppose that
{T}_{1}\cong\mathcal{O}(1)
. Then we have

\delta_{p}(\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)},H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C))=-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V).

Moreover, if

T
is decomposable, then
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)=\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathcal{O})}
and if
T
is non-crystalline, then

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)=0
.

Proof.

If
T=\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)
,
then we have

H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)=H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathcal{O})\oplus H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathcal{O}(1))\cong\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}.

Hence,
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)=\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\mathcal{O})}
.

Suppose that
T
is a non-split extension of
\mathcal{O}
by
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
As in the proof of Proposition
F.11
,
the image of
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})}

in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T})
is a one-dimensional
subspace contained in
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
.
In particular,
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\not={H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\cap{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)}=\begin{cases}\text{non-zero}\qquad\text{if \;$T$ crystalline,}\\
0\qquad\text{if \;$T$ non-crystalline.}\end{cases}

We proceed as in the proof of
Proposition
F.11
, and use the notation therein.
First, we show that
T^{\prime}
is generic.
Take an
{\mathcal{O}}
-basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
of
T
such that

\sigma(e_{1})=\kappa_{\rm{cyc}}(\sigma)e_{1},\quad\sigma(e_{2})=e_{2}+b(\sigma)e_{1}

for
\sigma\in G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
and
b(\sigma)\in\mathcal{O}
.
Let
(\pi^{n})
be the ideal generated by
b(\sigma)
for all

\sigma\in G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
.
Put
b^{\prime}(\sigma):=b(\sigma)/\pi^{n}
and
e_{2}^{\prime}:=e_{2}/\pi^{n}
.
Then we have

C_{0}\cong\mathcal{O}/(\pi^{n}).

It follows that
T^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}e_{1}+\mathcal{O}e_{2}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the extension class is represented by the cocycle
b^{\prime}(\sigma)
,
which is non-zero modulo
\pi
. Hence,
T^{\prime}
is generic.

If
V
is crystalline, then
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)=+1
and the argument in the proof of
Proposition
F.11
applies.
Suppose that
V
is non-crystalline.
Put
T_{2}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{O}e_{2}^{\prime}
. Then, we have a commutative diagram with
exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
T^{\prime}
is generic, the image of
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T_{1})

in
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})
equals
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})
by Proposition
5.8
.
Hence, the map

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\rightarrow\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})}=\overline{H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T^{\prime})}=H^{1}_{-}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\overline{T}^{\prime})

is surjective.
In particular, the image of
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
by
h\circ g
is one-dimensional.
Let
\overline{v}
be an element of
\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
such that

h\circ g(\overline{v})\not=0.

On the other hand,
\overline{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)_{\rm{tor}}}
is not contained in the kernel of
g
.
In fact, the element
w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)_{\rm{tor}}
represented by
the generator
e_{2}^{\prime}\ \mathrm{mod}\ T\in C_{0}
is given
by the cocycle
b^{\prime}(\sigma)e_{1}
and so its reduction
\overline{w}
is not contained in
C={\mathbb{F}}\overline{e_{2}}
. Therefore,

\overline{w}\notin H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)
, and we also have
h\circ g(\overline{w})=0

since
f(w)=0
.
Hence,
\{\overline{v},\overline{w}\}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-basis of

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}
, and so

\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\cap{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},C)}=0.

∎

F.3.3.
The irreducible case

Let
{T}
be an anomalous symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of

of a

We approach Theorem
F.7

below.

Let

.
Then, by the pull-back by

(F.6)

we have

.

We begin with a preliminary.

Lemma F.13
.

We regard
C^{\prime}
as a submodule of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
by
the connecting map of (
F.7
).
The natural image of
C^{\prime}\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
is contained in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)

if and only if
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})\not=0
. In particular,

\rho
has a
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-equivariant splitting if and only if
the image of
C^{\prime}
is contained in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)
.

Proof.

Fix a generator
c^{\prime}
of
C^{\prime}
and let
t_{1}\in T_{1}
be a lift of
c^{\prime}
.
For
\sigma\in G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
, put

t_{\sigma}:=\sigma(t_{1})-t_{1}\in T\text{ and }\overline{t}_{\sigma}:=t_{\sigma}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \pi T.

Note that the latter is a cocycle associated with
c^{\prime}
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T})
.
Since
C
is generated by
c:=\pi c^{\prime}
, the element defined by the cocyle

\overline{t}_{\sigma}
is in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C)
if and only if
\overline{t}_{\sigma}=a_{\sigma}c
for some
a_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{O}
.
In other words, if and only if

\sigma(t_{1})-t_{1}=a_{\sigma}\pi t_{1}+\pi s_{\sigma}=\pi(a_{\sigma}t_{1}+s_{\sigma})

for some
a_{\sigma}\in\mathcal{O}
and
s_{\sigma}\in T
.

Note that a general element of
T_{1}
is of the form
at_{1}+s
for some
a\in\mathcal{O}
and
s\in T
.
Hence, the above displayed condition is equivalent to
\sigma(t_{1})-t_{1}\in\pi T_{1}
for all
\sigma
, and in turn to

\overline{t_{1}}\in H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})
.
On the other hand, suppose that there exists a non-zero element
\overline{s}_{1}\in H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})
.
Then
\rho
induces
H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{1})\cong H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C^{\prime})
, and we may assume that
\rho(\overline{s}_{1})=c^{\prime}
.
Hence,
\overline{s}_{1}=\overline{t}_{1}
and the proof concludes.
∎

If
T_{1}
is also anomalous, then we construct
T_{2}
by replacing
T
in the construction (
F.7
) with
T_{1}
.
Hence, inductively, we obtain a sequence

T_{0}:=T\subset T_{1}\subset T_{2}\subset\cdots\subset T_{m}

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-stable
{\mathcal{O}}
-lattices in
V
such that

H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})\not=0\text{ if $0\leq i<m$ and }H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{m})=0.

(F.9)

Note that such an
m
exists by the irreducibility of

.
For
0\leq i<m
, write
C^{\prime}_{i}\subset H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V/T_{i})
for the submodule corresponding to the one-dimensional trivial representation in
\overline{T}_{i}
.
For simplicity of notation, we often let
C
denote the one-dimensional trivial representation in
\overline{T}_{i}
and
also identify it with
C^{\prime}_{i}
by the multiplication by
\pi
. By construction, we have an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{0}

(F.10)

of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-representations.

Lemma F.14
.

Pick an integer
0\leq i\leq m
. Let
C_{i}:=H^{0}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V/T_{i})
be the
{\mathcal{O}}
-torsion part of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
.

i)

The free part
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})_{\rm{fr}}:=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})/C_{i}
is of rank two, and the image

\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})

of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
is three dimensional.
Moreover, there exists an
{\mathbb{F}}
-basis of
\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}
consisting of the image of an
{\mathcal{O}}
-basis of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})_{\rm{fr}}
and a generator of
C_{i}
.

ii)

For
0<i<m
, the residual representation
\overline{T}_{i}
decomposes as
\overline{T}_{i}^{+}\oplus\overline{T}_{i}^{-}

where
\overline{T}_{i}^{-}\cong C
and by the canonical projection, we have

\overline{T}_{i}^{+}\cong\overline{T}_{i-1}^{+}\cong C(1)
.

Proof.

i) The first part is clear since
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V_{i})=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V)
is two dimensional.
Hence, we abstractly have

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\cong\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}\oplus C_{i}

and so
\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}:=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})/\pi
is three dimensional.

ii) By the Snake lemma, we have an exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{T}_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{T}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}_{i-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

(F.11)

Hence,
\overline{T}_{i}
contains a submodule isomorphic to
\overline{T}_{i-1}^{+}\cong C(1)
. Since
i<m
, it also contains a submodule isomorphic to
C
.
∎

Put
T=T_{0}
,
\overline{T}_{0}^{-}:=C
and
\overline{T}_{0}^{+}:=\overline{T}/C
.

Theorem F.15
.

Let
{T}
be an anomalous symplectic self-dual de Rham representation of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
of rank two over an integer ring
{\mathcal{O}}
of a
p
-adic local field. Suppose that
T\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is irreducible.
Let
T=T_{0}\subset T_{1}\subset\dots\subset T_{m}
be a chain of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
-stable
{\mathcal{O}}
-lattices as above with
T_{m}
being generic.
Let
v^{\pm}_{m}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}
-basis of the signed submodule
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{m})\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{m})
.
Then for
0\leq i<m
, there exist elements
v^{+}_{i},v^{-}_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
with the following properties.

Write
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
for the
{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
generated by
v_{i}^{\pm}
and
the torsion part
C_{i}
.

1)

The elements
v^{+}_{i},v^{-}_{i}
form a basis of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})_{\rm{fr}}
.

2)

\overline{H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}
is a Lagrangian subspace of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})
.

3)

The
{\mathbb{F}}
-dimension of
\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
is even, and
that of
\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
odd and so the latter equals
1
.
In other words,
for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
, we have

\delta_{p}(\overline{H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})},H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-}))=(-1)^{\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})}=\epsilon\cdot 1.

4)

Consider the map
f_{i}:H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\rightarrow H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})
induced by (
F.10
).
If
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})\cap\overline{C}_{i}=0
, then

f(v_{i}^{+})-v^{+}_{i+1}\in C_{i+1},\quad f(v_{i}^{-})=\pi v^{-}_{i+1}.

If
\overline{C}_{i}\subset H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
, then

f(v_{i}^{+})=\pi v^{+}_{i+1},\quad f(v_{i}^{-})-v^{-}_{i+1}\in C_{i+1}.

In particular, we have
H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})=H^{1}_{\epsilon}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
for

\epsilon=-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V)
, and

\delta_{p}(\overline{H^{1}_{\rm{f}}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T)},H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C))=-\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}(V).

Proof.

Note that the property 2) is a consequence of the property 4).
Indeed, the cup product pairing
(x,c)\in\mathcal{O}
must be
0
for any
x\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
and
c\in C_{i}
,
and 4) implies that
v_{i}^{\pm}
is isotropic.
Hence, the subspace
\overline{H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}
is maximal isotropic.
In particular,
(\overline{v_{i}}^{+},\overline{v_{i}}^{-})\not=0
.

In the following we construct the desired elements
v_{i}^{\pm}

by induction for
i=m-1,m-2,\cdots,0
.

The base case
.
First, suppose that
i=m-1
.
Then the
{\mathcal{O}}
-module module
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{m})
is free by property 3) of Proposition
2.12
, and so
C_{m}=0
.
The exact sequence (
F.10
) induces the following one

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}_{m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{m-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{m-1}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C^{\prime}_{m-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{m-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

(F.12)

Since

Let

be a (lift of) basis of the free part.
Write

Since the image of
\iota_{m-1}
is the one dimensional

,
we have
\pi|b
and

. Since the cokernel of

a
or

Hence, by changing the variable, we may assume that

Then considering the cokernel of

Hence, we can choose

.

, we have

, and so
the kernel of
\iota_{m-1}
is isomorphic to the image of
C^{\prime}_{m-1}
.
Hence, we can modify
w_{2}
by an element of
C^{\prime}_{m-1}
so that
\overline{w}_{2}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{m-1}^{-})
.
Then define
v_{m-1}^{+}=w_{1}
and
v_{m-1}^{-}=w_{2}
.

The inductive step
.
We now suppose that
i<m-1
and that the desired elements
v_{i+1}^{+}
and
v_{i+1}^{-}
exist by the induction hypothesis.

The exact sequence (
F.10
) induces the following one

\textstyle{C_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{i+1}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C_{i}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1}).}

(F.14)

Since
H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})=C_{i}^{\vee}
and
H^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})=C_{i+1}^{\vee}
are both cyclic,
we have

|C_{i}|=|C_{i+1}|\text{ or }|C_{i}|=|{\mathbb{F}}||C_{i+1}|.

(F.15)

Case I
. Suppose that
|C_{i}|=|{\mathbb{F}}||C_{i+1}|
.

Then in the exact sequence (
F.14
),
the image of
h
is one dimensional and hence, so is
\mathrm{Im}\,g_{i+1}
.
The map
f_{i}
induces a map
C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}
with kernel
C_{i}^{\prime}
. By looking at the order,
it must be surjective. Hence,
g_{i+1}(C_{i+1})=0
, and
one of the elements
\{v_{i+1}^{+},v_{i+1}^{-}\}
generates the one dimensional space
\mathrm{Im}\,g_{i+1}
.

We have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{C_{i}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Im}\,g_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C^{\prime}_{i})}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{p_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}_{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{i+1}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{+}).}

(F.16)

By construction,
p_{i+1}\circ\iota_{i}
is an isomorphism.
In particular,
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}

does not intersect non-trivially with
\ker\,p_{i+1}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
.
Since
\overline{f}_{i}
induces an isomorphism
\overline{C}_{i}\cong\overline{C}_{i+1}
on one dimensional spaces,
we have
\overline{C}_{i}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=0
.

We now determine the image of
\iota_{i}
.
Let
c_{i+1}
be a generator of
C_{i+1}
.
Since
\overline{c}_{i+1}\in\overline{f}_{i}(\overline{C}_{i})
, it is contained in
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
.
In particular,
H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})\not=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
, and so

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})=0,\quad\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})=1

by the induction hypothesis.
In particular, the images by
p_{i+1}
of
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+},\overline{c}_{i+1}\in H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})
generate the two dimensional space

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{+})
, and

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
for some
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
.
Take
d_{i+1}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
so that

\{\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+},\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-},\overline{c}_{i+1},d_{i+1}\}
is
a basis of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1})
.
Note that
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
is a two dimensional space containing
\overline{c}_{i+1}
and not
intersecting
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
. So
there exist
a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}
such that

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}+a(\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1})+bd_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}.

(F.17)

Since
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
is Lagrangian and
\overline{c}_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
, the above element is orthogonal to
\overline{c}_{i+1}
.
The element
d_{i+1}
is not orthogonal to
c_{i+1}
because otherwise,
c_{i+1}
is orthogonal to all elements
of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1})
. Therefore, we have
b=0
.
Then the element (
F.17
) is isotropic only when
a=0
.
Hence, the space
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
is generated by
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}
and
\overline{c}_{i+1}
.

Since
C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}
is surjective, we may
choose a (lift of) basis
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
of the free part of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})

satisfying

f_{i}(w_{1})=mv^{+}_{i+1}+n(v^{-}_{i+1}+\alpha c_{i+1}),\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=m^{\prime}v^{+}_{i+1}+n^{\prime}(v^{-}_{i+1}+\alpha c_{i+1})

for some
m,n,m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
.
Since
\overline{f_{i}(w_{j})}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
, we have
n\equiv n^{\prime}\equiv 0\ \mathrm{mod}\ \pi
.
Considering that
\mathrm{Im}\,g_{i+1}
is a one dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space,
m
or
m^{\prime}
is a unit.
Hence, by changing the basis, we may assume that
m=1
and
m^{\prime}=0
. Then again, by the one dimensionality of
\mathrm{Im}\,g_{i+1}
,
the ratio
n^{\prime}/\pi
must be a unit. Hence, by changing the basis, we may assume that

f_{i}(w_{1})=v^{+}_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=\pi(v^{-}_{i+1}+\alpha c_{i+1}).

Since
\pi\alpha c_{i+1}\in f_{i}(C_{i})
, we obtain the desired basis by putting
w_{1}=v_{i}^{+}
and
w_{2}=v_{i}^{-}
.
Then the image of
\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}
by
\overline{f}_{i}
is
the two dimensional space
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
. Hence,
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=0
. We have
v_{i}^{-}\in\ker\,\overline{f_{i}}=\ker\,p_{i}=H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})

and
\overline{C_{i}}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=0
. It follows that

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=1.

Case II
.
Suppose that
|C_{i}|=|C_{i+1}|
in (
F.15
).

Then we have the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C_{i}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

(F.18)

Note that
f_{i}
induces a map
C_{i}\rightarrow C_{i+1}
whose kernel is
C_{i}^{\prime}
.
By considering the order, the cokernel
C_{i+1}/f_{i}(C_{i})
is one-dimensional.
Hence, the image of a generator
c_{i+1}
of
C_{i+1}
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C_{i}^{\prime})
is non-zero.
The three dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space
\overline{H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},{T}_{i+1})}
has a basis

\{\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+},\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-},\overline{c}_{i+1}\}
, and
the
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},C_{i}^{\prime})
is generated by the image of

c_{i+1}
, and one of the images of
v_{i+1}^{+}
and
v_{i+1}^{-}
.

As before, we proceed via the commutative diagram (
F.16
).
We now have two cases depending on the dimension of
\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
, which is
0
or
2

by the induction hypothesis.

Subcase 1
. First suppose that

\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})=0.

Then the images of
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}
and
\overline{c}_{i+1}
by
p_{i+1}
generate

H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{+})
, and
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
for some
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}
.
Take
d_{i+1}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
so that

\{\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+},\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-},\overline{c}_{i+1},d_{i+1}\}
is
a basis of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1})
.
Since
p_{i+1}\circ\iota_{i}
is an isomorphism, there exist
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{F}}
such that

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{++}:=\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}+a(\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1})+bd_{i+1},\quad\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}:=\overline{c}_{i+1}+c(\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1})+dd_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}.

Since
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
is Lagrangian and
\overline{c}_{i+1}
is not orthogonal to
d_{i+1}
as before,
d
must be zero for

\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}
to be isotropic. Then
c
is non-zero, for
\overline{g}_{i+1}(\overline{c}_{i+1})=g_{i+1}({c}_{i+1})\not=0
and so
\overline{c}_{i+1}\not\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
. If
b=0
, then
a=0
since

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{++}
is isotropic and in turn
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{++}=\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}
is not orthogonal to
\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}

because
c\not=0
. This would contradict the fact that
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
is Lagrangian and hence
b\not=0
.
We conclude that
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
is generated by

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{++}:=\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}+a(\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1})+bd_{i+1},\quad\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}:=\overline{c}_{i+1}+c(\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha\overline{c}_{i+1})

with
bc\not=0
.

As before, choose
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
as a (lift of) basis of the free part of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
, and
write

f_{i}(w_{1})=lv^{+}_{i+1}+m(v^{-}_{i+1}+\alpha c_{i+1})+nc_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=l^{\prime}v^{+}_{i+1}+m^{\prime}(v_{i+1}^{-}+\alpha c_{i+1})+n^{\prime}c_{i+1}

for
l,m,n,l^{\prime},m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
.
Since
\overline{f_{i}(w_{j})}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
and it has no
d_{i+1}
-component,
\overline{f_{i}(w_{j})}

is a scalar multiple of
\overline{c}_{i+1}^{\prime}
. Hence,
l\equiv l^{\prime}\equiv 0\ \mathrm{mod}\ \pi
.
Since the cokernel of
f_{i}
is a two-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space,
m
or
m^{\prime}
must be a unit.
Therefore, by changing the variable if necessary, we may assume that
m=1
and
m^{\prime}=0
. Then
\mathrm{Coker}\,f_{i}
is
generated by the images of
v^{+}_{i+1}
and
c_{i+1}
. Since it is a two-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}
-vector space, it follows that

l^{\prime}/\pi
is a unit, and so we may assume that
l^{\prime}=\pi
and
l=0
by changing the basis.
Thus, we have

f_{i}(w_{1})=(v^{-}_{i+1}+\alpha c_{i+1})+nc_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=\pi v^{+}_{i+1}+n^{\prime}c_{i+1},

and
\pi|n^{\prime}
by the two-dimensionality of
\mathrm{Coker}\,f_{i}
.
Since
\pi c_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,f_{i}
, there exist elements
v^{+}_{i},v^{-}_{i}
such that

f_{i}(v^{-}_{i})-v^{-}_{i+1}\in C_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(v^{+}_{i})=\pi v^{+}_{i+1}.

In this case we have
\overline{v}^{+}_{i},\overline{c}_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
since they are killed by
\overline{f}_{i}
, and

\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
is generated by
\overline{c}_{i}
since
v^{-}_{i}
is not killed by
\overline{f}_{i}
.
Hence,
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=2
and

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=1.

Subcase 2
.
Finally, suppose that

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})=2.

Then
\overline{v}^{+}_{i+1},\overline{c}_{i+1}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
, and
the space

\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i+1})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
, which is one dimensional by the induction hypothesis,
is generated by
\overline{c}_{i+1}
.
Since
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1})=\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}+H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i+1}^{-})
,
there exist
a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}
such that

\overline{v}_{i+1}^{--}:=\overline{v}_{i+1}^{-}+a\overline{v}_{i+1}^{+}+b\overline{c}_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}.

Then take
d_{i+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
so that

\{\overline{v}_{i+1}^{--},d_{i+1}\}
generates
\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
. Since
\overline{v}_{i+1}^{--}
is isotropic, we have
a=0
.

As before, choose
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
as a (lift of) basis of the free part of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
, and
write

f_{i}(w_{1})=lv^{+}_{i+1}+m({v}_{i+1}^{-}+bc_{i+1})+nc_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=l^{\prime}v^{+}_{i+1}+m^{\prime}(v_{i+1}^{-}+bc_{i+1})+n^{\prime}c_{i+1}

for
l,m,n,l^{\prime},m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
.
Since
\overline{f_{i}(w_{j})}\in\mathrm{Im}\,\iota_{i}
and it has no
d_{i}
-component,
it follows that
l\equiv l^{\prime}\equiv n\equiv n^{\prime}\equiv 0\ \mathrm{mod}\ \pi
.
Then, by changing the basis, we may assume that
m=1
and
m^{\prime}=0
, and in turn
l^{\prime}=\pi
and
l=n=n^{\prime}=0
.
(Note that
\pi c_{i+1}\in f(C_{i})
.) Thus,

f_{i}(w_{1})={v}_{i+1}^{-}+bc_{i+1},\quad f_{i}(w_{2})=\pi v^{+}_{i+1}.

So we put
v_{i}^{-}:=w_{1}
and
v_{i}^{+}:=w_{2}
.
In this case note that
\overline{v}^{+}_{i},\overline{c}_{i}\in H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
, and

\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})
is generated by
\overline{c}_{i}
.
Hence, it follows that
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{+}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=2
and

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}}\overline{H^{1}_{-}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})}\cap H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},\overline{T}_{i}^{-})=1.

As for the last assertion, note that
H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V_{i})=H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},V_{m})
and

H^{1}_{\pm}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
contains the torsion part of
H^{1}({\mathbb{Q}}_{p},T_{i})
.
∎

F.4.
Relative
p
-parity conjecture

Theorem F.16
.

Let
F
be a number field and
p
an odd prime totally split in
F
.
Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be geometric symplectic self-dual
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-representations of
G_{F}
of rank two
which are residually symplectically isomorphic, where
L
is a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Put
V_{i}=T_{i}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Then the relative
p
-parity Conjecture
F.3
is true for
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, i.e.

(-1)^{\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V_{1})-\chi_{\rm{f}}(F,V_{2})}=\varepsilon(V_{1})/\varepsilon(V_{2}).

Proof.

Note that
F_{v}={\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
for
v|p
by our assumption.

In light of of (
F.3
), the compatibility of local constants (
1.4
) for
v\nmid p
and the compatibility for
v|p
as established in Theorem
F.7
,
it suffices to show that

\prod_{v\in S_{\infty}}\frac{\varepsilon_{v}(V_{1})}{\varepsilon_{v}(V_{2})}=\prod_{v\in S_{p}}\frac{\Gamma(V_{1}|_{G_{F_{v}}})}{\Gamma(V_{2}|_{G_{F_{v}}})}.

By definition (
F.1
) of the left hand side,
this is equivalent to showing that

(-1)^{\sum_{v\in S_{p}}g_{v}^{-}(V_{1})-g_{v}^{-}(V_{2})}=(-1)^{\sum_{v\in S_{p}}g_{v}^{+}(V_{1})-g_{v}^{+}(V_{2})}.

(F.19)

The self-duality implies that

h_{m}(V_{i}|_{G_{F_{v}}})=h_{m}(V_{i}^{*}(1)|_{G_{F_{v}}})=h_{-1-m}(V_{i}^{*}(1))|_{G_{F_{v}}}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Therefore, letting
h_{m}(V_{i}):=h_{m}(V_{i}|_{G_{F_{v}}})
, we have

\displaystyle g_{v}^{-}(V_{i})+g_{v}^{+}(V_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in{\mathbb{Z}}}mh_{m}(V_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m<0}m\,h_{m}(V_{i})+\sum_{m\geq 0}m\,h_{-1-m}(V_{i})

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{m<0}m\,h_{m}(V_{i})-\sum_{m\geq 0}(-1-m)\,h_{-1-m}(V_{i})-\sum_{m\geq 0}\,h_{-1-m}(V_{i})

\displaystyle=-\dim_{L}D_{v}(V_{i})/\mathrm{Fil^{0}}(D_{v}(V_{i}))

\displaystyle=-\dim_{L}V_{i}\,/2

\displaystyle=-1.

The assertion (
F.19
) follows from this.
∎

F.5.
p
-parity conjecture for Hilbert modular forms

F.5.1.
Setup

Let
F
be a totally real field.

Denote by
I
the set of embedding
F\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}

and put
t=\sum_{\sigma\in I}\sigma\in{\mathbb{Z}}[I].

Let
k=\sum_{\sigma\in I}k_{\sigma}\sigma\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}[I]
and
put

k_{0}:=\max\{k_{\sigma}|\sigma\in I\},\qquad v:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(k_{0}t-k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}[I].

For an integral ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}
of
F
,
put

U_{0}({\mathfrak{n}})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})\;\middle|\;c\in{\mathfrak{n}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}\right\},

where
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}:={\mathcal{O}}_{F}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}

for
\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\varprojlim_{n}{\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Let
S_{k}(U_{0}({\mathfrak{n}}))
denote the space
S_{k,v+k-t,I}(U_{0}({\mathfrak{n}});\mathrm{M}_{2}(F);{\mathbb{C}})
